It seems natural to say that c can be financed (or is budget-feasible) from the endowment x provided that there is a predictable process $\theta =$ $( \theta ^ { 0 } , \theta ^ { 1 } , \theta ^ { 2 } , \dots , \theta ^ { d } )$ for which $( \theta , c )$ is a consumption strategy with $V _ { 0 } ( \theta ) = x$ and $V _ { t } ( \theta ) \geq 0$ for all $t \in \mathbb { T }$ . By (5.37), we require that 

$$
\overline {{V}} _ {t} (\theta) = x + \sum_ {u = 1} ^ {t} \theta_ {u} \cdot \Delta \overline {{S}} _ {u} - \sum_ {u = 1} ^ {t} \overline {{c}} _ {u} \geq 0\tag{5.39}
$$

if such a strategy $\theta$ exists. But $\overline { S }$ is a $Q \mathrm { . }$ -martingale, so, taking expectations, (5.39) becomes, with $\textstyle C = \sum _ { u = 1 } ^ { t } c _ { u }$ as cumulative consumption, 

$$
E _ {Q} \left(\overline {{C}} _ {t}\right) = E _ {Q} \left(\sum_ {u = 1} ^ {t} \overline {{c}} _ {u}\right) \leq x.\tag{5.40}
$$

The budget constraint (5.40) is therefore necessary if the consumption C is to be financed by the endowment x. It is also suficient as shown in the following. 

Given a consumption process C with $c _ { t } ~ = ~ \Delta C _ { t }$ , define the process $\overline { { U } } _ { t } = x - \overline { { C } } _ { t }$ . Since C is predictable and $c _ { t + 1 } \geq 0$ 2 

$$
\overline {{U}} _ {t + 1} = E _ {Q} \left(\overline {{U}} _ {t + 1} | \mathcal {F} _ {t}\right) \leq \overline {{U}} _ {t},
$$

so that $\overline { U }$ is a supermartingale. By (5.40), $E _ { Q } \left( \overline { { U } } _ { t } \right) \geq 0$ for all $t \in \mathbb { T }$ . But - then we can find a hedging strategy θ for the claim $X = 0$ with $V _ { 0 } ( \theta ) = x$ and $V _ { t } ( \theta ) \geq 0$ for all t. We have proved the following. 

Theorem 5.6.2. The consumption process C can be financed by an initial endowment x if and only if the constraint (5.40) is satisfied. 

Chapter 6 

# Continuous-Time Stochastic Calculus

## 6.1 Continuous-Time Processes

In this and the succeeding chapters, the time parameter takes values in either a finite interval [0, T] or the infinite intervals $[ 0 , \infty ) , [ 0 , \infty ]$ . We denote the time parameter set by T in each case. 

## Filtrations and Stopping Times

Suppose $( \Omega , { \mathcal { F } } , P )$ is a probability space. As before, we use the concept of a filtration on $( \Omega , { \mathcal { F } } , P )$ to model the acquisition of information as time evolves. The definition of a filtration is as in Chapter 2 and now takes account of the change in the time set T. 

Definition 6.1.1. A filtration $\mathbb { F } = ( \mathscr { F } _ { t } ) _ { t \in \mathbb { T } }$ is an increasing family of sub-σ-fields of ${ \mathcal { F } } ( { \mathrm { i . e . , } } { \mathcal { F } } _ { t } \subset { \mathcal { F } }$ and if $s \leq t$ , then $\mathcal { F } _ { s } \subset \mathcal { F } _ { t } )$ 

We assume that F satisfies the ‘usual conditions’. This means the filtration F is: 

(a) complete; that is, every null set in belongs to $\mathcal { F } _ { 0 }$ and thus to each $\mathcal { F } _ { t }$ , and 

(b) right continuous; that is, $\begin{array} { r } { \mathcal { F } _ { t } = \bigcap _ { s > t } \mathcal { F } _ { s } } \end{array}$ for $t \in \mathbb { T }$ 

Remark 6.1.2. Just as in the discrete case, $\mathcal { F } _ { t }$ represents the history of some process or processes up to time t. However, all possible histories must be allowed. If an event $A \in { \mathcal { F } }$ is $\mathcal { F } _ { t }$ -measurable, then it only depends on what has happened to time t. Unlike the situation we discussed in Chapter 2, new information can arrive at any time $t \in [ 0 , T ]$ (or even $t \in [ 0 , \infty ) )$ , and the filtration consists of an uncountable collection of σ-fields. The right continuity assumption is specific to this situation. 

Definition 6.1.3. Suppose the time parameter T is $[ 0 , \infty ] \ ( \mathrm { o r } \ [ 0 , \infty )$ , or $[ 0 , T ] )$ . A random variable τ taking values in T is a stopping time if, for every $t \geq 0$ 

$$
\{\tau \leq t \} \in \mathcal {F} _ {t}.
$$

Remark 6.1.4. Consequently, the event $\{ \tau \leq t \}$ depends only on the history up to time t. The first time a stock price reaches a certain level is a stopping time, as is, say, the first time the price reaches a certain higher level after dropping by a specified amount. However, the last time, before some given date, at which the stock price reaches a certain level is not a stopping time because to say it is the ‘last time’ requires information about the future. Note that in the continuous-time setting it does not make sense to replace the condition $\{ \tau \leq t \} \in \mathcal { F } _ { t }$ by $\{ \tau = t \} \in \mathcal { F } _ { t }$ . Many of the properties of stopping times carry over to this setting, however. 

Just as in Chapter 5, a constant random variable, $T ( \omega ) = t$ for all $\omega \in \Omega$ , is a stopping time. If T is any stopping time, then $T + s$ is also a stopping time for $s \geq 0$ 

We continue with some basic properties of stopping times. 

Proposition 6.1.5. If S and T are stopping times, then $S \wedge T$ and $S \vee T$ are also stopping times. Consequently, $i f \left( T _ { n } \right) _ { n \in \mathbb { N } }$ is a sequence of stopping times, then $ { \wedge } _ { n } T _ { n } = \operatorname* { i n f } _ { n } T _ { n }$ and $\vee _ { n } T _ { n } = \operatorname* { s u p } _ { n } T _ { n }$ are stopping times. 

Proof. The proof is identical to that given in Example 5.2.3 for the discrete case. □ 

Definition 6.1.6. Suppose T is a stopping time with respect to the filtration $( \mathcal { F } _ { t } )$ . Then the σ-field $\mathcal { F } _ { T }$ of events occurring up to time T is the collection of events $A \in { \mathcal { F } }$ satisfying 

$$
A \cap \{T \leq t \} \in \mathcal {F} _ {t} \text {   for   all   } t \in \mathbb {T}.
$$

Exercise 6.1.7. Prove that $\mathcal { F } _ { T }$ is a σ-field. 

One then can establish the following (compare with Exercise 5.2.6 for the discrete case). 

Theorem 6.1.8. Suppose S, T are stopping times. 

a) If $S \leq T$ , then $\mathcal { F } _ { S } \subset \mathcal { F } _ { T }$ 

b) If $A \in { \mathcal { F } } _ { S }$ , then $A \cap \{ S \leq T \} \in { \mathcal { F } } _ { T }$ 

Proof. (a) Suppose that $B \in \mathcal { F } _ { S }$ . Then, for $t \in \mathbb { T }$ 

$$
B \cap \{T \leq t \} = B \cap \{S \leq t \} \cap \{T \leq t \} \in \mathcal {F} _ {t}.
$$

(b) Suppose that $A \in { \mathcal { F } } _ { S }$ . For $t \in \mathbb { T }$ , we have 

$$
A \cap \{S \leq T \} \cap \{T \leq t \} = (A \cap \{S \leq t \}) \cap \{T \leq t \} \cap \{S \wedge t \leq T \wedge t \}.
$$

Each of the three sets on the right-hand side is in $\mathcal { F } _ { t }$ : the first because $A \in { \mathcal { F } } _ { S }$ , the second because $T$ is a stopping time, and the third because S t and $T \wedge t$ are <sub>t</sub>-measurable random variables. □ 

Definition 6.1.9. A continuous-time stochastic process $X$ taking values in a measurable space $( E , \mathcal { E } )$ is a family of random variables $\{ X _ { t } \}$ defined on $( \Omega , { \mathcal { F } } , P )$ , indexed by t, that take values in (E, ). 

That is, for each t, we have a random variable $X _ { t } ( \cdot )$ with values in $E .$ Alternatively, for each ω (i.e., fixing ω and letting t vary), we have a sample path $X . ( \omega )$ of the process. 

Remark 6.1.10. X could represent the evolution of the price of oil or the price of a stock over time. 

For some (future) time t, $X _ { t } ( \omega )$ is a random quantity, a random variable. Each $\omega$ represents a ‘state of the world’ corresponding to which there is a price $X _ { t } ( \omega )$ . Conversely, fixing $\omega$ means one realization of the world, as time evolves, is considered. This gives a realization, or path, of the price X (ω) as a function of time t. 

## Equivalence of Processes

A natural question is to ask when two stochastic processes model the same phenomenon. We discuss several possible definitions for stochastic processes defined on a probability space $( \Omega , { \mathcal { F } } , P )$ and taking values in the measurable space $( E , \mathcal { E } )$ 

The weakest notion of equivalence of processes reflects the fact that in practice one can only observe a stochastic process at finitely many instants. Assume for simplicity that $E = \mathbb { R }$ and $\mathcal { E }$ is the Borel σ-field on R. Then we can form the family of finite-dimensional distributions of the process $X = ( X _ { t } ) _ { t \geq 0 }$ by considering the probability that for $n \in \mathbb N$ 2 times $t _ { 1 } , t _ { 2 } , \ldots , t _ { n } \ \in \ \mathbb { T }$ and a Borel set $A \ \subset \ \mathbb { R } ^ { n }$ , the random vector $( X _ { t _ { 1 } } , X _ { t _ { 2 } } , \ldots , X _ { t _ { n } } )$ takes values in A. Indeed, set 

$$
\phi_ {t _ {1}, t _ {2}, \dots , t _ {n}} ^ {X} (A) = P \left(\left\{\omega \in \Omega : \left(X _ {t _ {1}} (\omega), X _ {t _ {2}} (\omega), \dots , X _ {t _ {n}} (\omega)\right) \in A \right\}\right).
$$

For each family $\{ t _ { 1 } , t _ { 2 } , \ldots , t _ { n } \}$ , this defines $\phi _ { t _ { 1 } , t _ { 2 } , \dots , t _ { n } } ^ { X }$ as a measure on $\mathbb { R } ^ { n }$ We say that two processes X and $Y$ are equivalent (or have the same law) if their families of finite-dimensional distributions coincide, and then we write $X \sim Y$ 

Note that the preceding does not require Y to be defined on the same probability space as X. This means that we can avoid complicated questions about the ‘proper’ probability space for a particular problem since only the finite-dimensional distributions and not the full realisations of the process (i.e., the various random ‘paths’ it traces out) are relevant for our description of the probabilities concerned. It turns out that if we consider the process as a map $X : \Omega \mapsto \mathbb { R } ^ { \mathbb { T } } \left( { \mathrm { i . e . , ~ } } \omega \mapsto X ( \cdot , \omega ) \right)$ and we stick to Borel sets $A$ in $\mathbb { R } ^ { \mathbb { T } }$ , then the finite-dimensional distributions give us sufficient information to identify a canonical version of the process, up to equivalence. (This is the famous Kolmogorov extension theorem; see [194, Theorem 2.2]). 

However, at least when T is uncountable, most of the interesting sets in $\mathbb { R } ^ { \mathbb { T } }$ are not Borel sets, so that we need a somewhat stronger concept of ‘equivalence’ that ‘fixes’ the paths of our process X tightly enough. Two such definitions are now given; each of them requires the two processes concerned to be defined on the same probability space. 

Definition 6.1.11. Suppose $( X _ { t } ) _ { t \geq 0 }$ and $( Y _ { t } ) _ { t \geq 0 }$ are two processes defined on the same probability space $( \Omega , { \mathcal { F } } , P )$ and taking values in $( E , \mathcal { E } )$ . The process $\{ Y _ { t } \}$ is said to be a modification of $( X _ { t } )$ if 

$$
X _ {t} = Y _ {t} \text { a.s. for all } t \in \mathbb {T};
$$

i.e., 

$$
P (X _ {t} = Y _ {t}) = 1 \text {   for   all   } t \in \mathbb {T}.
$$

Definition 6.1.12. The processes $( X _ { t } )$ and $( Y _ { t } )$ defined as in Defini tion 6.1.11 are said to be indistinguishable if, for almost every $\omega \in \Omega$ ， 

$$
X _ {t} (\omega) = Y _ {t} (\omega) \text {   for   all   } t \in \mathbb {T}.\tag{6.1}
$$

The diference between Definition 6.1.11 and Definition 6.1.12 is that in Definition 6.1.11 the set of zero measure on which $X _ { t }$ and $Y _ { t }$ may difer may depend on t, whereas in Definition 6.1.12 there is a single set of zero measure outside of which (6.1) holds. When the time index set is countable, the two definitions coincide. 

Exercise 6.1.13. A process X is right-continuous if for almost every $\omega$ the map $t \mapsto X _ { t } ( \omega )$ is right-continuous. Show that if the processes X and $Y$ are right-continuous and one is a modification of the other, then they are indistinguishable. 

Definition 6.1.14. Suppose $A \subset [ 0 , \infty ] \times \Omega$ and that $\mathbf { 1 } _ { A } ( t , \omega ) = \mathbf { 1 } _ { A }$ is the indicator function of $A ;$ that is, 

$$
\mathbf {1} _ {A} (t, \omega) = \left\{ \begin{array}{l l} 1 & \text { if } (t, \omega) \in A, \\ 0 & \text { if } (t, \omega) \notin A. \end{array} \right.
$$

Then A is called evanescent if $\mathbf { 1 } _ { A }$ is indistinguishable from the zero process. 

Exercise 6.1.15. Show that A is evanescent if the projection 

$$
\{\omega \in \Omega : \text { there   exists } t \text { with } (t, \omega) \in A \}
$$

of A onto Ω is a set of measure zero. 

Finally, we recall the following. 

Definition 6.1.16. A process $( t , \omega ) \mapsto X _ { t } ( \omega )$ from $( [ 0 , T ] \times \Omega , B ( [ 0 , T ] \times \mathcal F ) )$ to a measurable space $( E , \mathcal { E } )$ is said to be progressively measurable, or progressive, if for every $t \in [ 0 , T ]$ the map $( s , \omega ) \to X _ { s } ( \omega )$ of $[ 0 , T ] \times \Omega$ to E is measurable with respect to the σ-field $B ( [ 0 , \mathrm { T } ] ) \times \mathcal { F } _ { t }$ 

## 6.2 Martingales

Definition 6.2.1. Suppose $( \mathcal { F } _ { t } ) _ { t \geq 0 }$ is a filtration of the measurable space $( \Omega , { \mathcal { F } } )$ and $( X _ { t } )$ is a stochastic process defined on $( \Omega , { \mathcal { F } } )$ with values in $( E , \mathcal { E } )$ . Then X is said to be adapted to $( \mathcal { F } _ { t } )$ if $X _ { t }$ is $\mathcal { F } _ { t }$ -measurable for each t. 

The random process that models the concept of randomness in the most fundamental way is a martingale; we now give the continuous-time definition for $t \in [ 0 , \infty ]$ ; the discrete-time analogue was discussed in Chapters 2 through 5. 

Definition 6.2.2. Suppose $( \Omega , { \mathcal { F } } , P )$ is a probability space with a filtration $( \mathcal { F } _ { t } ) _ { t \geq 0 }$ . A real-valued adapted stochastic process $( M _ { t } )$ is said to be a martingale with respect to the filtration $( \mathcal { F } _ { t } )$ if $E \left| M _ { t } \right| \leq \infty$ for all t and for all $s \leq t$ 

$$
E \left(M _ {t} \mid \mathcal {F} _ {s}\right) = M _ {s}.
$$

If the equality is replaced by $\leq \mathrm { ( r e s p . ) } \geq )$ , then $( M _ { t } )$ is said to be a supermartingale (resp. submartingale). 

Remark 6.2.3. A martingale is a purely random process in the sense that, given the history of the process so far, the expected value of the process at some later time is just its present value. Note that in particular 

$$
E \left(M _ {t}\right) = E \left(M _ {0}\right) \text {   for   all   } t \geq 0.
$$

## Brownian Motion

The most important example of a continuous-time martingale is a Brownian motion. This process is named for Robert Brown, a Scottish botanist who studied pollen grains in suspension in the early nineteenth century. He observed that the pollen was performing a very random movement and thought this was because the pollen grains were alive. We now know this rapid movement is due to collisions at the molecular level. 

Definition 6.2.4. A standard Brownian motion $( B _ { t } ) _ { t \geq 0 }$ is a real-valued stochastic process that has continuous sample paths and stationary independent Gaussian increments. In other words, 

a) $\begin{array} { r } { B _ { 0 } = 0 \ \mathrm { a . s . } } \end{array}$ 

b) $t \to B _ { t } ( \omega )$ is continuous a.s. 

c) For $s \leq t$ , the increment $B _ { t } - B _ { s }$ is a Gaussian random variable that has mean 0, variance $t - s$ , and is independent of $\mathcal { F } _ { s } = \sigma \left\{ B _ { u } : u \le s \right\}$ 

We can immediately establish the following. 

Theorem 6.2.5. Suppose $\left( B _ { t } \right)$ is a standard Brownian motion with respect to the filtration $( \mathcal { F } _ { t } ) _ { t \geq 0 }$ . Then 

a) $( B _ { t } ) _ { t \geq 0 }$ is an $\mathcal { F } _ { t }$ -martingale. 

b) $\left( B _ { t } ^ { 2 } - t \right) _ { t > 0 }$ is an <sub>t</sub>-martingale. 

c) $\left( e ^ { \sigma B _ { t } - \frac { \sigma ^ { 2 } } { 2 } t } \right) _ { t \geq 0 }$ is an $\mathcal { F } _ { t } – m a r t i n g a l e$ 

Proof. a) Since $B _ { t } - B _ { s }$ is independent of $\mathcal { F } _ { s }$ for all $s \leq t$ , we have 

$$
E \left(B _ {t} - B _ {s} \mid \mathcal {F} _ {s}\right) = E \left(B _ {t} - B _ {s}\right) = 0.
$$

Consequently, $E \left( B _ { t } \left| \mathcal { F } _ { s } \right. \right) = B _ { s }$ a.s. 

b) For $\left( B _ { t } ^ { 2 } - t \right)$ , we have 

$$
\begin{array}{r l} & E \left(B _ {t} ^ {2} - B _ {s} ^ {2} | \mathcal {F} _ {s}\right) = E \left((B _ {t} - B _ {s}) ^ {2} + 2 B _ {s} (B _ {t} - B _ {s}) | \mathcal {F} _ {s}\right) \\ & = E \left((B _ {t} - B _ {s}) ^ {2} | \mathcal {F} _ {s}\right) + 2 B _ {s} E \left((B _ {t} - B _ {s}) | \mathcal {F} _ {s}\right). \end{array}\tag{6.2}
$$

The second term in (6.2) is zero by the first part. Independence implies that 

$$
E \left(\left(B _ {t} - B _ {s}\right) ^ {2} \mid \mathcal {F} _ {s}\right) = E \left(\left(B _ {t} - B _ {s}\right) ^ {2}\right) = t - s.
$$

Therefore $E \left( B _ { t } ^ { 2 } - t | \mathcal { F } _ { s } \right) = B _ { s } ^ { 2 } - s ,$ 

c) If Z is a standard normal random variable, with density $\scriptstyle { \frac { 1 } { \sqrt { 2 \pi } } } e ^ { - { \frac { x ^ { 2 } } { 2 } } }$ 2 then 

$$
E \left(e ^ {\lambda Z}\right) = \frac {1}{\sqrt {2 \pi}} \int_ {- \infty} ^ {\infty} e ^ {\lambda x} e ^ {- \frac {x ^ {2}}{2}} d x = e ^ {\frac {\lambda^ {2}}{2}} \text {   for   } \lambda \in \mathbb {R}.
$$

For $s < t$ , by independence and stationarity, we have 

$$
\begin{array}{r l} & E \left(e ^ {\sigma B _ {t} - \frac {\sigma^ {2}}{2} t} | \mathcal {F} _ {s}\right) = e ^ {\sigma B _ {s} - \frac {\sigma^ {2}}{2} t} E \left(e ^ {\sigma (B _ {t} - B _ {s})} | \mathcal {F} _ {s}\right) \\ & \qquad = e ^ {\sigma B _ {s} - \frac {\sigma^ {2}}{2} t} E \left(e ^ {\sigma (B _ {t} - B _ {s})}\right) \\ & \qquad = e ^ {\sigma B _ {s} - \frac {\sigma^ {2}}{2} t} E \left(e ^ {\sigma B _ {t - s}}\right). \end{array}
$$

Now $\sigma B _ { t - s }$ is $N \left( 0 , \sigma ^ { 2 } ( t - s ) \right)$ ; that is, if Z is $N ( 0 , 1 )$ as previously, -the random variable $\sigma B _ { t - s }$ has the same law as $\sigma { \sqrt { t - s } } Z$ and 

$$
E \left(e ^ {\sigma B _ {t - s}}\right) = E \left(e ^ {\sigma \sqrt {t - s} Z}\right) = e ^ {\frac {\sigma^ {2}}{2} (t - s)}.
$$

Therefore 

$$
E \left(e ^ {\sigma B _ {t} - \frac {\sigma^ {2}}{2} t} \mid \mathcal {F} _ {s}\right) = e ^ {\sigma B _ {s} - \frac {\sigma^ {2}}{2} s} \text {   a.s.   for   } s <   t.
$$

Conversely, we prove in Theorem 6.4.16 that a continuous process that satisfies the first two statements in Theorem 6.2.5 is, in fact, a Brownian motion. (Indeed, the third statement characterises a Brownian motion.) 

## Uniform Integrability and Limit Theorems

We discussed the role of uniform integrability in some detail in the discretetime setting of Chapter 5. Here we review briefly how these ideas carry over to continuous-time martingales. Definition 5.3.1 immediately prompts the following. 

Definition 6.2.6. A martingale $\{ M _ { t } \}$ with $t \in [ 0 , \infty )$ (or $t \in [ 0 , T ] )$ is said to be uniformly integrable if the set of random variables $\{ M _ { t } \}$ is uniformly integrable. 

Remark 6.2.7. If $\{ M _ { t } \}$ is a uniformly integrable martingale on $[ 0 , \infty )$ , then lim $M _ { t } = M _ { \infty }$ exists a.s. as we proved for the discrete case in Chapter 5. 

Again, a consequence of $\{ M _ { t } \}$ being a uniformly integrable martingale on $[ 0 , \infty )$ is that $M _ { \infty } = \operatorname* { l i m } M _ { t }$ in the $L ^ { 1 }$ norm; i.e., lim<sub>t</sub> $\| M _ { t } - M _ { \infty } \| _ { 1 } = 0$ In this case, $\{ M _ { t } \}$ is a martingale on $[ 0 , \infty ]$ and 

$$
M _ {t} = E \left(M _ {\infty} \mid \mathcal {F} _ {t}\right) \text {   a.s.   for   all   } t.
$$

We again say that M is closed by the random variable $M _ { \infty }$ 

Recall from the examples following Definition 5.3.1 that if a class of random variables is in $L ^ { 1 } ( \Omega , { \mathcal { F } } , P )$ and is L<sup>p</sup>-bounded for some $p > 1$ , then is uniformly integrable. 

Notation 6.2.8. Write for the set of uniformly integrable martingales. 

An important concept is that of ‘localization’. If  is a class of processes, then $\mathcal { C } _ { \ell \mathrm { o c } }$ is the set of processes defined as follows. We say that $X \in \mathcal { C } _ { \ell \mathrm { o c } }$ if there is an increasing sequence $\left\{ T _ { n } \right\}$ of stopping times such that lim $T _ { n } =$ a.s. and $X _ { t \wedge T _ { n } } \in \mathcal { C }$ . For example,  might be the bounded processes, or the processes of bounded variation. 

Notation 6.2.9. $\mathcal { M } _ { \ell \mathrm { o c } }$ denotes the set of local martingales. 

The defining relation for martingales, $E \left( M _ { t } \vert \mathcal { F } _ { s } \right) = M _ { s }$ , can again be extended to stopping times. This result, which is the analogue of Theorem 5.3.9, is again known as Doob’s optional stopping theorem since it says the martingale equality is preserved even if (non-anticipative) random stopping rules are allowed. A complete proof of this result in continuous time can be found in [109, Theorem 4.12, Corollary 4.13]. Note that our discussion of the discrete case in Chapter 5 showed how the extension from bounded to more general stopping times required the martingale convergence theorem and conditions under which a supermartingale is closed by an $L ^ { 1 } { \mathrm { - f u n c t i o n } }$ . This condition is also required in the following. 

Theorem 6.2.10. Suppose $( M _ { t } ) _ { t \geq 0 }$ is a right-continuous supermartingale (resp. submartingale) with respect to the filtration $( \mathcal { F } _ { t } )$ . If S and T are two $( \mathcal { F } _ { t } )$ -stopping times such that $S \leq T$ a.s., then 

$$
E \left(M _ {T} | \mathcal {F} _ {S}\right) \leq M _ {S} a. s. (r e s p., E \left(M _ {T} | \mathcal {F} _ {S}\right) \geq M _ {S} a. s.).
$$

Corollary 6.2.11. In particular, if $( M _ { t } ) _ { t \geq 0 }$ is a right-continuous martingale and S, T are ( <sub>t</sub>)-stopping times with $S \leq T$ , then 

$$
E \left(M _ {T} \mid \mathcal {F} _ {S}\right) = M _ {S} a. s.
$$

Remark 6.2.12. Note that, if T is any $( \mathcal { F } _ { t } ) { \mathrm { - s t o p p i n g } }$ time, then $E \left( M _ { T } \right) =$ $E \left( M _ { 0 } \right)$ 

The following is a consequence of the optional stopping theorem. Note that we write $x ^ { + } = \operatorname* { m a x } \left\{ x , 0 \right\}$ and $x ^ { - } = \operatorname* { m a x } \left\{ - x , 0 \right\}$ 

Lemma 6.2.13. Suppose $X _ { t } , t \in [ 0 , \infty ]$ is a supermartingale. Then 

$$
\alpha P \left(\left(\inf _ {t} X _ {t}\right) \leq - \alpha\right) \leq \sup _ {t} E \left(X _ {t} ^ {-}\right) f o r a l l \alpha \geq 0.
$$

Proof. Write 

$$
S (\omega) = \inf \left\{t: X _ {t} (\omega) \leq - \alpha \right\}
$$

and $S _ { t } = S \wedge t$ . Using the optional stopping theorem (Theorem 6.2.10), we have 

$$
E \left(X _ {S _ {t}}\right) \geq E \left(X _ {t}\right).
$$

Therefore 

$$
E \left(X _ {t}\right) \leq - \alpha P \left\{\inf _ {s \leq t} X _ {s} \leq - \alpha \right\} + \int_ {\left\{\inf _ {s \leq t} X _ {s} > - \alpha \right\}} X _ {t} d P;
$$

that is, 

$$
\begin{array}{l} \alpha P \left\{\inf _ {s \leq t} X _ {s} \leq - \alpha \right\} \leq E \left(- X _ {t}\right) + \int_ {\left\{\inf _ {s \leq t} X _ {s} > - \alpha \right\}} X _ {t} d P \\ = \int_ {\left\{\inf _ {s \leq t} X _ {s} \leq - \alpha \right\}} - X _ {t} d P \\ \leq E \left(X _ {t} ^ {-}\right). \end{array}\tag{6.3}
$$

Letting $t \to \infty$ in (6.3), the result follows. 

As a consequence, we can deduce Doob’s maximal theorem. 

Theorem 6.2.14. Suppose $( X _ { t } ) _ { t \in [ 0 , \infty ] }$ is a martingale. Then 

$$
\alpha P \left\{\sup _ {t} | X _ {t} | \geq \alpha \right\} \leq \sup _ {t} \| X _ {t} \| _ {1} f o r a l l \alpha \geq 0.
$$

Proof. From Jensen’s inequality (see Proposition 5.3.4), if X is a martingale, then $Y _ { t } = - | X _ { t } |$ is a (negative) supermartingale with $\| Y _ { t } \| _ { 1 } =$ $\left. \boldsymbol { X } _ { t } \right. _ { 1 } = E \left( \boldsymbol { Y } _ { t } ^ { - } \right)$ . In addition, 

$$
\left\{\inf _ {t} Y _ {t} \leq - \alpha \right\} = \left\{\sup _ {t} | X _ {t} | \geq \alpha \right\},
$$

so the result follows from Lemma 6.2.13. 

## 6.2. MARTINGALES

Before proving Doob’s $L ^ { p }$ inequality, we first establish the following result. 

Theorem 6.2.15. Suppose X and Y are two positive random variables defined on the probability space $( \Omega , { \mathcal { F } } , P )$ such that $X \in L ^ { p }$ for some $p , 1 <$ $p < \infty$ , and 

$$
\alpha P (\{Y \geq \alpha \}) \leq \int_ {\{Y \geq \alpha \}} X d P f o r a l l \alpha > 0.
$$

Then 

$$
\left\| Y \right\| _ {p} \leq q \left\| X \right\| _ {p},
$$

where $\textstyle { \frac { 1 } { p } } + { \frac { 1 } { q } } = 1$ 

Proof. Let $F ( \lambda ) = P ( Y > \lambda )$ be the complementary distribution function of $Y$ . Using integration by parts, 

$$
\begin{array}{l l} E \left(Y ^ {p}\right) = - \int_ {0} ^ {\infty} \lambda^ {p} d F (\lambda) \\ = \int_ {0} ^ {\infty} F (\lambda) d (\lambda^ {p}) - \lim _ {h \to \infty} \left(\lambda^ {p} F (\lambda)\right) _ {0} ^ {h} \\ \leq \int_ {0} ^ {\infty} F (\lambda) d (\lambda^ {p}) \\ \leq \int_ {0} ^ {\infty} \lambda^ {- 1} \left(\int_ {\{Y \geq \lambda \}} X d P\right) d (\lambda^ {p}) & \text { by   hypothesis } \\ = E \left(X \int_ {0} ^ {Y} \lambda^ {- 1} d (\lambda^ {p})\right) & \text { by   Fubini's   theorem } \\ = \left(\frac {p}{p - 1}\right) E \left(X Y ^ {p - 1}\right) \\ \leq q \| X \| _ {p} \| Y ^ {p - 1} \| _ {q} & \text { by   Hölder's   inequality. } \end{array}
$$

That is, 

$$
E \left(Y ^ {p}\right) \leq q \left\| X \right\| _ {p} \left(E \left(Y ^ {p q - q}\right)\right) ^ {\frac {1}{q}}.
$$

If $\| Y \| _ { p }$ is finite, the result follows immediately because $p q \mathrm { ~ - ~ } q \mathrm { ~ = ~ } p .$ Otherwise, consider the random variable 

$$
Y _ {k} = Y \wedge k, k \in N.
$$

Then $Y _ { k } \in L ^ { p }$ and $Y _ { k }$ also satisfies the hypotheses. Therefore 

$$
\left\| Y _ {k} \right\| _ {p} \leq q \left\| X \right\| _ {p}.
$$

Letting $k \to \infty$ , the result follows. 

Theorem 6.2.16. Suppose $( X _ { t } ) _ { t \geq 0 }$ is a right-continuous positive submartingale. Write $X ^ { * } ( \omega ) = \operatorname* { s u p } _ { t } X _ { t } ( \omega )$ . Then, for $1 < p \le \infty , X ^ { * } \in L ^ { p }$ if and only if 

$$
\sup _ {t} \left\| X _ {t} \right\| _ {p} <   \infty .
$$

Also, for $1 < p < \infty$ and $q ^ { - 1 } = 1 - p ^ { - 1 }$ ， 

$$
\left\| X ^ {*} \right\| _ {p} \leq q \sup _ {t} \left\| X _ {t} \right\| _ {p}.
$$

Proof. When $p = \infty$ , the first part of the theorem is immediate because 

$$
\sup _ {t} \left\| X _ {t} \right\| _ {\infty} = B <   \infty
$$

implies that $X _ { t } \le B$ a.s. for all $t \in [ 0 , \infty ]$ . The right-continuity is required to ensure there is a single set of measure zero outside which this inequality is satisfied for all t. Also, for $1 < p < \infty$ , if $X ^ { * } \in L ^ { p }$ , then 

$$
\sup _ {t} \| X _ {t} \| _ {p} \leq \| X ^ {*} \| _ {p} <   \infty .
$$

As in Section 5.3, the random variables $( X _ { t } )$ are uniformly integrable, so from [109, Corollaries 3.18 and 3.19] we have that 

$$
X _ {\infty} (\omega) = \lim _ {t \to \infty} X _ {t} (\omega)
$$

exists a.s. Using Fatou’s lemma, we obtain 

$$
E \left(\lim _ {t} X _ {t} ^ {p}\right) \leq \operatorname * {l i m i n f} _ {t} E \left(X _ {t} ^ {p}\right) \leq \sup _ {t} E \left(X _ {t} ^ {p}\right) <   \infty .
$$

Therefore $X _ { \infty } \in L ^ { p }$ and $\left. X _ { \infty } \right. _ { p } \leq \operatorname* { s u p } _ { t } \left. X _ { t } \right. _ { p } .$ 

Write $X _ { t } ^ { * } ( \omega ) = \mathrm { s u p } _ { s < t } X _ { s } ( { \bf \dot { \omega } } )$ . Then $\left\{ - X _ { t } \right\}$ is a supermartingale, so from inequality (6.3) in Lemma 6.2.13, for any $\alpha > 0$ 2 

$$
\alpha P \left(\inf _ {s \leq t} (- X _ {s}) \leq - \alpha\right) = \alpha P \left(X _ {t} ^ {*} \geq \alpha\right) \leq \int_ {\{X _ {t} ^ {*} \geq \alpha \}} X _ {t} d P \leq \int_ {\{X ^ {*} \geq \alpha \}} X _ {t} d P.
$$

Letting $t \to \infty$ , we have for any $\alpha > 0$ 

$$
\alpha P \left(X ^ {*} \geq \alpha\right) \leq \int_ {\left\{X ^ {*} \geq \alpha \right\}} X _ {\infty} d P.
$$

Therefore, Theorem 6.2.15 can be applied with $Y = X ^ { * }$ and $X = X _ { \infty }$ to obtain 

$$
\left\| X ^ {*} \right\| _ {p} \leq q \left\| X _ {\infty} \right\| _ {p}
$$

and the result follows. 

The following important special case arises when $p = q = 2$ and the time interval is taken as $[ 0 , T ]$ 

Corollary 6.2.17 (Doob’s Inequality). Suppose $( M _ { t } ) _ { t \geq 0 }$ is a continuous martingale. Then 

$$
E \left(\sup _ {0 \leq t \leq T} | M _ {t} | ^ {2}\right) \leq 4 E \left(| M _ {T} | ^ {2}\right).
$$

## 6.3 Stochastic Integrals

In discrete time the discounted value of a portfolio process having initial value $V _ { 0 }$ and generated by a self-financing strategy $( H _ { t } ) _ { t \geq 0 }$ is given by 

$$
V _ {0} + \sum_ {j = 1} ^ {n} H _ {j} (\overline {{S}} _ {j} - \overline {{S}} _ {j - 1}).
$$

Recall that under an equivalent measure the discounted price process $\overline { S }$ is a martingale. Consequently, the preceding value process is a martingale transform. The natural extension to continuous time of such a martingale transform is the stochastic integral $\begin{array} { r } { \int _ { 0 } ^ { t } H _ { s } d \overline { { S } } _ { s } } \end{array}$ However, $d \overline { { S } } = \overline { { S } } \sigma \dot { d } \overline { { W } } _ { t }$ 2 where $( W _ { t } )$ 4is a Brownian motion. Almost all sample paths $W . ( \omega )$ of Brownian motion are known to be of unbounded variation. They are therefore certainly not diferentiable. The integral $\textstyle \int H d { \overline { { S } } }$ cannot be defined as $\textstyle \int H { \frac { d S } { d t } }$ 4dt or even as a Stieltjes integral. It can, however, be defined as the 4limit of suitable approximating sums in $L ^ { 2 } ( \Omega )$ 

We work initially on the time interval [0, T]. Suppose $( W _ { t } )$ is an $( \mathcal { F } _ { t } ) .$ Brownian motion defined on $( \Omega , { \mathcal { F } } , P )$ for $t \in [ 0 , T ]$ ; that is, W is adapted to the filtration $( \mathcal { F } _ { t } )$ 

## Simple Processes

Definition 6.3.1. A real-valued simple process on $[ 0 , T ]$ is a function H for which 

a) there is a partition $0 = t _ { 0 } < t _ { 1 } < . . . ~ t _ { n } = T$ ; and 

b) $H _ { t _ { 0 } } = H _ { 0 } ( \omega )$ and $H _ { t } = H _ { i } ( \omega )$ for $t \in ( t _ { i } , t _ { i + 1 } ]$ , where $H _ { i } ( \cdot )$ is $\mathcal { F } _ { t _ { i } }$ measurable and square integrable. That is, 

$$
H _ {t} = H _ {0} (\omega) + \sum_ {i = 0} ^ {n - 1} H _ {i} (\omega) \mathbf {1} _ {(t _ {i}, t _ {i + 1} ]} \mathrm{for} t \in [ 0, T ].
$$

Definition 6.3.2. If H is a simple process, the stochastic integral of H with respect to the Brownian motion $( W _ { t } )$ is the process defined for $t \in ( t _ { k } , t _ { k + 1 } ]$ 2 by 

$$
\int_ {0} ^ {t} H _ {s} d W _ {s} = \sum_ {i = 0} ^ {k - 1} H _ {i} (W _ {t _ {i + 1}} - W _ {t _ {i}}) + H _ {k} (W _ {t} - W _ {t _ {k}}).
$$

This can be written as a martingale transform: 

$$
\int_ {0} ^ {t} H _ {s} d W _ {s} = \sum_ {i = 0} ^ {n} H _ {i} (W _ {t _ {i + 1} \wedge t} - W _ {t _ {i} \wedge t}).
$$

We write $\begin{array} { r } { \int _ { 0 } ^ { t } H d W = \int _ { 0 } ^ { t } H _ { s } d W _ { s } } \end{array}$ 

4 4Note that, because $W _ { 0 } = 0$ , there is no contribution to the integral at $t = 0$ 

Theorem 6.3.3. Suppose H is a simple process. Then: 

a) $\begin{array} { r l } {  { ( \int _ { 0 } ^ { t } H _ { s } d W _ { s } ) } } \end{array}$ is a continuous $\mathcal { F } _ { t } { - } m a r t i n g a l e .$ 

b) $\begin{array} { r } { E \left( \left[ \int _ { 0 } ^ { t } H _ { s } d W _ { s } \right] ^ { 2 } \right) = E \left( \int _ { 0 } ^ { t } H _ { s } ^ { 2 } d s \right) } \end{array}$ 

c) $\begin{array} { r } { E \left( \operatorname* { s u p } _ { 0 \leq t \leq T } \left| \int _ { 0 } ^ { t } H _ { s } d W _ { s } \right| ^ { 2 } \right) \leq 4 E \left( \int _ { 0 } ^ { T } H _ { s } ^ { 2 } d s \right) . } \end{array}$ 

Proof. a) For $t \in ( t _ { k } , t _ { k + 1 } ]$ , we have 

$$
\int_ {0} ^ {t} H _ {s} d W _ {s} = \sum_ {i = 0} ^ {k - 1} H _ {i} (W _ {t _ {i + 1}} - W _ {t _ {i}}) + H _ {k} (W _ {t} - W _ {t _ {k}}).
$$

Now $W _ { t } ( \cdot )$ is continuous a.s. in $t ;$ hence so $\begin{array} { r } { \int _ { 0 } ^ { t } H _ { s } d W _ { s } } \end{array}$ . Suppose that $0 \leq s \leq t \leq T$ . Recall that 

$$
\int_ {0} ^ {t} H _ {s} d W _ {s} = \sum_ {i = 0} ^ {n} H _ {i} (W _ {t _ {i + 1} \wedge t} - W _ {t _ {i} \wedge t}),
$$

where $H _ { i }$ is $\mathcal { F } _ { t _ { i } } \mathrm { - m e a s u r a b l e }$ . Now if $s \leq t _ { i }$ , then 

$$
\begin{array}{r l} & E \left(H _ {i} \left[ W _ {t _ {i + 1} \wedge t} - W _ {t _ {i} \wedge t} \right] | \mathcal {F} _ {s}\right) = E \left(E \left(H _ {i} \left[ W _ {t _ {i + 1} \wedge t} - W _ {t _ {i} \wedge t} \right] | \mathcal {F} _ {t _ {i}}\right) | \mathcal {F} _ {s}\right) \\ & \qquad = E \left(H _ {i} E \left(W _ {t _ {i + 1} \wedge t} - W _ {t _ {i} \wedge t} | \mathcal {F} _ {t _ {i}}\right) | \mathcal {F} _ {s}\right) \\ & \qquad = 0 = H _ {i} \left(W _ {t _ {i + 1} \wedge s} - W _ {t _ {i} \wedge s}\right) \end{array}
$$

because $t _ { i + 1 } \wedge s = t _ { i } \wedge s = s$ . If $s \geq t _ { i }$ , then 

$$
\begin{array}{c} E \left(H _ {i} \left[ W _ {t _ {i + 1} \wedge t} - W _ {t _ {i} \wedge t} \right] | \mathcal {F} _ {s}\right) = H _ {i} E \left(W _ {t _ {i + 1} \wedge t} - W _ {t _ {i}} | \mathcal {F} _ {s}\right) \\ = H _ {i} \left(W _ {t _ {i + 1} \wedge s} - W _ {t _ {i} \wedge s}\right). \end{array}
$$

Consequently, for $s \leq t$ 

$$
E \left(\int_ {0} ^ {t} H _ {s} d W _ {s} | \mathcal {F} _ {s}\right) = \int_ {0} ^ {s} H _ {u} d W _ {u}
$$

and $\textstyle \left( \int _ { 0 } ^ { t } H d W \right)$ is a continuous martingale. 

b) Now suppose $i < j$ so that $i + 1 \le j$ . Then 

$$
E \left(H _ {i} H _ {j} \left(W _ {t _ {i + 1} \wedge t} - W _ {t _ {i} \wedge t}\right) \left(W _ {t _ {j + 1} \wedge t} - W _ {t _ {j} \wedge t}\right)\right)
$$

$$
\begin{array}{l} = E \left(E \left(H _ {i} H _ {j} \left(W _ {t _ {i + 1} \wedge t} - W _ {t _ {i} \wedge t}\right) \left(W _ {t _ {j + 1} \wedge t} - W _ {t _ {j} \wedge t}\right) \big | \mathcal {F} _ {t _ {j}}\right)\right) \\ = E \left(H _ {i} H _ {j} \left(W _ {t _ {i + 1} \wedge t} - W _ {t _ {i} \wedge t}\right) E \left(W _ {t _ {j + 1} \wedge t} - W _ {t _ {j} \wedge t} \big | \mathcal {F} _ {t _ {j}}\right)\right) \\ = 0. \end{array}
$$

Also, 

$$
\begin{array}{c} E \left(H _ {i} ^ {2} \left(W _ {t _ {i + 1} \wedge t} - W _ {t _ {i} \wedge t}\right) ^ {2}\right) = E \left(H _ {i} ^ {2} E \left(\left(W _ {t _ {i + 1} \wedge t} - W _ {t _ {i} \wedge t}\right) ^ {2}   | \mathcal {F} _ {t _ {i}}\right)\right) \\ = E \left(H _ {i} ^ {2} \left(t _ {i + 1} \wedge t - t _ {i} \wedge t\right)\right). \end{array}
$$

Consequently, 

$$
\begin{array}{r l r} & & E \left(\left(\int_ {0} ^ {t} H d W\right) ^ {2}\right) = \sum_ {i = 0} ^ {n} E \left(H _ {i} ^ {2} (t _ {i + 1} \wedge t - t _ {i} \wedge t)\right) \\ & & = E \left(\int_ {0} ^ {t} H _ {s} ^ {2} d s\right) = \int_ {0} ^ {t} E (H _ {s} ^ {2}) d s. \end{array}
$$

c) For the final part, apply Doob’s maximal inequality, Corollary 6.2.17, to the martingale $\begin{array} { r l } {  { ( \int _ { 0 } ^ { t } H _ { s } d W _ { s } ) } } \end{array}$ 

Notation 6.3.4. We write for the space of processes adapted to $( \mathcal { F } _ { t } )$ that satisfy $\begin{array} { r } { E \left( \int _ { 0 } ^ { T } H _ { s } ^ { 2 } d s \right) < \infty } \end{array}$ 

Lemma 6.3.5. Suppose $\{ H _ { s } \} \in { \mathcal { H } }$ . Then there is a sequence $\{ H _ { s } ^ { n } \}$ of simple processes such that 

$$
\lim _ {n \to \infty} E \left(\int_ {0} ^ {T} | H _ {s} - H _ {s} ^ {n} | ^ {2} d s\right) = 0.
$$

Outline of the Proof. Fix $f \in \mathcal H$ , and define a sequence of simple functions converging to f by setting 

$$
f _ {n} (t, \omega) = n \int_ {\frac {k - 1}{n}} ^ {\frac {k}{n}} f (s, \omega) d s \text {for} t \in \left[ \frac {k}{n}, \frac {k + 1}{n} \right].
$$

If the integral diverges, replace it by 0. By Fubini’s theorem, this only happens on a null set in Ω since f is integrable on $\mathbb { T } \times \Omega$ 

Note that, using progressive measurability (recall Definition 6.1.16), as a random variable, the preceding integral is $\mathcal { F } _ { \frac { k } { n } } \mathrm { - m e a s u r a b l e }$ , so that $f _ { n }$ is a simple process as defined in Definition 6.3.1. We show in the following that $\begin{array} { r } { \int _ { 0 } ^ { T } \left| f _ { n } ( t , \omega ) - f ( t , \omega ) \right| ^ { 2 } } \end{array}$ dt converges to 0 whenever $f ( \cdot , \omega ) \in L ^ { 2 } [ 0 , T ]$ 2 4and also that, for all such $\omega \in \Omega$ 

$$
\int_ {0} ^ {T} | f _ {n} (t, \omega) | ^ {2} d t \leq \int_ {0} ^ {T} | f (t, \omega) | ^ {2} d t.
$$

Thus the dominated convergence theorem allows us to conclude that 

$$
E \left(\int_ {0} ^ {T} | f _ {n} - f | d t\right)\rightarrow 0 \text {   as   } n \rightarrow \infty .
$$

We write $f _ { h } ~ = ~ f _ { n }$ when $\begin{array} { r } { h \ = \ \frac { 1 } { n } } \end{array}$ . The proof now reduces to a problem in $L ^ { 2 } [ 0 , T ]$ , namely to show that if $\bar { f } \in L ^ { 2 } [ 0 , T ]$ is fixed, then as $h \downarrow 0$ , the ‘time averages’ $f _ { h }$ defined for $t ~ \in ~ \left[ ( k - 1 ) h , k h \wedge h ^ { - 1 } \right)$ by $\begin{array} { r } { f _ { h } ( t ) = \frac { 1 } { h } \int _ { ( k - 1 ) h } ^ { k h } f ( s ) d s } \end{array}$ and 0 outside $\left[ h , h ^ { - 1 } \right)$ remain $L ^ { 2 } .$ -dominated by $f$ and converge to $f$ in $L ^ { 2 } \mathrm { - n o r m }$ . To prove this, first consider the estimate 

$$
\int_ {0} ^ {T} f _ {h} ^ {2} (t) d t \leq \sum_ {k = 1} ^ {[ \frac {T}{h} ]} \left| \int_ {(k - 1) h} ^ {k h} f (s) d s \right| ^ {2},
$$

which is exact if $\frac { T } { h } \in \mathbb { N }$ or $T = \infty$ . Now the Schwarz inequality, applied to $1 \cdot f$ , shows that each term in the latter sum is bounded above by $\begin{array} { r } { h \cdot \int _ { ( k - 1 ) h } ^ { k h } f ^ { 2 } ( s ) d s ; } \end{array}$ ; hence the sum is bounded by $\begin{array} { r } { h \cdot \int _ { 0 } ^ { [ \frac { T } { h } ] \cdot h } f ^ { 2 } ( s ) d s \leq h } \end{array}$ $\begin{array} { r } { \int _ { 0 } ^ { T } f ^ { 2 } ( s ) d s } \end{array}$ , which proves domination. To prove the convergence, consider $\varepsilon > 0$ and note that if $f$ is a step function, then $f _ { h }$ will converge to $f$ as $h \downarrow 0$ . Since the step functions are dense in $L ^ { 2 } [ 0 , T ]$ , choose a step function $f ^ { \varepsilon }$ such that $\| f ^ { \varepsilon } - f \| < \varepsilon ( \mathrm { w i t h } \| \cdot \|$ denoting the norm in $L ^ { 2 } [ 0 , T ] )$ . Note that since $f _ { h }$ is also a step function, $f _ { h } - f _ { h } ^ { \varepsilon } = ( f - f ^ { \varepsilon } ) _ { h }$ . Moreover, by the definition of $f _ { h }$ , it is easy to verify that $\| { \ddot { f } } _ { h } - f _ { h } ^ { \varepsilon } \| \leq \| f - f ^ { \varepsilon } \|$ . Therefore we can write 

$$
\left\| f _ {h} - f \right\| = \left\| f _ {h} ^ {\varepsilon} - f ^ {\varepsilon} + (f - f ^ {\varepsilon}) _ {h} - (f - f ^ {\varepsilon}) \right\| \leq \left\| f _ {h} ^ {\varepsilon} - f ^ {\varepsilon} \right\| + 2 \left\| f - f ^ {\varepsilon} \right\|.
$$

But the first term goes to 0 as $h \downarrow 0$ since $f _ { h }$ is a step function, while the second is less than 2ε. This proves the result. □ 

## The Integral as a Stochastic Process

Theorem 6.3.6. Suppose $( W _ { t } ) _ { t \geq 0 }$ is a Brownian motion on the filtration $( \mathcal { F } _ { t } )$ . Then there is a unique linear map I from  into the space of continuous $\mathcal { F } _ { t } { - } m a r t i n g a l e s$ on $[ 0 , T ]$ such that: 

a) If H is a simple process in , then 

$$
I (H) _ {t} = \int_ {0} ^ {t} H _ {s} d W _ {s}.
$$

b) $H t \leq T ,$ 

$$
E \left(\left(I (H) _ {t}\right) ^ {2}\right) = E \left(\int_ {0} ^ {t} H _ {s} ^ {2} d s\right).
$$

The second identity is called the isometry property of the integral. 

Proof. For H a simple process, one defines $\begin{array} { r } { I ( H ) _ { t } = \int _ { 0 } ^ { t } H _ { s } d W _ { s } } \end{array}$ . Suppose $H \in { \mathcal { H } }$ and $( H ^ { n } )$ 4 is a sequence of simple processes converging to H. Then 

$$
\begin{array}{c} I (H ^ {n + p} - H ^ {n}) _ {t} = \int_ {0} ^ {t} (H _ {s} ^ {n + p} - H _ {s} ^ {n}) d W _ {s} \\ = \int_ {0} ^ {t} H _ {s} ^ {n + p} d W _ {s} - \int_ {0} ^ {t} H _ {s} ^ {n} d W _ {s}. \end{array}
$$

From Doob’s inequality (Corollary 6.2.17), 

$$
E \left(\sup _ {0 \leq t \leq T} \left| I (H ^ {n + p} - H ^ {n}) _ {t} \right| ^ {2}\right) \leq 4 E \left(\int_ {0} ^ {T} \left| H _ {s} ^ {n + p} - H _ {s} ^ {n} \right| ^ {2} d s\right).\tag{6.4}
$$

Consequently, there is a subsequence $\left( H ^ { k _ { n } } \right)$ such that 

$$
E \left(\sup _ {t \leq T} \left| I \left(H ^ {k _ {n + 1}}\right) _ {t} - I \left(H ^ {k _ {n}}\right) _ {t} \right| ^ {2}\right) \leq 2 ^ {- n}.
$$

Almost surely, the sequence of continuous functions $I ( H ^ { k _ { n } } ) _ { t } , 0 \leq t \leq T$ is uniformly convergent on $[ 0 , T ]$ to a function $I ( H ) _ { t }$ . Letting $p  \infty$ in (6.4), we see that 

$$
E \left(\sup _ {t \leq T} | I (H) _ {t} - I (H ^ {n}) _ {t} | ^ {2}\right) \leq 4 E \left(\int_ {0} ^ {T} | H _ {s} - H _ {s} ^ {n} | ^ {2} d s\right).
$$

This argument also implies that $I ( H )$ is independent of the approximating sequence $( H ^ { n } )$ 

Now $E \left( I ( H ^ { n } ) _ { t } | \mathcal { F } _ { s } \right) = I ( H ^ { n } ) .$ <sub>s</sub> a.s. The integrals $\{ I ( H ^ { n } ) , I ( H ) \}$ belong to $L ^ { 2 } ( \Omega , \mathcal { F } , \dot { P } )$ , so 

$$
\begin{array}{r l} & {\| E (I (H) _ {t} | \mathcal {F} _ {s}) - I (H) _ {s} \| _ {2} \leq \| E (I (H) _ {t} | \mathcal {F} _ {s}) - E (I (H ^ {n}) _ {t} | \mathcal {F} _ {s}) \| _ {2}} \\ & {\qquad + \| E (I (H ^ {n}) _ {t} | \mathcal {F} _ {s}) - I (H ^ {n}) _ {s} \| _ {2} + \| I (H ^ {n}) _ {s} - I (H) _ {s} \| _ {2}.} \end{array}
$$

The right-hand side can be made arbitrarily small, so $I ( H ) _ { t }$ is an $\mathcal { F } _ { t ^ { - } }$ martingale. 

The remaining results follow by continuity and from the density in of simple processes. □ 

Notation 6.3.7. We write $\begin{array} { r } { I ( H ) _ { t } = \int _ { 0 } ^ { t } H _ { s } d W _ { s } } \end{array}$ for $H \in { \mathcal { H } }$ 

Lemma 6.3.8. For $H \in { \mathcal { H } }$ 

$$
a) E \left(\sup _ {0 \leq t \leq T} \left| \int_ {0} ^ {t} H _ {s} d W _ {s} \right| ^ {2}\right) \leq 4 E \left(\int_ {0} ^ {T} | H | _ {s} ^ {2} d s\right).
$$

b) $I f \tau$ is an $( \mathcal { F } _ { t } )$ -stopping time such that $\tau \leq T$ , then 

$$
\int_ {0} ^ {\tau} H _ {s} d W _ {s} = \int_ {0} ^ {T} \mathbf {1} _ {\{s \leq \tau \}} H _ {s} d W _ {s}.
$$

Proof. a) Let $( H ^ { n } )$ be a sequence of simple processes approximating H. We know that 

$$
E \left(I (H ^ {n}) _ {T} ^ {2}\right) = E \left(\int_ {0} ^ {T} | H _ {s} ^ {n} | ^ {2} d s\right)
$$

so, taking limits, we have 

$$
E \left(I (H) _ {T} ^ {2}\right) = E \left(\int_ {0} ^ {T} | H _ {s} | ^ {2} d s\right).
$$

Also, 

$$
E \left(\sup _ {t \leq T} I (H ^ {n}) _ {t} ^ {2}\right) \leq 4 E \left(\int_ {0} ^ {T} | H _ {s} ^ {n} | ^ {2} d s\right).
$$

Taking limits, the result follows. 

b) Suppose $\tau$ is a stopping time of the form 

$$
\tau = \sum_ {1 \leq i \leq n} t _ {i} \mathbf {1} _ {A _ {i}},\tag{6.5}
$$

where $A _ { i } \cap A _ { j } = \varnothing$ for $i \neq j$ and $A _ { i } \in \mathcal { F } _ { t _ { i } }$ . Then 

$$
\int_ {0} ^ {T} \mathbf {1} _ {\{s > \tau \}} H _ {s} d W _ {s} = \int_ {0} ^ {T} \left(\sum_ {1 \leq i \leq n} \mathbf {1} _ {A _ {i}} \mathbf {1} _ {\{s > t _ {i} \}}\right) H _ {s} d W _ {s}.
$$

Now for each i the process $\mathbf { 1 } _ { \{ s > t _ { i } \} } \mathbf { 1 } _ { A _ { i } } H _ { s }$ is adapted and in $\mathcal { H } ;$ it is zero if $s \leq t _ { i }$ and equals $\mathbf { 1 } _ { A _ { i } } H _ { s }$ otherwise. Therefore 

$$
\begin{array}{r l} & {\int_ {0} ^ {T} \left(\sum_ {1 \leq i \leq n} \mathbf {1} _ {A _ {i}} \mathbf {1} _ {\{s > t _ {i} \}}\right) H _ {s} d W _ {s}} \\ & {\qquad = \sum_ {1 \leq i \leq n} \mathbf {1} _ {A _ {i}} \int_ {t _ {i}} ^ {T} H _ {s} d W _ {s} = \int_ {\tau} ^ {T} H _ {s} d W _ {s}.} \end{array}
$$

Consequently, for $\tau$ of the form (6.5), 

$$
\int_ {0} ^ {T} \mathbf {1} _ {\{s \leq \tau \}} H _ {s} d W _ {s} = \int_ {0} ^ {\tau} H _ {s} d W _ {s}.
$$

Now an arbitrary stopping time $\tau$ can be approximated by a decreas ing sequence of stopping times $\tau _ { n }$ where 

$$
\tau_ {n} = \sum_ {i = 0} ^ {2 ^ {n}} \frac {(k + 1) T}{2 ^ {n}} {\bf 1} _ {\mathcal {A}},
$$

where $\begin{array} { r } { A = \left\{ \frac { k T } { 2 ^ { n } } \leq \tau < \frac { ( k + 1 ) T } { 2 ^ { n } } \right\} } \end{array}$ , so that lim $\tau _ { n } = \tau \ \mathrm { a . s }$ . Consequently, because $I ( \dot { H } )$ is almost surely continuous in $t ,$ 

$$
\lim _ {n \to \infty} \int_ {0} ^ {\tau_ {n}} H _ {s} d W _ {s} = \int_ {0} ^ {\tau} H _ {s} d W _ {s} \mathrm{a.s.}
$$

Also, 

$$
\begin{array}{c} E \left(\left| \int_ {0} ^ {T} \mathbf {1} _ {\{s \leq \tau \}} H _ {s} d W _ {s} - \int_ {0} ^ {T} \mathbf {1} _ {\{s \leq \tau_ {n} \}} H _ {s} d W _ {s} \right| ^ {2}\right) \\ = E \left(\int_ {0} ^ {T} \mathbf {1} _ {\{\tau <   s \leq \tau_ {n} \}} H _ {s} ^ {2} d s\right), \end{array}
$$

and this converges to zero by the dominated convergence theorem. Therefore 

$$
\lim _ {n \to \infty} \int_ {0} ^ {T} {\bf 1} _ {\{s \leq \tau_ {n} \}} H _ {s} d W _ {s} = \int_ {0} ^ {T} {\bf 1} _ {\{s \leq \tau \}} H _ {s} d W _ {s}
$$

both a.s. and in $L ^ { 2 } ( \Omega )$ ; the result follows. 

Notation 6.3.9. Write 

$$
\widehat {\mathcal {H}} = \left\{\{H _ {s} \}: H \text {is} \mathcal {F} _ {t} \text {-adapted and} \int_ {0} ^ {T} H _ {s} ^ {2} d s <   \infty \text {a.s.} \right\}.
$$

The preceding definition and results for the stochastic integral can be extended from $\mathcal { H }$ to $\widehat { \mathcal { H } }$ 

Theorem 6.3.10. There is a unique linear map $\widehat { I }$ of $\widehat { \mathcal { H } }$ into the space of continuous processes defined on $[ 0 , T ]$ such that: 

a) If $\left\{ H _ { t } \right\} _ { 0 \leq t \leq T }$ is in $\mathcal { H }$ , then for all $t \in [ 0 , T ]$ the processes $\widehat { I } ( H )$ <sub>t</sub> and $I ( H ) _ { t }$ are indistinguishable. 

b) If $\{ H ^ { n } \} _ { n \geq 0 }$ is a sequence in $\widehat { \mathcal { H } }$ such that $\textstyle \int _ { 0 } ^ { T } ( H _ { s } ^ { n } ) ^ { 2 } d s$ converges to zero in probability, then 

$$
\sup _ {0 \leq t \leq T} \left| \widehat {I} (H ^ {n}) _ {t} \right|
$$

converges to zero in probability. 

Remark 6.3.11. In fact, $\widehat { I } ( H ) _ { t }$ is a local martingale, meaning that there ,is a non-decreasing sequence $T _ { n }$ of stopping times with limit $T$ such that $T _ { n } \leq T _ { n + 1 } \leq T$ and for each n, $\hat { I ( H ) } _ { T _ { n } \wedge t }$ is a martingale. 

Notation 6.3.12. One writes $\begin{array} { r } { { \widehat { I } } ( H ) _ { t } = \int _ { 0 } ^ { t } H _ { s } d W _ { s } } \end{array}$ 

Proof. From Theorem 6.3.6, we know that for $H \in { \mathcal { H } } , I ( H )$ is defined. Suppose $H \in { \widehat { \mathcal { H } } }$ . Define 

$$
T _ {n} = \inf \left\{0 \leq u \leq T: \int_ {0} ^ {u} H _ {s} ^ {2} d s \geq n \right\} \wedge T.
$$

(Here and elsewhere we adopt the convention that in $\operatorname { u f } ( \varnothing ) = \infty . )$ Because $H _ { s }$ is adapted, $\textstyle \int _ { 0 } ^ { t } H _ { s } ^ { 2 } d s$ is adapted and $T _ { n }$ is an $( \mathcal { F } _ { t } )$ -stopping time. 

Then write $\begin{array} { r } { H _ { s } ^ { n } = \mathbf { 1 } _ { \{ s < T _ { n } \} } H _ { s } } \end{array}$ . The processes $H ^ { n }$ are therefore in $\mathcal { H }$ and 

$$
\int_ {0} ^ {t} H _ {s} ^ {n} d W _ {s} = \int_ {0} ^ {t} \mathbf {1} _ {\{s \leq T _ {n} \}} H _ {s} ^ {n + 1} d W _ {s} = \int_ {0} ^ {T _ {n} \wedge t} H _ {s} ^ {n + 1} d W _ {s}.
$$

Therefore, on the set $\begin{array} { r } { \left\{ \int _ { 0 } ^ { T } H _ { u } ^ { 2 } d u < n \right\} } \end{array}$ , for all $t \leq T$ 

$$
I (H ^ {n}) _ {t} = I (H ^ {n + 1}) _ {t}.
$$

Now 

$$
\bigcup_ {n \geq 0} \left\{\int_ {0} ^ {T} H _ {u} ^ {2} d u <   n \right\} = \left\{\int_ {0} ^ {T} H _ {u} ^ {2} d u <   \infty \right\}.
$$

Thus we define $\widehat { I } ( H ) _ { t }$ by putting ${ \widehat { I } } ( H ) _ { t } ~ = ~ I ( H ^ { n } )$ <sub>t</sub> on $\begin{array} { r } { \left\{ \int _ { 0 } ^ { T } H _ { s } ^ { 2 } d s < n \right\} } \end{array}$ 

Clearly $\widehat { I } ( H ) _ { t } = I ( H ) _ { t }$ and is continuous a.s. 

,For the second assertion, write 

$$
B = \left\{\int_ {0} ^ {T} H _ {u} ^ {2} d u \geq \frac {1}{N} \right\}, \quad A = \left\{\omega : \sup _ {0 \leq t \leq T} \left| \widehat {I} (H) _ {t} \right| \geq \varepsilon \right\}.
$$

Then 

$$
P (A) = P (A \cap B) + P (A \cap B ^ {c}) \leq P (B) + P (A \cap B ^ {c}).
$$

Therefore, for any $\varepsilon > 0$ 2 

$$
\begin{array}{l} P \left(\sup _ {0 \leq t \leq T} \left| \widehat {I} (H) _ {t} \right| \geq \varepsilon\right) \leq P \left(\int_ {0} ^ {T} H _ {u} ^ {2} d u \geq \frac {1}{N}\right) \\ \quad + P \left(\left\{\int_ {0} ^ {T} H _ {u} ^ {2} d u <   \frac {1}{N} \right\} \cap \left\{\sup _ {0 \leq t \leq T} \left| \widehat {I} (H) _ {t} \right| \geq \varepsilon \right\}\right). \end{array}\tag{6.6}
$$

Write 

$$
\tau_ {N} = \inf \left\{s \leq T: \int_ {0} ^ {s} H _ {u} ^ {2} d u \geq \frac {1}{N} \right\} \wedge T.
$$

On the set $B ^ { c }$ , we have 

$$
\int_ {0} ^ {t} H _ {s} d W _ {s} = \int_ {0} ^ {t} {\bf 1} _ {\{s \leq \tau_ {N} \}} H _ {s} d W _ {s}.
$$

Therefore, with ${ \cal G } _ { s } = { \cal H } _ { s } { \bf 1 } _ { \{ s \leq \tau _ { N } \} } , \ : { \cal G } _ { s } = { \cal H } _ { s }$ on $B ^ { c }$ , and from Doob’s inequality, it follows that 

$$
E \left(\sup _ {0 \leq t \leq T} \left| \int_ {0} ^ {t} G _ {s} d W _ {s} \right| ^ {2}\right) \leq 4 E \left(\int_ {0} ^ {t} (G _ {s}) ^ {2} d s\right) \leq \frac {4}{N}.\tag{6.7}
$$

Using Chebychev’s inequality, we also have, by (6.7), 

$$
\begin{array}{l} P (A \cap B ^ {c}) = P \left(B ^ {c} \cap \left\{\sup _ {0 \leq t \leq T} \left| \widehat {I} (H) _ {t} \right| \geq \varepsilon \right\}\right) \\ \quad \leq P \left(\sup _ {0 \leq t \leq T} \left| \widehat {I} (G) _ {t} \right| \geq \varepsilon\right) \\ \quad \leq \frac {1}{\varepsilon^ {2}} E \left(\sup _ {0 \leq t \leq T} \left| \int_ {0} ^ {t} G _ {s} d W _ {s} \right| ^ {2}\right) \leq \frac {4}{N \varepsilon^ {2}}. \end{array}
$$

Consequently, from (6.6), 

$$
P \left(\sup _ {0 \leq t \leq T} \left| \widehat {I} (H) _ {t} \right| \geq \varepsilon\right) \leq P \left(\int_ {0} ^ {T} H _ {u} ^ {2} d u \geq \frac {1}{N}\right) + \frac {4}{N \varepsilon^ {2}}.
$$

Hence we see that if $( H ^ { n } )$ is a sequence in $\widehat { \mathcal { H } }$ such that $\begin{array} { r } { \left( \int _ { 0 } ^ { T } ( H _ { u } ^ { n } ) ^ { 2 } d u \right) } \end{array}$ converges to zero in probability, then $\left( \operatorname* { s u p } _ { 0 \leq t \leq T } \left| { \widehat { I } } ( H ^ { n } ) _ { t } \right| \right)$ converges to zero in probability. The continuity of the operator $\widehat { I }$ 'is therefore established. 

If $H \in { \widehat { \mathcal { H } } }$ , then, with $H _ { s } ^ { n } = \mathbf { 1 } _ { \left\{ s < T _ { n } \right\} } H _ { s }$ ,, we see that $\begin{array} { r } { \left( \int _ { 0 } ^ { T } ( H _ { s } - H _ { s } ^ { n } ) ^ { 2 } d s \right) } \end{array}$ ,converges to zero in probability. Using the continuity property, we see that the map $\widehat { I }$ is uniquely defined. 

,Similarly, for H, $\dot { K } \in \widehat { \mathcal { H } }$ , suppose there are the approximating sequences $H ^ { n } , K ^ { n } \in \mathcal { H }$ . Now $\begin{array} { r } { \left( \int _ { 0 } ^ { T } ( H _ { s } - H _ { s } ^ { n } ) ^ { 2 } d s \right) } \end{array}$ and $\begin{array} { r } { \left( \int _ { 0 } ^ { T } ( K _ { s } - K _ { s } ^ { n } ) ^ { 2 } d s \right) } \end{array}$ converge to zero in probability as $n \to \infty$ . Furthermore, 

$$
I (\alpha H ^ {n} + \beta K ^ {n}) _ {t} = \alpha I (H ^ {n}) _ {t} + \beta I (K ^ {n}) _ {t}.
$$

Letting $n \to \infty$ , we see that $\widehat { I }$ is a linear map. 

## 6.4 The Itˆo Calculus

If $f ( t )$ is a real-valued, diferentiable function for $t \geq 0$ and $f ( 0 ) = 0$ , then 

$$
f (t) ^ {2} = 2 \int_ {0} ^ {t} f (s) \dot {f} (s) d s = 2 \int_ {0} ^ {t} f (s) d f (s).
$$

However, if W is a Brownian motion we know that $E \left( W _ { t } ^ { 2 } \right) = t$ . Consequently, $W _ { t } ^ { 2 }$ cannot be equal to $2 \int _ { 0 } ^ { t } W _ { s } d W _ { s }$ because this integral is a (local) martingale and $\begin{array} { r } { E \left( 2 \int _ { 0 } ^ { t } W _ { s } d W _ { s } \right) = 0 } \end{array}$ 

The Itˆo calculus is described for a class of processes known as Itˆo processes, which we will now define. 

## Itˆo Processes and the Diferentiation Rule

Definition 6.4.1. Suppose $( \Omega , { \mathcal { F } } , P )$ is a probability space with a filtration $( \mathcal { F } _ { t } ) _ { t \geq 0 }$ , and $( W _ { t } )$ is a standard $( \mathcal { F } _ { t } )$ -Brownian motion. A real-valued Itˆo process $( X _ { t } ) _ { t \geq 0 }$ is a process of the form 

$$
X _ {t} = X _ {0} + \int_ {0} ^ {t} K _ {s} d s + \int_ {0} ^ {t} H _ {s} d W _ {s},
$$

where 

(a) $X _ { 0 }$ is $\mathcal { F } _ { 0 }$ -measurable, 

(b) K and H are adapted to $\mathcal { F } _ { t }$ , and 

(c) $\begin{array} { r } { \int _ { 0 } ^ { T } \left| K _ { s } \right| d s < \infty \ a . s . \ a n d \int _ { 0 } ^ { T } \left| H _ { s } ^ { 2 } \right| d s < \infty \ a . s . } \end{array}$ 

We can then obtain a uniqueness property that is a consequence of the following result. 

Lemma 6.4.2. Suppose the process $\textstyle \int _ { 0 } ^ { t } K _ { s } d s = M _ { t }$ is a continuous martingale, where $\begin{array} { r } { \int _ { 0 } ^ { T } | K _ { s } | d s < \infty \ a . s . } \end{array}$ 4<sub>. Then</sub> $M _ { t } = 0$ a.s. for all $t \leq T$ , and there is a set $N \subset \Omega$ of measure zero such that, for $\omega \notin N , K _ { s } ( \omega ) = 0$ for almost all s. 

Proof. Suppose initially that $\begin{array} { r } { \int _ { 0 } ^ { T } | K _ { s } | d s \le C < \infty \mathrm { ~ a . s ~ } } \end{array}$ . Then, with $\begin{array} { r } { t _ { i } ^ { n } = \frac { i T } { N } } \end{array}$ for $0 \leq i \leq n$ , we have 

$$
\begin{array}{l} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} (M _ {t _ {i} ^ {n}} - M _ {t _ {i - 1} ^ {n}}) ^ {2} \leq \sup _ {i} \left| M _ {t _ {i} ^ {n}} - M _ {t _ {i - 1} ^ {n}} \right| \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \left| M _ {t _ {i} ^ {n}} - M _ {t _ {i - 1} ^ {n}} \right| \\ \qquad = \sup _ {i} \left| M _ {t _ {i} ^ {n}} - M _ {t _ {i - 1} ^ {n}} \right| \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \left| \int_ {t _ {i - 1} ^ {n}} ^ {t _ {i} ^ {n}} K _ {s} d s \right| \\ \qquad \leq \sup _ {i} \left| M _ {t _ {i} ^ {n}} - M _ {t _ {i - 1} ^ {n}} \right| \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \int_ {t _ {i - 1} ^ {n}} ^ {t _ {i} ^ {n}} | K _ {s} |   d s \\ \qquad \leq C \sup _ {i} \left| M _ {t _ {i} ^ {n}} - M _ {t _ {i - 1} ^ {n}} \right|. \end{array}
$$

Consequently, $\begin{array} { r } { \operatorname* { l i m } _ { n \to \infty } \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { n } ( M _ { t _ { i } ^ { n } } - M _ { t _ { i - 1 } ^ { n } } ) ^ { 2 } = 0 \mathrm { a . s . } } \end{array}$ , so by the bounded convergence theorem, we have 

$$
\lim _ {n \to \infty} E \left(\sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \left(M _ {t _ {i} ^ {n}} - M _ {t _ {i - 1} ^ {n}}\right) ^ {2}\right) = 0 = E \left(M _ {t} ^ {2} - M _ {0} ^ {2}\right),
$$

as M is a martingale. By definition, $M _ { 0 } = 0$ a.s. Consequently, $M _ { t } = 0 \ \mathrm { a . s . }$ 2 and so $M _ { t } = 0 \ \mathrm { a . s }$ . for $t \leq T$ 

Now relax the assumption that $\begin{array} { r } { \int _ { 0 } ^ { T } | K _ { s } | d s \ } \end{array}$ is bounded. Write 

$$
T _ {n} = \inf \left\{0 \leq s \leq T: \int_ {0} ^ {s} | K _ {u} | d u \geq n \right\} \wedge T.
$$

Then $T _ { n }$ is a stopping time because K is adapted, and $\begin{array} { r } { \operatorname* { l i m } _ { n \to \infty } T _ { n } = T } \end{array}$ The preceding result shows that $M _ { t \wedge T _ { n } } = 0 \ \mathrm { a . s . }$ , and so $\begin{array} { r } { \operatorname* { l i m } _ { n \to \infty } M _ { t \wedge T _ { n } } = } \end{array}$ $0 = M _ { t } { \mathrm { ~ a . s . } }$ □ 

Corollary 6.4.3. Suppose M is a martingale of the form $\int _ { 0 } ^ { t } H _ { s } d W _ { s } +$ $\textstyle \int _ { 0 } ^ { t } K _ { s } d s$ with $\begin{array} { r } { \int _ { 0 } ^ { t } H _ { s } ^ { 2 } d s < \infty \ a . s . } \end{array}$ . and $\begin{array} { r } { \int _ { 0 } ^ { t } \left| K _ { s } \right| d s < \infty ~ a . s . } \end{array}$ . Then $\textstyle \int _ { 0 } ^ { t } K _ { s } d s$ is 4 4 4a martingale that is zero a.s. and there is a P-null set $N \subset \Omega$ 4such that, for ω $\notin N , K _ { s } ( \omega ) = 0$ for almost all s. 

Corollary 6.4.4. Suppose the Itˆo process X has representations 

$$
\begin{array}{l} {X _ {t} = X _ {0} + \int_ {0} ^ {t} K _ {s} d s + \int_ {0} ^ {t} H _ {s} d W _ {s},} \\ {X _ {t} = X _ {0} ^ {\prime} + \int_ {0} ^ {t} K _ {s} ^ {\prime} d s + \int_ {0} ^ {t} H _ {s} ^ {\prime} d W _ {s}.} \end{array}
$$

Then $X _ { 0 } = X _ { 0 } ^ { \prime } ~ a . s . , H _ { s } = H _ { s } ^ { \prime } ~ a . s . ( d s \times d P )$ , and $K _ { s } = K _ { s } ^ { \prime } \ a . s . ( d s \times d P )$ In particular, if X is a martingale, then $K = 0$ 

Proof. Clearly $X _ { 0 } = X _ { 0 } ^ { \prime }$ . Therefore 

$$
\int_ {0} ^ {t} (K _ {s} - K _ {s} ^ {\prime}) d s = \int_ {0} ^ {t} (H _ {s} ^ {\prime} - H _ {s}) d W _ {s},
$$

and $\begin{array} { r l r } { \int _ { 0 } ^ { t } ( K _ { s } - K _ { s } ^ { \prime } ) d s } \end{array}$ is a martingale. The result follows from Lemma 6.4.2. □ 

Remark 6.4.5. Suppose $( W _ { t } ) _ { t \geq 0 }$ is a Brownian motion and 

$$
\pi = \left\{0 = t _ {0} \leq t _ {1} \leq \dots \leq t _ {N} = t \right\}
$$

is a partition of [0, t]. Write 

$$
| \pi | = \max _ {i} (t _ {i + 1} - t _ {i}).
$$

Then 

$$
E \left(\sum_ {i = 0} ^ {N - 1} \left(W _ {t _ {i + 1}} - W _ {t _ {i}}\right) ^ {2}\right) = E \left(\sum_ {i = 0} ^ {N - 1} \left(W _ {t _ {i + 1}} ^ {2} - W _ {t _ {i}} ^ {2}\right)\right) = t.\tag{6.8}
$$

In fact, we can show that $\begin{array} { r } { \sum _ { i = 0 } ^ { N - 1 } ( W _ { t _ { i + 1 } } - W _ { t _ { i } } ) ^ { 2 } } \end{array}$ converges to t almost surely as $| \pi | \to 0$ 

Choose a sequence $\left( \pi _ { n } \right)$ of partitions with $| \pi _ { n } | \to 0$ as $n \to \infty$ . Write 

$$
Q _ {n} = \sum_ {\pi_ {n}} (W _ {t _ {i + 1}} - W _ {t _ {i}}) ^ {2};
$$

then we have shown that $Q _ { n } \ \to \ t$ in $L ^ { 2 }$ as $n  \infty$ . By Chebychev’s inequality, we have, for any $\varepsilon > 0$ , that 

$$
P (| Q _ {n} - t | > \varepsilon) \leq \frac {E ((Q _ {n} - t) ^ {2})}{\varepsilon^ {2}}.
$$

Set $E \left( ( Q _ { n } - t ) ^ { 2 } \right) \ = \ q _ { n }$ , so that $q _ { n } \to 0$ as $n  \infty$ . Choosing a sub-- sequence, we can assume that $q _ { n } < { \frac { 1 } { 2 ^ { 2 n } } }$ . Letting $\textstyle \varepsilon _ { n } \ = \ { \frac { 1 } { 2 ^ { n } } }$ and writing $\begin{array} { r } { A _ { n } = \left\{ \left| Q _ { n } - t \right| > \frac { 1 } { 2 ^ { n } } \right\} } \end{array}$ , we obtain $\begin{array} { r } { P ( A _ { n } ) \leq \frac { 1 } { 2 ^ { n } } } \end{array}$ , so that $\textstyle \sum _ { n = 1 } ^ { \infty } P ( A _ { n } ) < \infty$  By the first Borel-Cantelli lemma, it follows that $P ( \cap _ { n \geq 1 } A _ { n } ) = 0$ , and hence that $Q _ { n } \to t { \mathrm { ~ a . s . } }$ . as $n \to \infty$ 

For a general, continuous (local) martingale $( M _ { t } ) _ { t \geq 0 }$ 2 

$$
\lim _ {| \pi | \rightarrow 0} \sum_ {i = 0} ^ {N} \left(M _ {t _ {i + 1}} - M _ {t _ {i}}\right) ^ {2}
$$

exists and is a predictable, continuous increasing process denoted by $\langle M \rangle _ { t }$ From Jensen’s inequality, $M ^ { 2 }$ is a submartingale and it turns out that $\langle M \rangle$ is the unique (continuous) increasing process in the Doob-Meyer decomposition of $\bar { M } ^ { 2 }$ . This decomposition is entirely analogous to the Doob decomposition described in Section 5.3, but the technical complexities in volved are substantially greater in continuous time. For details, see the development in [109, Chapter 10] or [199, Chapter 3]. M is called the (predictable) quadratic variation of M. Consequently, (6.8) states that for a Brownian motion W, 

$$
\langle W \rangle_ {t} = t.
$$

For $H \in { \widehat { \mathcal { H } } }$ we have seen that $\begin{array} { r } { M _ { t } = \int _ { 0 } ^ { t } H _ { s } d W _ { s } } \end{array}$ is a local martingale. It ,is shown in [109] that in this case 

$$
\langle M \rangle_ {t} = \int_ {0} ^ {t} H _ {s} ^ {2} d s \mathrm{a.s.}
$$

In some sense, (6.8) indicates that, very formally, $( d W ) ^ { 2 } \simeq d t$ , or $( d W ) \simeq$ $\sqrt { d t }$ 

Suppose X is an Itˆo process on $0 \leq t \leq T$ 2 

$$
X _ {t} = X _ {0} + \int_ {0} ^ {t} K _ {s} d s + \int_ {0} ^ {t} H _ {s} d W _ {s},\tag{6.9}
$$

where $\textstyle \int _ { 0 } ^ { T } | K _ { s } | d s < \infty$ a.s. and $\begin{array} { r } { \int _ { 0 } ^ { T } \left| H _ { s } \right| ^ { 2 } d s < \infty } \end{array}$ a.s. Considering partitions $\pi = \{ 0 = t _ { 0 } \leq t _ { 1 } \leq \cdot \cdot \cdot \leq t _ { N } = t \}$ of $[ 0 , t ]$ , it can be shown that 

$$
\lim _ {| \pi | \rightarrow 0} \sum_ {i = 0} ^ {N} \left(X _ {t _ {i + 1}} - X _ {t _ {i}}\right) ^ {2}
$$

converges a.s. to 

$$
\int_ {0} ^ {t} | H _ {s} | ^ {2} d s.
$$

That is, $\langle X \rangle _ { t } = \langle M \rangle _ { t } ,$ where $\begin{array} { r } { M _ { t } = \int _ { 0 } ^ { t } H _ { s } d W _ { s } } \end{array}$ is the martingale term in the representation (6.9) of X. 

Again, if X is a diferentiable process (that is, if $H _ { s } = 0$ in (6.9)), then the usual chain rule states that, for a diferentiable function $f _ { ; }$ 

$$
f (X _ {t}) = f (X _ {0}) + \int_ {0} ^ {t} f ^ {\prime} (X _ {s}) d X _ {s}.
$$

However, if X is an Itˆo process, the diferentiation rule (commonly known as the Itˆo formula) has the following form. 

Theorem 6.4.6. Suppose $\{ X _ { t } \} _ { t \ge 0 }$ is an Itˆo process of the form 

$$
X _ {t} = X _ {0} + \int_ {0} ^ {t} K _ {s} d s + \int_ {0} ^ {t} H _ {s} d W _ {s}.
$$

Suppose f is twice diferentiable. Then 

$$
f (X _ {t}) = f (X _ {0}) + \int_ {0} ^ {t} f ^ {\prime} (X _ {s}) d X _ {s} + \frac {1}{2} \int_ {0} ^ {t} f ^ {\prime \prime} (X _ {s}) d \left<   X \right> _ {s}.
$$

Here, by definition, $\begin{array} { r } { \langle X \rangle _ { t } = \int _ { 0 } ^ { t } H _ { s } ^ { 2 } d s , } \end{array}$ ; that is, the (predictable) quadratic 4variation of X is the quadratic variation of its martingale component 

$$
\int_ {0} ^ {t} H _ {s} d W _ {s}.
$$

Also, 

$$
\int_ {0} ^ {t} f ^ {\prime} (X _ {s}) d X _ {s} = \int_ {0} ^ {t} f ^ {\prime} (X _ {s}) K _ {s} d s + \int_ {0} ^ {t} f ^ {\prime} (X _ {s}) H _ {s} d W _ {s}.
$$

For a proof see [109]. More generally, the diferentiation rule can be proved in the following form. 

Theorem 6.4.7. If $F : [ 0 , \infty ) \times \mathbb { R } \to \mathbb { R }$ is continuously diferentiable in the first component and twice continuously diferentiable in the second, then 

$$
\begin{array}{r l} & F (t, X _ {t}) = F (0, X _ {0}) + \int_ {0} ^ {t} \frac {\partial F}{\partial s} (s, X _ {s}) d s \\ & \qquad + \int_ {0} ^ {t} \frac {\partial F}{\partial x} (s, X _ {s}) d X _ {s} + \frac {1}{2} \int_ {0} ^ {t} \frac {\partial^ {2} F}{\partial x ^ {2}} (s, X _ {s}) d \langle X \rangle_ {s}. \end{array}
$$

Example 6.4.8. (i) Let us consider the case when $K _ { s } = 0 , H _ { s } = 1$ . Then 

$$
X _ {t} = X _ {0} + W _ {t},
$$

where $W _ { t }$ is standard Brownian motion. Taking $f ( x ) = x ^ { 2 }$ , we have $\langle X \rangle _ { t } = \langle W \rangle _ { t } = t$ so 

$$
X _ {t} ^ {2} = X _ {0} ^ {2} + 2 \int_ {0} ^ {t} W _ {s} d W _ {s} + \frac {1}{2} \int_ {0} ^ {t} 2 d s.
$$

That is, 

$$
X _ {t} ^ {2} - X _ {0} ^ {2} - t = 2 \int_ {0} ^ {t} W _ {s} d W _ {s}.
$$

For any $T < \infty$ , we have $\begin{array} { r } { E \left( \int _ { 0 } ^ { T } W _ { s } ^ { 2 } d s \right) < \infty } \end{array}$ , so from Theorem 6.3.6, $\textstyle \int _ { 0 } ^ { t } W _ { s } d W _ { s }$ is a martingale. If $X _ { 0 } = 0$ , then $X _ { t } = W _ { t }$ and we see that $\ddot { W } _ { t } ^ { 2 } - t$ is a martingale. 

(ii) An often-used model for a price process is the so-called ‘log-normal model. In this case, it is supposed the price process $S _ { t }$ evolves according to the stochastic dynamics 

$$
\frac {d S _ {t}}{S _ {t}} = \mu d t + \sigma d W _ {t},\tag{6.10}
$$

where $\mu$ and $\sigma$ are real constants and $S _ { 0 } = X _ { 0 }$ . This means that 

$$
S _ {t} = X _ {0} + \int_ {0} ^ {t} S _ {s} \mu d s + \int_ {0} ^ {t} S _ {s} \sigma d W _ {s}.
$$

Assuming such a process S exists, it is therefore an Itˆo process with 

$$
K _ {s} = \mu S _ {s},
$$

$$
H _ {s} = \sigma S _ {s}.
$$

Then $\begin{array} { r } { \langle X \rangle _ { t } = \int _ { 0 } ^ { t } \sigma ^ { 2 } S _ { s } ^ { 2 } d s } \end{array}$ . Assuming $S _ { t } > 0$ and applying Itˆo’s formula with $f ( x ) = \log x$ (formally, because the logarithmic function is not twice continuously diferentiable everywhere), 

$$
\begin{array}{l} \log S _ {t} = \log X _ {0} + \int_ {0} ^ {t} \mu \frac {d S _ {s}}{S _ {s}} + \frac {1}{2} \int_ {0} ^ {t} \left(- \frac {1}{S _ {s} ^ {2}}\right) \sigma^ {2} S _ {s} ^ {2} d s \\ \qquad = \log X _ {0} + \int_ {0} ^ {t} \left(\mu - \frac {\sigma^ {2}}{2}\right) d s + \int_ {0} ^ {t} \sigma d W _ {s} \\ \qquad = \log X _ {0} + \left(\mu - \frac {\sigma^ {2}}{2}\right) t + \sigma W _ {t}. \end{array}
$$

Consequently, 

$$
S _ {t} = X _ {0} \exp \left\{\left(\mu - \frac {\sigma^ {2}}{2}\right) t + \sigma W _ {t} \right\}.
$$

Exercise 6.4.9. Consider the function 

$$
F (t, x) = X _ {0} \exp \left\{\left(\mu - \frac {\sigma^ {2}}{2}\right) t + \sigma x \right\}.
$$

Apply the Itˆo formula of Theorem 6.4.7 to $S _ { t } = F ( t , W _ { t } )$ to show that $S _ { t }$ does satisfy the log-normal equation (6.10). This ‘justifies’ our formal application of the Itˆo formula. 

Exercise 6.4.10. Let B be a Brownian motion, and suppose that the processes $X , Y$ have dynamics given by 

$$
\begin{array}{r c l} {d X _ {t}} & = & {X _ {t} (\mu_ {X} d t + \sigma_ {X} d B _ {t}),} \\ {d Y _ {t}} & = & {Y _ {t} (\mu_ {Y} d t + \sigma_ {Y} d B _ {t}).} \end{array}
$$

Define Z by $\begin{array} { r } { Z _ { t } = \frac { Y _ { t } } { X _ { t } } } \end{array}$ . Show that Z is also log-normal, with dynamics 

$$
d Z _ {t} = Z _ {t} (\mu_ {Z} d t + \sigma_ {Z} d B _ {t}),
$$

and determine the coeficients $\mu _ { Z }$ and $\sigma _ { Z }$ in terms of the coeficients of X and Y. 

## Multidimensional Itˆo Processes

Definition 6.4.11. Suppose we have a probability space $( \Omega , { \mathcal { F } } , P )$ with a filtration $( \mathcal F _ { t } ) _ { t \geq 0 }$ . An m-dimensional $( \mathcal { F } _ { t } )$ -Brownian motion is a process $W _ { t } = \left( W _ { t } ^ { 1 } , W _ { t } ^ { 2 } , \cdot \cdot \cdot , W _ { t } ^ { m } \right)$ whose components $W _ { t } ^ { i }$ are standard, independent $( \dot { \mathcal { F } _ { t } } )$ -Brownian motions. 

We can extend our definition of an Itˆo process to the situation where the (scalar) stochastic integral involves an m-dimensional Brownian motion. 

Definition 6.4.12. $( X _ { t } ) _ { 0 \leq t \leq T }$ is an Itˆo process if 

$$
X _ {t} = X _ {0} + \int_ {0} ^ {t} K _ {s} d s + \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {m} \int_ {0} ^ {t} H _ {s} ^ {i} d W _ {s} ^ {i},
$$

where the K and $H ^ { i }$ are adapted to $\begin{array} { r } { ( \mathcal { F } _ { t } ) , \int _ { 0 } ^ { T } | K _ { s } | d s < \infty \mathrm { a . s . } } \end{array}$ , and 

$$
\int_ {0} ^ {T} \left| H _ {s} ^ {i} \right| ^ {2} d s <   \infty \text { a.s.   for   all } i = 1, 2, \ldots , m.
$$

An n-dimensional Itˆo process is then a process $X _ { t } = ( X _ { t } ^ { 1 } , \ldots , X _ { t } ^ { N } ) $ 2 each component of which is an Itˆo process in the sense of Definition 6.4.12. The diferentiation rule takes the following form. 

Theorem 6.4.13. Suppose $X _ { t } = ( X _ { t } ^ { 1 } , \ldots , X _ { t } ^ { N } ) $ is an n-dimensional Itˆo process with 

$$
X _ {t} ^ {i} = X _ {0} ^ {i} + \int_ {0} ^ {t} K _ {s} ^ {i} d s + \sum_ {j = 1} ^ {m} \int_ {0} ^ {t} H _ {s} ^ {i j} d W _ {s} ^ {j},
$$

and suppose $f \ : / \theta , T ] \times \mathbb { R } ^ { n } \  \ \mathbb { R }$ is in $C ^ { 1 , 2 }$ (the space of functions once continuously diferentiable in t and twice continuously diferentiable in $x \in$ $\mathbb { R } ^ { n } )$ . Then 

$$
\begin{array}{l} f (t, X _ {t} ^ {1}, \ldots , X _ {t} ^ {n}) = f (0, X _ {0} ^ {1}, \ldots , X _ {0} ^ {n}) + \int_ {0} ^ {t} \frac {\partial f}{\partial s} (s, X _ {s} ^ {1}, \ldots , X _ {s} ^ {n}) d s \\ \qquad + \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \int_ {0} ^ {t} \frac {\partial f}{\partial x _ {i}} (s, X _ {s} ^ {1}, \ldots , X _ {s} ^ {n}) d X _ {s} ^ {i} \\ \qquad + \frac {1}{2} \sum_ {i, j = 1} ^ {n} \int_ {0} ^ {t} \frac {\partial^ {2} f}{\partial x _ {i} \partial x _ {j}} (s, X _ {s} ^ {1}, \ldots , X _ {s} ^ {n}) d \left\langle X ^ {i}, X ^ {j} \right\rangle_ {s}. \end{array}
$$

Here 

$$
d X _ {s} ^ {i} = K _ {s} ^ {i} d s + \sum_ {j = 1} ^ {m} H _ {s} ^ {i, j} d W _ {s} ^ {j}, \quad d \left\langle X ^ {i}, X ^ {j} \right\rangle_ {s} = \sum_ {r = 1} ^ {m} H _ {s} ^ {i, r} H _ {s} ^ {j, r} d s.
$$

Remark 6.4.14. For components 

$$
X _ {t} ^ {p} = X _ {0} ^ {p} + \int_ {0} ^ {t} K _ {s} ^ {p} d s + \sum_ {j = 1} ^ {m} \int_ {0} ^ {t} H _ {s} ^ {p j} d W _ {s} ^ {j},
$$

$$
X _ {t} ^ {q} = X _ {0} ^ {q} + \int_ {0} ^ {t} K _ {s} ^ {q} d s + \sum_ {j = 1} ^ {m} \int_ {0} ^ {t} H _ {s} ^ {q j} d W _ {s} ^ {j},
$$

it is shown in [227] that for partitions $\pi = \{ 0 = t _ { 0 } \leq t _ { 1 } \leq \cdot \cdot \cdot \leq t _ { N } = t \}$ ， 

$$
\lim _ {| \pi | \to 0} \sum_ {i} \left(X _ {t _ {i + 1}} ^ {p} - X _ {t _ {i}} ^ {p}\right) \left(X _ {t _ {i + 1}} ^ {q} - X _ {t _ {i}} ^ {q}\right)
$$

converges in probability to 

$$
\int_ {0} ^ {t} \sum_ {r = 1} ^ {m} H _ {s} ^ {p r} H _ {s} ^ {q r} d s.
$$

This process is the predictable covariation of $X ^ { p }$ and $X ^ { q }$ and is denoted by 

$$
\langle X ^ {p} X ^ {q} \rangle_ {t} = \sum_ {r = 1} ^ {m} \int_ {0} ^ {t} H _ {s} ^ {p r} H _ {s} ^ {q r} d s.\tag{6.11}
$$

We note that $\langle X ^ { p } X ^ { q } \rangle$ is symmetric and bilinear as a function on Itˆo processes. 

Taking 

$$
Y _ {t} = Y _ {0} + \int_ {0} ^ {t} K _ {s} ^ {\prime} d s, \qquad X _ {t} = X _ {0} + \int_ {0} ^ {t} K _ {s} d s + \sum_ {j = 1} ^ {m} H _ {s} ^ {j} d W _ {s} ^ {j},
$$

we see that $\langle X , Y \rangle _ { t } = 0$ . Furthermore, formula (6.11) gives 

$$
\left\langle \int_ {0} ^ {t} H _ {s} ^ {p i} d W _ {s} ^ {i}, \int_ {0} ^ {t} H _ {s} ^ {q j} d W _ {s} ^ {j} \right\rangle = \left\{ \begin{array}{l l} \int_ {0} ^ {t} H _ {s} ^ {p i} H _ {s} ^ {q i} d s & \text {if i = j ,} \\ 0 & \text {if i\neq j .} \end{array} \right.
$$

Remark 6.4.15. We noted in 6.4.5 that if $\left( M _ { t } \right) _ { t \geq 0 }$ is a continuous local martingale, then $\langle M \rangle _ { t }$ is the unique continuous increasing process in the Doob-Meyer decomposition of the submartingale $M _ { t } ^ { 2 }$ . If 

$$
X _ {t} = X _ {0} + \int_ {0} ^ {t} K _ {s} d s + \int_ {0} ^ {t} H _ {s} d M _ {s},
$$

where H and K are adapted, $\begin{array} { r } { \int _ { 0 } ^ { T } \left| K _ { s } \right| d s < \infty \mathrm { a . s . } } \end{array}$ ., and $\begin{array} { r } { \int _ { 0 } ^ { T } H _ { s } ^ { 2 } d s < \infty \mathrm { a . s . } } \end{array}$ 2 4the diferentiation formula has the form 

$$
\begin{array}{r l} f (X _ {t}) = f (X _ {0}) + \int_ {0} ^ {t} \frac {\partial f}{\partial x} (X _ {s}) K _ {s} d s \\ & \quad + \int_ {0} ^ {t} \frac {\partial f}{\partial x} (X _ {s}) H _ {s} d M _ {s} + \frac {1}{2} \int_ {0} ^ {t} \frac {\partial^ {2} f}{\partial x ^ {2}} (X _ {s}) H _ {s} ^ {2} d \langle M \rangle_ {s}. \end{array}
$$

Using without proof the analogue of the Itˆo rule (Theorem 6.4.6) for general square integrable martingales M (see [109, p. 138]), we can prove the converse of Theorem 6.2.5. 

Theorem 6.4.16. Suppose $( W _ { t } ) _ { t \geq 0 }$ is a continuous (scalar) local martingale on the filtered probability space $\left( \Omega , \mathcal { F } , P , \mathcal { F } _ { t } \right)$ , such that $\left( W _ { t } ^ { 2 } - t \right) _ { t \geq 0 }$ is a local martingale. Then $( W _ { t } )$ is a Brownian motion. 

Proof. We must show that, for $0 \leq s \leq t$ , the random variable $W _ { t } - W _ { s }$ is independent of $\mathcal { F } _ { s }$ and is normally distributed with mean 0 and covariance $t - s$ 

In terms of characteristic functions, this means we must show that 

$$
E \left(e ^ {i u (W _ {t} - W _ {s})} \mid \mathcal {F} _ {s}\right) = E \left(e ^ {i u (W _ {t} - W _ {s})}\right) = \exp \left\{- \frac {u ^ {2} (t - s)}{2} \right\} \text {   for   all   } u \in \mathbb {R}.
$$

To this end, consider the (complex-valued) function 

$$
f (x) = e ^ {i u x}.
$$

Applying the diferentiation rule to the real and imaginary parts of $f ( x )$ 2 we have 

$$
f (W _ {t}) = e ^ {i u W _ {t}} = f (W _ {s}) + \int_ {s} ^ {t} i u e ^ {i u W _ {r}} d W _ {r} - \frac {1}{2} \int_ {s} ^ {t} u ^ {2} e ^ {i u W _ {r}} d r\tag{6.12}
$$

because d $\langle W \rangle _ { r } = d r$ by hypothesis. Furthermore, the real and imaginary parts of iu $\int _ { s } ^ { t } e ^ { i u W _ { r } } d W _ { r }$ are in fact square integrable martingales because the integrands are bounded by 1. Consequently, $\begin{array} { r } { E \left( i u \int _ { s } ^ { t } e ^ { i u W _ { r } } d W _ { r } \left| \mathcal { F } _ { s } \right. \right) = } \end{array}$ 0 a.s. For any $A \in \mathcal { F } _ { s }$ , we may multiply (6.12) by $\mathbf { 1 } _ { A } e ^ { - i u W _ { s } }$ and take expectations to deduce that 

$$
E \left(e ^ {i u (W _ {t} - W _ {s})} \mathbf {1} _ {A}\right) = P (A) - \frac {1}{2} u ^ {2} \int_ {0} ^ {t} E \left(e ^ {i u (W _ {r} - W _ {s})} \mathbf {1} _ {A}\right) d r.
$$

Solving this equation, we have 

$$
E \left(e ^ {i u (W _ {t} - W _ {s})} {\bf 1} _ {A}\right) = P (A) \exp \left\{- \frac {u ^ {2} (t - s)}{2} \right\}.
$$

□ 

## 6.5 Stochastic Diferential Equations

We first establish a useful result known as Gronwall’s lemma. 

Lemma 6.5.1. Suppose α and $\beta$ are integrable functions on $[ a , b ]$ . If there is a constant H such that 

$$
\alpha (t) \leq \beta (t) + H \int_ {a} ^ {t} \alpha (s) d s f o r t \in [ a, b ],\tag{6.13}
$$

then 

$$
\alpha (t) \leq \beta (t) + H \int_ {a} ^ {t} e ^ {H (t - s)} \beta (s) d s.
$$

Note that $i f \beta ( t ) = B$ , a constant, then 

$$
\alpha (t) \leq B e ^ {H (t - a)}.\tag{6.14}
$$

Proof. Write 

$$
A (t) = \int_ {a} ^ {t} \alpha (s) d s, \quad g (t) = A (t) e ^ {- H t}.
$$

Then 

$$
g ^ {\prime} (t) = \alpha (t) e ^ {- H t} - H A (t) e ^ {- H t} \leq \beta (t) e ^ {- H t}
$$

from (6.13). Integrating, we obtain 

$$
g (t) - g (a) \leq \int_ {a} ^ {t} \beta (s) e ^ {- H s} d s.
$$

That is, 

$$
A (t) \leq e ^ {H t} \int_ {a} ^ {t} \beta (s) e ^ {- H s} d s.
$$

Using (6.13) again, we have 

$$
\alpha (t) \leq \beta (t) + H A (t) = \beta (t) + H \int_ {a} ^ {t} \beta (s) e ^ {H (t - s)} d s.
$$

Definition 6.5.2. Suppose $( \Omega , { \mathcal { F } } , P )$ is a probability space with a filtration $( \mathcal { F } _ { t } ) _ { 0 \leq t \leq T }$ . Let $( W _ { t } ) = ( ( W _ { t } ^ { 1 } , \dots , W _ { t } ^ { m } ) )$ be an m-dimensional $( \mathcal F _ { t } ) .$ Brownian motion and $f ( x , t )$ and $\sigma ( x , t )$ be measurable functions of $x \in \mathbb { R } ^ { n }$ and $t \in [ 0 , T ]$ with values in $\mathbb { R } ^ { n }$ and $L ( \mathbb { R } ^ { m } , \mathbb { R } ^ { n } )$ , the space of $m \times n$ matrices, respectively. We take $\xi$ to be an $\mathbb { R } ^ { n }$ -valued, $\mathcal { F } _ { 0 }$ -measurable random variable. 

A process $X _ { t } , \ 0 \leq t \leq T$ is a solution of the stochastic diferential equation 

$$
d X _ {t} = f (X _ {t}, t) d t + \sigma (X _ {t}, t) d W _ {t}
$$

with initial condition $X _ { 0 } = \xi$ if for all t the integrals 

$$
\int_ {0} ^ {t} f (X _ {s}, s) d s \mathrm{and} \int_ {0} ^ {t} \sigma (X _ {s}, s) d W _ {s}
$$

are well-defined and 

$$
X _ {t} = \xi + \int_ {0} ^ {t} f (X _ {s}, s) d s + \int_ {0} ^ {t} \sigma (X _ {s}, s) d W _ {s} \mathrm{a.s.}\tag{6.15}
$$

Theorem 6.5.3. Suppose the assumptions of Definition 6.5.2 apply. In addition, assume that $\xi , f$ , and σ satisfy 

$$
