| f (x, t) - f (x ^ {\prime}, t) | + | \sigma (x, t) - \sigma (x ^ {\prime}, t) | \leq K | x - x ^ {\prime} |,\tag{6.16}
$$

$$
\left| f (x, t) \right| ^ {2} + \left| \sigma (x, t) \right| ^ {2} \leq K _ {0} ^ {2} \left(1 + | x | ^ {2}\right),\tag{6.17}
$$

$$
E \left(\left| \xi \right| ^ {2}\right) <   \infty .
$$

Then there is a solution X of (6.15) such that 

$$
E \left(\sup _ {0 \leq t \leq T} | X _ {t} | ^ {2}\right) <   C \left(1 + E \left(| \xi | ^ {2}\right)\right).
$$

Note, for the matrix $\sigma$ , that $\left| \sigma \right| ^ { 2 } = \mathrm { T r } ( \sigma \sigma ^ { * } )$ . This solution is unique in the sense that, if X- is also a solution, then they are indistinguishable in the sense of Definition 6.1.12. 

Proof. Uniqueness: Suppose that X and $X ^ { \prime }$ are solutions of (6.15). Then, for all $t \in [ 0 , T ]$ 2 

$$
X _ {t} - X _ {t} ^ {\prime} = \int_ {0} ^ {t} \left(f (X _ {s}, s) - f (X _ {s} ^ {\prime}, s)\right) d s + \int_ {0} ^ {t} \left(\sigma (X _ {s}, s) - \sigma (X _ {s} ^ {\prime}, s)\right) d W _ {s}.
$$

Therefore 

$$
\begin{array}{c} | X _ {t} - X _ {t} ^ {\prime} | ^ {2} \leq 2 \left(\int_ {0} ^ {t} (f (X _ {s}, s) - f (X _ {s} ^ {\prime}, s)) d s\right) ^ {2} \\ + 2 \left(\int_ {0} ^ {t} (\sigma (X _ {s}, s) - \sigma (X _ {s} ^ {\prime}, s)) d W _ {s}\right) ^ {2}. \end{array}
$$

Taking expectations, we obtain 

$$
\begin{array}{c} E \left(| X _ {t} - X _ {t} ^ {\prime} | ^ {2}\right) \leq 2 \int_ {0} ^ {t} E \left((f (X _ {s}, s) - f (X _ {s} ^ {\prime}, s)) ^ {2}\right) d s \\ + 2 \int_ {0} ^ {t} E \left(| \sigma (X _ {s}, s) - \sigma (X _ {s} ^ {\prime}, s) | ^ {2}\right) d s. \end{array}
$$

Write $\phi ( t ) = E \left( | X _ { t } - X _ { t } ^ { \prime } | ^ { 2 } \right)$ and use the Lipschitz conditions (6.16) to deduce that 

$$
\phi (t) \leq 2 (T + 1) K ^ {2} \int_ {0} ^ {t} \phi (s) d s.
$$

Gronwall’s inequality (Lemma 6.5.1) therefore implies that $\phi ( t ) = 0$ for all $t \in [ 0 , T ]$ . Consequently, 

$$
| X _ {t} - X _ {t} ^ {\prime} | = 0 \mathrm{a.s.}
$$

The process $| X _ { t } - X _ { t } ^ { \prime } |$ is continuous, so there is a set $N \in \mathcal { F } _ { 0 }$ of measure zero such that if $\omega \not \in N , X _ { t } ( \omega ) = X _ { t } ^ { \prime } ( \omega )$ for all $t \in [ 0 , T ]$ . That is, $X ^ { \prime }$ is a modification of X. 

Existence: Write $X _ { t } ^ { 0 } = \xi$ for $0 \leq t \leq T$ . Define a sequence of processes $\left( X _ { t } ^ { N } \right)$ by 

$$
X _ {t} ^ {N} = \xi + \int_ {0} ^ {t} f (X _ {s} ^ {n - 1}, s) d s + \int_ {0} ^ {t} \sigma (X _ {s} ^ {n - 1}, s) d W _ {s}.\tag{6.18}
$$

It can be shown that $\sigma ( X _ { s } ^ { n - 1 } , s ) \in \mathcal { H }$ , so the stochastic integrals are defined. 

Using arguments similar to those in the uniqueness proof, we can show that 

$$
E \left(\left| X _ {t} ^ {n + 1} - X _ {t} ^ {N} \right| ^ {2}\right) \leq L \int_ {0} ^ {t} E \left(\left| X _ {s} ^ {n} - X _ {s} ^ {n - 1} \right| ^ {2}\right) d s,\tag{6.19}
$$

where $L = 2 ( 1 + T ) K ^ { 2 }$ . Iterating (6.19), we see that 

$$
E \left(\left| X _ {t} ^ {n + 1} - X _ {t} ^ {N} \right| ^ {2}\right) \leq L ^ {n} \int_ {0} ^ {t} \frac {(t - s) ^ {n - 1}}{(n - 1) !} E \left(\left| X _ {s} ^ {1} - \xi \right| ^ {2}\right) d s
$$

and 

$$
E \left(\left| X _ {s} ^ {1} - \xi \right| ^ {2}\right) \leq L T K ^ {2} \left(1 + E \left(| \xi | ^ {2}\right)\right).
$$

Therefore 

$$
E \left(\left| X _ {t} ^ {n + 1} - X _ {t} ^ {N} \right| ^ {2}\right) \leq C \frac {T ^ {n}}{n !}.\tag{6.20}
$$

Also, 

$$
\begin{array}{l} \sup _ {0 \leq t \leq T} \left| X _ {t} ^ {n + 1} - X _ {t} ^ {N} \right| \leq \int_ {0} ^ {T} \left| f \left(X _ {s} ^ {n}, s\right) - f \left(X _ {s} ^ {n - 1}, s\right) \right| d s \\ \qquad + \sup _ {0 \leq t \leq T} \left| \int_ {0} ^ {t} \left(\sigma (X _ {s} ^ {n}, s) - \sigma (X _ {s} ^ {n - 1}, s)\right) d W _ {s} \right|; \end{array}
$$

so, using the vector form of Doob’s inequality (Corollary 6.2.17), we have 

$$
\begin{array}{l} E \left(\sup _ {0 \leq t \leq T} \left| X _ {t} ^ {n + 1} - X _ {t} ^ {n} \right| ^ {2}\right) \leq 2 T K ^ {2} \int_ {0} ^ {T} E \left(\left| X _ {s} ^ {n} - X _ {s} ^ {n - 1} \right| ^ {2}\right) d s \\ \qquad + C E \left(\int_ {0} ^ {T} \left| X _ {s} ^ {n} - X _ {s} ^ {n - 1} \right| ^ {2} d s\right) \\ \qquad \leq C _ {1} \frac {T ^ {n - 1}}{(n - 1) !} \end{array}
$$

using (6.20). Consequently, 

$$
\sum_ {n = 1} ^ {\infty} P \left(\sup _ {0 \leq t \leq T} \left| X _ {t} ^ {n + 1} - X _ {t} ^ {n} \right| > \frac {1}{n ^ {2}}\right) \leq \sum_ {n = 1} ^ {\infty} n ^ {4} C _ {1} \frac {T ^ {n - 1}}{(n - 1) !}.
$$

The series on the right converges. Therefore, almost surely, the series $\begin{array} { r } { \xi + \sum _ { n = 0 } ^ { \infty } ( X _ { t } ^ { n + 1 } - X _ { t } ^ { n } ) } \end{array}$ converges uniformly in t, and so $X _ { t } ^ { n }$ converges to some $X _ { t }$ uniformly in t. 

Each $X ^ { n }$ is a continuous process, so X is a continuous process. Now 

$$
\begin{array}{r l} & E \left(| X _ {t} ^ {n} | ^ {2}\right) \leq 3 \left[ E \left(| \xi | ^ {2}\right) + K _ {0} ^ {2} T \int_ {0} ^ {t} \left(1 + E \left(| X _ {s} ^ {n - 1} | ^ {2}\right)\right) d s \right. \\ & \left. + K _ {0} ^ {2} \int_ {0} ^ {t} \left(1 + E \left(| X _ {s} ^ {n - 1} | ^ {2}\right)\right) d s \right], \end{array}
$$

so 

$$
E \left(\left| X _ {t} ^ {n} \right| ^ {2}\right) \leq C \left(1 + E \left(\left| \xi \right| ^ {2}\right)\right) + C \int_ {0} ^ {t} E \left(\left| X _ {s} ^ {n - 1} \right| ^ {2}\right) d s.
$$

By recurrence, taking $C > 1$ 

$$
\begin{array}{l} E \left(| X _ {t} ^ {n} | ^ {2}\right) \leq \left(1 + E \left(| \xi | ^ {2}\right)\right) \left(C + C ^ {2} t + \dots + C ^ {n - 1} \frac {t ^ {n}}{n !}\right) \\ \quad \leq C \left(1 + E \left(| \xi | ^ {2}\right)\right) e ^ {C t}. \end{array}
$$

Using the bounded convergence theorem, we can take the limit in (6.18) to deduce that 

$$
X _ {t} = \xi + \int_ {0} ^ {t} f (X _ {s}, s) d s + \int_ {0} ^ {t} \sigma (X _ {s}, s) d W _ {s} \mathrm{a.s.}
$$

Therefore, X is the unique solution of the equation (6.15). 

## 6.6 Markov Property of Solutions of SDEs

Definition 6.6.1. Let $( \Omega , { \mathcal { F } } , P )$ be a probability space with filtration $( \mathcal { F } _ { t } ) _ { t \geq 0 }$ . An adapted process $( X _ { t } )$ is said to be a Markov process with respect to the filtration $( \mathcal { F } _ { t } )$ if 

$$
E \left(f (X _ {t})   | \mathcal {F} _ {s}\right) = E \left(f (X _ {t})   | X _ {s}\right) \text { a.s. for all } t \geq s \geq 0
$$

for every bounded real-valued Borel function f defined on $\mathbb { R } ^ { d }$ 

Consider a stochastic diferential equation as in (6.15) with coeficients satisfying the conditions of Theorem 6.5.3 so the solution exists. Consider a point $x \in \mathbb { R } ^ { n }$ and for $s \leq t$ write $X _ { s } ( x , t )$ for the solution process of the equation 

$$
X _ {s} (x, t) = x + \int_ {s} ^ {t} f \left(X _ {s} (x, u), u\right) d u + \int_ {s} ^ {t} \sigma \left(X _ {s} (x, u), u\right) d W _ {u}.\tag{6.21}
$$

We quote the following results. 

Theorem 6.6.2. $X _ { s } ( x , t )$ is a continuous function of its arguments, and if the coeficients f and σ are $C ^ { 1 }$ functions of their first argument, the solution $X _ { s } ( x , t )$ is $C ^ { 1 }$ in x. 

Proof. See Kunita [204]. 

Write $X _ { s } ( x , t , \omega )$ for the solution of (6.21), so $X _ { s } ( x , t , \omega ) : \mathbb { R } ^ { d } \times [ s , T ] \times$ $\Omega  \mathbb { R } ^ { d }$ , and $\mathcal { F } ^ { W } ( s , t )$ for the completion of the σ-field generated by $W _ { s + u } - W _ { s } , 0 \leq u \leq t - s$ 

Theorem 6.6.3. For $t \in [ s , T ]$ , the restriction of $X _ { s } ( x , u , \omega )$ to $\mathbb { R } ^ { d } \times [ s , t ] \times$ Ω is $\mathcal { B } ( \mathbb { R } ^ { d } ) \times \mathcal { B } ( [ s , t ] ) \times \mathcal { F } ^ { W } ( s , t )$ -measurable. 

Proof. [109, Lemma 14.23]. 

We next prove the ‘flow’ property of solutions of equation (6.21). 

Lemma 6.6.4. If $X _ { s } ( x , t )$ is the solution of (6.21) and $X _ { r } ( x , t )$ is the solution of (6.21) starting at time r with $r \leq s \leq t _ { \ast }$ then $X _ { r } ( x , t ) \ =$ $X _ { s } \left( X _ { r } ( x , s ) , t \right)$ in the sense that one is a modification of the other. 

Proof. By definition, 

$$
\begin{array}{l} X _ {r} (x, t) = x + \int_ {r} ^ {t} f \left(X _ {r} (x, u), u\right) d u + \int_ {r} ^ {t} \sigma \left(X _ {r} (x, u), u\right) d W _ {u} \\ \qquad = X _ {r} (x, s) + \int_ {s} ^ {t} f \left(X _ {r} (x, u), u\right) d u + \int_ {s} ^ {t} \sigma \left(X _ {r} (x, u), u\right) d W _ {u}. \end{array}\tag{6.22}
$$

However, for any $y \in \mathbb { R } ^ { n }$ ， 

$$
X _ {s} (y, t) = y + \int_ {s} ^ {t} f \left(X _ {s} (y, u), u\right) d u + \int_ {s} ^ {t} \sigma \left(X _ {s} (y, u), u\right) d W _ {u}.
$$

Therefore, using the continuity of the solution, 

$$
\begin{array}{l} X _ {s} \left(X _ {r} (x, s), t\right) = X _ {r} (x, s) + \int_ {s} ^ {t} f \left(X _ {s} (X _ {r} (x, s), u), u\right) d u \\ \qquad \qquad \qquad + \int_ {s} ^ {t} \sigma \left(X _ {s} (X _ {r} (x, s), u), u\right) d W _ {u}. \end{array}\tag{6.23}
$$

Using the uniqueness of the solution, we see from (6.22) and (6.23) that $X _ { r } ( x , s )$ is a modification of $X _ { s } \left( X _ { r } ( x , s ) , t \right)$ □ 

Before establishing the Markov property of solutions of (6.21), we prove a general result on conditional expectations. 

Lemma 6.6.5. Given a probability space $( \Omega , { \mathcal { G } } , P )$ and measurable spaces $( E , { \mathcal { E } } ) , ( F , { \mathcal { F } } )$ , suppose that ${ \mathcal { A } } \subset { \mathcal { G } }$ and $X : \Omega  E$ and $Y : \Omega  F$ are random variables such that X is -measurable and $Y$ is independent of . 

For any bounded real-valued Borel function Φ defined on $( E \times F , \mathcal { E } \times \mathcal { F } )$ 2 consider the function $\phi$ defined for all $x \in E$ by 

$$
\phi (x) = E \left(\Phi (x, Y)\right).
$$

Then $\phi$ is a Borel function on $( E , \mathcal { E } )$ and 

$$
E (\Phi (X, Y) | \mathcal {A}) = \phi (X) a. s.
$$

Proof. Write $P _ { Y }$ for the probability law of $Y$ . Then 

$$
\phi (x) = \int_ {F} \Phi (x, y) d P _ {Y} (y).
$$

The measurability of Φ follows from Fubini’s theorem. 

Suppose Z is any -measurable random variable. Write $P _ { X , Z }$ for the probability law of $( X , Z )$ . Then, because Y is independent of $( X , Z )$ 

$$
\begin{array}{l} E \left(\Phi (X, Y) Z\right) = \iint \Phi (x, y) z d P _ {X, Z} (x, z) d P _ {Y} (y) \\ \qquad = \int \left(\int \Phi (x, y) d P _ {Y} (y)\right) z d P _ {X, Z} (x, z) \\ \qquad = \int \phi (x) z d P _ {X, Z} (x, y) \\ \qquad = E \left(\phi (X) Z\right). \end{array}
$$

This identity is true for all such Z; the result follows. 

Lemma 6.6.6. Suppose $X _ { s } ( x , t , \omega )$ is the solution of (6.21) and $g : \mathbb { R } ^ { d } $ R is a bounded Borel-measurable function. Then 

$$
f (x, \omega) = g \left(X _ {s} (x, t, \omega)\right)
$$

is $B ( \mathbb { R } ^ { d } ) \times \mathcal { F } ^ { W } ( s , t )$ -measurable. 

Proof. Write for the collection of sets $A \in B ( \mathbb { R } ^ { d } )$ for which the lemma is true with $g = \mathbf { 1 } _ { A }$ . If $f ( x , \omega ) = \mathbf { 1 } _ { A } \left( X _ { s } ( x , t , \omega ) \right)$ , then 

$$
\{(x, \omega): f (x, \omega) = 1 \} = \{(x, \omega): X _ {s} (x, t, \omega) \in A \} \in \mathcal {B} (\mathbb {R} ^ {d}) \times \mathcal {F} ^ {W} (s, t).
$$

The lemma is therefore true for all $A \in B (  { \mathbb { R } } ^ { d } )$ , and the result follows for general g by approximation with simple functions. □ 

We now show that solutions of stochastic diferential equations of the form (6.21) are Markov processes with respect to the right-continuous (and completed) filtration $( \mathcal { F } _ { t } )$ generated by the Brownian motion $( W _ { t } ) _ { t \geq 0 }$ and the initial value $x \in \mathbb { R } ^ { d }$ 

Theorem 6.6.7. Suppose $X _ { 0 } ( x , t )$ is the solution of (6.21) such that $X _ { 0 } ( x , 0 ) = x \in \mathbb { R } ^ { d }$ . For any bounded real-valued Borel function g defined on $\mathbb { R } ^ { d }$ , we have 

$$
E \left(g (X _ {t})   | \mathcal {F} _ {s}\right) = E \left(g (X _ {t})   | X _ {s}\right) \text {   for   all   } 0 \leq s \leq t.
$$

More precisely, if 

$$
\phi (z) = E \left(g \left(X _ {s} (z, t)\right)\right),
$$

then 

$$
E \left(g (X _ {t})   | \mathcal {F} _ {s}\right) = \phi \left(X _ {0} (x, s)\right) a. s.
$$

Proof. Suppose $g : \mathbb { R } ^ { d }  \mathbb { R }$ is any bounded Borel-measurable function. As in Lemma 6.6.6, write $f ( x , \omega ) = g \left( X _ { s } ( x , t , \omega ) \right)$ . Then, for each $x \in \mathbb { R } ^ { d }$ 2 $f ( x , \cdot )$ is $\mathcal { F } ^ { W } ( s , t )$ -measurable and thus independent of $\mathcal { F } _ { s }$ 

Write, as in Lemma 6.6.5, 

$$
\phi (x) = E \left(g \left(X _ {s} (x, t, \omega)\right)\right).
$$

If $Z$ is any ${ \mathcal { F } } _ { s } .$ -measurable random variable, 

$$
E \left(g \left(X _ {s} (Z, t, \omega)\right) | \mathcal {F} _ {s}\right) = \phi (Z).\tag{6.24}
$$

From the flow property of the solutions, Lemma 6.6.4, it follows that 

$$
X _ {t} = X _ {0} (x, t) = X _ {s} \left(X _ {0} (x, s), t\right)
$$

and $X _ { 0 } ( x , s )$ is $\mathcal { F } _ { s } { \mathrm { - m e a s u r a b l { \epsilon } } }$ . Substituting $Z = X _ { 0 } ( x , s )$ in (6.24), therefore, 

$$
E \left(g \left(X _ {0} (x, t)\right) | \mathcal {F} _ {s}\right) = E \left(g \left(X _ {t}\right) | \mathcal {F} _ {s}\right) = \phi \left(X _ {0} (x, s)\right) = \phi \left(X _ {s}\right).
$$

Consequently, $E \left( g ( X _ { t } ) \left| \mathcal { F } _ { s } \right. \right) = E \left( g ( X _ { t } ) \left| X _ { s } \right. \right)$ and the result follows. 

Theorem 6.6.8. Suppose $X _ { 0 } ( x , s ) = X _ { s } \in \mathbb { R } ^ { d }$ is the solution of (6.21), and consider the process 

$$
\beta_ {s} (1, t) = \beta_ {t} = e ^ {- \int_ {s} ^ {t} r (u, X _ {u}) d u},
$$

where $r ( s , x )$ is a positive measurable function. Then 

$$
d \beta_ {t} = - r (t, X _ {t}) \beta_ {t} d t, \quad \beta_ {s} = 1,
$$

and the augmented process $( \beta _ { t } , X _ { t } ) \in \mathbb { R } ^ { d + 1 }$ is given by an equation similar to (6.21). Consequently, the augmented process is Markov and, for any bounded Borel function $f :  { \mathbb { R } ^ { d } } \to  { \mathbb { R } }$ , 

$$
E \left(e ^ {- \int_ {s} ^ {t} r (u, X _ {u}) d u} f (X _ {t})   | \mathcal {F} _ {s}\right) = \phi (X _ {s}),
$$

where 

$$
\phi (x) = E \left(e ^ {- \int_ {s} ^ {t} r (u, X _ {s} (x, u)) d u} f \left(X _ {s} (x, t)\right)\right).
$$

# Continuous-Time European Options

In this chapter, we shall develop a continuous-time theory that is the analogue of that in Chapters 1 to 3. The simple model will consist of a riskless bond and a risky asset, which can be thought of as a stock. The dynamics of our model are described in Section 7.1. The following two sections present the fundamental results of Girsanov and martingale representation. These are then applied to discuss the hedging and pricing of European options. In particular, we establish the famous results of Black and Scholes, results that are applied widely in the finance industry in spite of the simplified nature of the model. Recall that the Black-Scholes pricing formula for a European call was derived in Section 2.7 as the limit of a sequence of prices in binomial models. 

## 7.1 Dynamics

We describe the dynamics of the Black-Scholes option pricing model. Our processes will be defined on a complete probability space $( \Omega , { \mathcal { F } } , P )$ . The time parameter t will take values in the intervals $[ 0 , \infty )$ or [0, T]. We suppose the market contains a riskless asset, or bond, whose price at time t is $S _ { t } ^ { 0 }$ and a risky asset, or stock, whose price at time t is $S _ { t } ^ { 1 }$ 

Let r be a non-negative constant that represents the instantaneous interest rate on the bond. (This instantaneous interest rate should not be confused with the interest rate over a period of time in discrete models.) We then suppose that the evolution in the price of the bond $S _ { t } ^ { 0 }$ is described by the ordinary diferential equation 

$$
d S _ {t} ^ {0} = r S _ {t} ^ {0} d t.\tag{7.1}
$$

If the initial value at time 0 of the bond is $S _ { 0 } ^ { 0 } = 1$ , then (7.1) can be solved 

to give 

$$
S _ {t} ^ {0} = e ^ {r t} \mathrm{for} t \geq 0.\tag{7.2}
$$

Let $\mu$ and $\sigma > 0$ be constants and $( B _ { t } ) _ { t \geq 0 }$ be a standard Brownian motion on $( \Omega , { \mathcal { F } } , P )$ . We suppose that the evolution in the price of the risky asset $S _ { t } ^ { 1 }$ is described by the stochastic diferential equation 

$$
d S _ {t} ^ {1} = S _ {t} ^ {1} (\mu d t + \sigma d B _ {t}).\tag{7.3}
$$

If the initial price at time 0 of the risky asset is $S _ { 0 } ^ { 1 }$ , then (7.3) can be solved to give 

$$
S _ {t} ^ {1} = S _ {0} ^ {1} \exp \left\{\mu t - \frac {\sigma^ {2}}{2} t + \sigma B _ {t} \right\}.\tag{7.4}
$$

Taking logarithms, we have 

$$
\log S _ {t} ^ {1} = \log S _ {0} ^ {1} + \left(\mu - \frac {\sigma^ {2}}{2}\right) t + \sigma B _ {t},\tag{7.5}
$$

and we see that log $S _ { t } ^ { 1 }$ evolves like a Brownian motion with drift $\textstyle ( \mu - { \frac { \sigma ^ { 2 } } { 2 } } ) t$ and volatility $\sigma .$ . In particular, log $S _ { t } ^ { 1 }$ is a normal random variable, which is often expressed by saying $S _ { t } ^ { 1 }$ is ‘log-normal’. It is immediate from (7.4) and (7.5) that $\left( S _ { t } ^ { 1 } \right)$ has continuous trajectories, and log $S _ { t } ^ { 1 }$ has independent stationary increments $\begin{array} { r } { ( \mathrm { s o } \ \frac { S _ { t } ^ { 1 } - S _ { v } ^ { 1 } } { S _ { v } ^ { 1 } } } \end{array}$ is independent of the σ-field $\sigma ( S _ { u } ^ { 1 } : u \le v )$ and $\frac { S _ { t } ^ { 1 } - S _ { v } ^ { 1 } } { S _ { v } ^ { 1 } }$ is identically distributed to $\frac { S _ { t - v } ^ { 1 } - S _ { 0 } ^ { 1 } } { S _ { 0 } ^ { 1 } } \Big )$ 

## 7.2 Girsanov’s Theorem

Girsanov’s theorem shows how martingales, in particular Brownian motion, transform under a diferent probability measure. We first define certain spaces of martingales. 

The set of martingales for which convergence results hold is the set of uniformly integrable martingales. As we noted in Chapters 5 and 6, this is not a significant restriction if the time horizon is finite $( \mathrm { i . e . , } T < \infty )$ Recall Definition 5.3.1 applied to a martingale: if $( M _ { t } )$ is a martingale, for $0 \leq t < \infty$ or $0 \leq t \leq T , \left( M _ { t } \right)$ is uniformly integrable if 

$$
\int_ {\{| M _ {t} (\omega) | \geq K \}} | M _ {t} (\omega) | d P (\omega)
$$

converges to 0 uniformly in t as $K  \infty$ 

If $( X _ { t } ) _ { t \geq 0 }$ is any real, measurable process, we shall write 

$$
X _ {t} ^ {*} = \sup _ {s \leq t} | X _ {s} |.
$$

We shall write for the space of right-continuous, uniformly integrable martingales. Consistent with Notation 6.2.9, $\mathcal { M } _ { \ell \mathrm { o c } }$ will denote the set of processes that are locally in (i.e., we say that $M \in \mathcal { M } _ { \ell \mathrm { o c } }$ if there exists an increasing sequence of stopping times $\left( T _ { n } \right)$ such that $\mathcal { M } _ { t } ^ { T _ { n } } = \mathcal { M } _ { t \wedge T _ { n } } \in$ $\mathcal { M } )$ . We call $\mathcal { M } _ { \ell \mathrm { o c } }$ the space of local martingales. Let $\mathcal { L }$ be the subset of $\mathcal { M } _ { \ell \mathrm { o c } }$ consisting of those local martingales for which $M _ { 0 } = 0 ~ \mathrm { a . s }$ 

For $M \in \mathcal { M }$ and $p \in [ 1 , \infty ]$ , write 

$$
\left\| M \right\| _ {\mathcal {H} ^ {p}} = \left\| M _ {\infty} ^ {*} \right\| _ {p}.
$$

Here $\left\| \cdot \right\| _ { p }$ denotes the norm on $L ^ { p } ( \Omega , \mathcal { F } , \mathcal { P } )$ . Then $\mathcal { H } ^ { p }$ is the space of martingales in such that 

$$
\| M \| _ {\mathcal {H} ^ {p}} <   \infty .
$$

In particular, $\mathcal { H } ^ { 2 }$ is the space of square integrable martingales. 

Suppose $( \Omega , { \mathcal { F } } , P )$ is a probability space with a filtration $( \mathcal { F } _ { t } ) _ { t \geq 0 }$ . Let $Q$ be a second probability measure on $( \Omega , { \mathcal { F } } )$ that is absolutely continuous with respect to P. Write 

$$
M _ {t} = \left\{ \begin{array}{l l} \frac {d Q}{d P} & \text { if } t = \infty , \\ E \left(M _ {\infty}   |   \mathcal {F} _ {t}\right) & \text { if } t <   \infty . \end{array} \right.
$$

Remark 7.2.1. In continuous time, versions of martingales are considered that are right-continuous and have left limits. There is a right-continuous version of M with left limits if the filtration $( \mathcal { F } _ { t } )$ satisfies the usual conditions (see [109, Theorem 4.11]). 

Lemma 7.2.2. $( X _ { t } M _ { t } )$ is a local martingale under P if and only $i f \left( X _ { t } \right)$ is a local martingale under $Q$ 

Proof. We prove the result for martingales. The extension to local martingales can be found in [168, Proposition 3.3.8]. Suppose $s \leq t$ and $A \in \mathcal { F } _ { s }$ Then 

$$
\int_ {A} X _ {t} d Q = \int_ {A} X _ {t} M _ {t} d P = \int_ {A} X _ {s} M _ {s} d P = \int_ {A} X _ {s} d Q,
$$

and the result follows. 

Suppose $( \Omega , { \mathcal { F } } , P )$ is a probability space. Recall from Theorem 6.4.16 that a real process $( B _ { t } ) _ { t \geq 0 }$ is a standard Brownian motion if: 

a) $t  B _ { t } ( \omega )$ is continuous a.s., 

b) $B _ { t }$ is a (local) martingale, and 

c) $B _ { t } ^ { 2 } - t$ is a (local) martingale. 

This characterisation of Brownian motion using properties $\mathrm { a ) - c ) }$ is due to L´evy, and it is shown in Theorem 6.4.16 that these properties imply the other well-known properties of Brownian motion, including, for example, that B is a Gaussian process with independent increments. 

Write $\mathcal { F } _ { t } ^ { 0 } = \sigma \left( B _ { s } : s \leq t \right)$ for the $\sigma { - } \mathrm { f i e l d }$ on Ω generated by the history of the Brownian motion up to time t. Then $( \mathcal { F } _ { t } ) _ { t \geq 0 }$ will denote the rightcontinuous complete filtration generated by the $\mathcal { F } _ { t } ^ { 0 }$ 

We show how $\left( B _ { t } \right)$ behaves under a change of measure. 

Theorem 7.2.3 (Girsanov). Suppose $( \theta _ { t } ) _ { 0 \leq t \leq T }$ is an adapted, measurable process such that $\begin{array} { r } { \int _ { 0 } ^ { T } \theta _ { s } ^ { 2 } d s < \infty \ a . s . } \end{array}$ . and also so that the process 

$$
\Lambda_ {t} = \exp \left\{- \int_ {0} ^ {t} \theta_ {s} d B _ {s} - \frac {1}{2} \int_ {0} ^ {t} \theta_ {s} ^ {2} d s \right\}
$$

is an $( \mathcal { F } _ { t } , P )$ martingale. Define a new measure $Q _ { \theta }$ on $\mathcal { F } _ { T }$ by putting 

$$
\left. \frac {d Q _ {\theta}}{d P} \right| _ {\mathcal {F} _ {T}} = \Lambda_ {T}.
$$

Then the process 

$$
W _ {t} = B _ {t} + \int_ {0} ^ {t} \theta_ {s} d s
$$

is a standard Brownian motion on $( \mathcal { F } _ { t } , Q _ { \theta } )$ 

Remark 7.2.4. A suficient condition, widely known as Novikov’s condition, for Λ to be a martingale is that 

$$
E \left(\exp \left\{\frac {1}{2} \int_ {0} ^ {T} \theta_ {s} ^ {2} d s \right\}\right) <   \infty
$$

(see [109]). 

Proof. Using the Itˆo rule and definition of Λ, we see, as in Exercise 6.4.9, that 

$$
\Lambda_ {t} = 1 - \int_ {0} ^ {t} \Lambda_ {s} \theta_ {s} d B _ {s}.\tag{7.6}
$$

Clearly $\Lambda _ { t } > 0$ a.s. and as Λ is a martingale 

$$
E \left(\Lambda_ {t}\right) = 1.
$$

Now for $A \in \mathcal { F } _ { T } , Q _ { \theta } ( A ) \ : = \ : \int _ { A } \Lambda _ { T } d P \geq 0$ and $\begin{array} { r } { Q _ { \theta } ( \Omega ) \ = \ \int _ { \Omega } \Lambda _ { T } d P \ = \ } \end{array}$ $E \left( \Lambda _ { t } \right) = 1 , \textnormal { s o } Q _ { \theta }$ 4is a probability measure. 

To show that $( W _ { t } )$ is a standard Brownian motion, we verify that it satisfies the conditions a)-c) above, which are required for the application of Theorem 6.4.16. By definition, $( W _ { t } )$ is a continuous process almost surely, as $\left( B _ { t } \right)$ is continuous a.s. and an indefinite integral is a continuous process. For the second condition, we must show that $( W _ { t } )$ is a local $( \mathcal { F } _ { t } )$ -martingale under the measure $Q _ { \theta }$ . Equivalently, from Lemma 7.2.2 we must show that $( \Lambda _ { t } W _ { t } )$ is a local martingale under P. Applying the Itˆo rule to (7.6) and (W<sub>t</sub>), we have 

$$
\begin{array}{r l}&{\Lambda_ {t} W _ {t} = W _ {0} + \int_ {0} ^ {t} \Lambda_ {s} d W _ {s} + \int_ {0} ^ {t} W _ {s} d \Lambda_ {s} + \int_ {0} ^ {t} d \left<   \Lambda , W \right> _ {s}}\\&{\qquad = W _ {0} + \int_ {0} ^ {t} \Lambda_ {s} d B _ {s} + \int_ {0} ^ {t} \Lambda_ {s} \theta_ {s} d s - \int_ {0} ^ {t} W _ {s} \Lambda_ {s} \theta_ {s} d B _ {s} - \int_ {0} ^ {t} \Lambda_ {s} \theta_ {s} d s}\end{array}
$$

$$
= W _ {0} + \int_ {0} ^ {t} \Lambda_ {s} (1 - W _ {s} \theta_ {s}) d B _ {s}
$$

and, as a stochastic integral with respect to $B , \left( \Lambda _ { t } W _ { t } \right)$ is a (local) martingale under P. 

The third condition is established similarly since 

$$
W _ {t} ^ {2} = 2 \int_ {0} ^ {t} W _ {s} d W _ {s} + \langle W \rangle_ {t} = 2 \int_ {0} ^ {t} W _ {s} d W _ {s} + t.
$$

We must prove that $W _ { t } ^ { 2 } - t$ is a local $( \mathcal { F } _ { t } , Q _ { \theta } )$ -martingale. However, 

$$
W _ {t} ^ {2} - t = 2 \int_ {0} ^ {t} W _ {s} d W _ {s},
$$

and we have established that $W _ { s }$ is a (local) martingale under $Q _ { \theta }$ . Consequently, the stochastic integral is a (local) martingale under $Q _ { \theta }$ and the result follows. □ 

## Hitting Times of Brownian Motion

We shall need the following results on hitting times of Brownian motion. Their proofs involve an exponential martingale M of a form similar to Λ. Suppose $( B _ { t } ) _ { t \geq 0 }$ is a standard Brownian motion with $B _ { 0 } = 0$ adapted to the filtration $( \mathcal { F } _ { t } )$ . Write 

$$
T _ {a} = \inf \left\{s \geq 0: B _ {s} = a \right\} \text {   for   } a \in \mathbb {R}.\tag{7.7}
$$

As usual, we take inf $\{ \varnothing \} = \infty$ 

Theorem 7.2.5. $T _ { a }$ in (7.7) is a stopping time that is almost surely finite and 

$$
E \left(e ^ {- \lambda T _ {a}}\right) = e ^ {- \sqrt {2 \lambda} | a |} f o r \lambda \geq 0.
$$

Proof. Suppose $a \geq 0$ . Because B is continuous, we have, with $\mathbb { Q } ^ { + }$ denoting the positive rationals, 

$$
\{T_{a}\leq t\} = \bigcap_{\varepsilon \in \mathbb{Q}^{+}}\left\{\sup_{r\leq t}B_{r} > a - \varepsilon \right\} = \bigcap_{\varepsilon \in \mathbb{Q}^{+}}\bigcap_{\substack{r\in \mathbb{Q}^{+}\\ r\leq t}}\{B_{r} > a - \varepsilon \} \in \mathcal{F}_{t}.
$$

Consequently, $T _ { a }$ is a stopping time. 

For any $\sigma \geq 0$ , the process 

$$
M _ {t} = \exp \left\{\sigma B _ {t} - \frac {\sigma^ {2}}{2} t \right\}
$$

is an $( \mathcal { F } _ { t } )$ )-martingale by Theorem 6.2.5. For $n \in \mathbb { Z } ^ { + }$ , consider the stopping time $T _ { a } \wedge n$ . Then, from the optional stopping theorem (Theorem 6.2.10), we have 

$$
E \left(M _ {T _ {a} \wedge n}\right) = E \left(M _ {0}\right) = 1.
$$

However, 

$$
M _ {T _ {a} \wedge n} = \exp \left\{\sigma B _ {T _ {a} \wedge n} - \frac {\sigma^ {2}}{2} (T _ {a} \wedge n) \right\} \leq \exp \left\{\sigma a \right\}.
$$

Now if $T _ { a } < \infty$ , then li $_ { 1 } \ L _ { n \to \infty } M _ { T _ { a } \land n } = M _ { T _ { a } }$ . If $T _ { a } = \infty$ , then $B _ { t } \leq a$ for all $t \geq 0$ , so that $\begin{array} { r } { \operatorname* { l i m } _ { n  \infty } M _ { T _ { a } \wedge n } = 0 } \end{array}$ 

Using Lebesgue’s dominated convergence theorem, we have 

$$
E \left(\mathbf {1} _ {\{T _ {a} <   \infty \}} M _ {T _ {a}}\right) = 1.\tag{7.8}
$$

Now $B _ { T _ { a } } = a$ if $T _ { a } < \infty$ . Therefore, 

$$
E \left(\mathbf {1} _ {\{T _ {a} <   \infty \}} e ^ {\sigma a} e ^ {- \frac {\sigma^ {2}}{2} T _ {a}}\right) = 1;
$$

consequently, 

$$
E \left(\mathbf {1} _ {\{T _ {a} <   \infty \}} e ^ {\frac {\sigma^ {2}}{2} T _ {a}}\right) = e ^ {- \sigma a}.\tag{7.9}
$$

Letting $\sigma \to 0$ in (7.9), we see that 

$$
E \left(\mathbf {1} _ {\{T _ {a} <   \infty \}}\right) = P (T _ {a} <   \infty) = 1.
$$

Hence almost every sample path of the Brownian motion reaches the value a, and 

$$
E \left(e ^ {\frac {- \sigma^ {2}}{2} T _ {a}}\right) = e ^ {- \sigma a}.
$$

Now $\left( - B _ { t } \right)$ is also an ( <sub>t</sub>)-Brownian motion, so the case $a < 0$ can be deduced by noting that 

$$
T _ {a} = \inf _ {s \geq 0} \left\{s \geq 0: - B _ {s} = - a \right\}.
$$

An application of Girsanov’s theorem enables us to deduce the following extension. 

Corollary 7.2.6. Suppose $\mu , a$ are real numbers. Write 

$$
T _ {a} (\mu) = \inf \left\{t \geq 0: \mu t + B _ {t} = a \right\}.
$$

Then 

$$
E \left(e ^ {- \alpha T _ {a} (\mu)}\right) = \exp \left\{\mu a - | a | \sqrt {\mu^ {2} + 2 \alpha} \right\} f o r a l l \alpha > 0.
$$

Proof. Introduce the probability measure $Q$ by setting 

$$
\left. \frac {d Q}{d P} \right| _ {\mathcal {F} _ {t}} = \exp \left\{\mu B _ {t} - \frac {\mu^ {2}}{2} t \right\}.
$$

From Girsanov’s theorem, the process $\widetilde { B }$ is a standard Brownian motion under $Q$ , where 2 

$$
\widetilde {B} _ {t} = B _ {t} - \mu t.
$$

Clearly, the hitting time $T _ { a } ( \mu )$ of $\widetilde { B } _ { t } + \mu t$ is the same as the hitting time $T _ { a } ( 0 )$ of $B _ { t }$ . Therefore, for all $\alpha > 0$ and $t > 0$ , we have 

$$
\begin{array}{l} E \left(\exp \left\{- \alpha (T _ {a} (\mu) \wedge t) \right\}\right) \\ = E \left(\exp \left\{- \alpha (T _ {a} (0) \wedge t) \right\} \exp \left\{\mu B _ {T _ {a} (0) \wedge t} - \frac {\mu^ {2}}{2} (T _ {a} (0) \wedge t) \right\}\right). \end{array}
$$

Now exp $\{ - \alpha ( T _ { a } ( 0 ) \wedge t ) \} \le e ^ { - \alpha t }$ . Noting that $T _ { a } ( 0 ) = T _ { a }$ , we have for $t < T _ { a }$ that $t < \infty$ . Therefore 

$$
\exp \left\{\mu B _ {T _ {a} \wedge t} - \frac {\mu^ {2}}{2} (T _ {a} \wedge t) \right\} \mathbf {1} _ {\{t <   T _ {a} \}} \leq \exp \left\{\mu B _ {t} - \frac {\mu^ {2}}{2} t \right\},
$$

which has expected value 1, and 

$$
E \left(\exp \left\{- \alpha (T _ {a} \wedge t) \right\} \exp \left\{\mu B _ {T _ {a} \wedge t} - \frac {\mu^ {2}}{2} (T _ {a} \wedge t) \right\} \mathbf {1} _ {\{t <   T _ {a} \}}\right) \leq e ^ {- \alpha t}.
$$

Suppose initially that $a \geq 0$ , and write 

$$
\widetilde {M} _ {t} = \exp \left\{- \alpha (T _ {a} \wedge t) \right\} \exp \left\{\mu B _ {T _ {a} \wedge t} - \frac {\mu^ {2}}{2} (T _ {a} \wedge t) \right\}.
$$

Then $\widetilde { M } _ { t } \leq \exp { \{ \mu a \} }$ and, again by the dominated convergence theorem, 

$$
\begin{array}{r l} & E \left(\lim _ {t \to \infty} \widetilde {M} _ {t}\right) = E \Bigg (\mathbf {1} _ {\{t <   T _ {a} \}} e ^ {- \alpha T _ {a}} e ^ {\mu B _ {T _ {a}} - \frac {\mu^ {2}}{2} T _ {a}} \Bigg) \\ & \qquad = e ^ {\mu a} E \left(\mathbf {1} _ {\{T _ {a} <   \infty \}} e ^ {- (\alpha + \frac {\mu^ {2}}{2}) T _ {a}}\right) \\ & \qquad = e ^ {\mu a} e ^ {- \sqrt {2 \alpha + \mu^ {2}} | a |}. \end{array}
$$

Again the case when $a < 0$ can be discussed by considering $- B$ 

We have therefore established that 

$$
\begin{array}{r} E \left(\mathbf {1} _ {\{T _ {a} <   \infty \}} e ^ {- \alpha T _ {a}} e ^ {\mu B _ {T _ {a}} - \frac {\mu^ {2}}{2} T _ {a}}\right) = E \left(\mathbf {1} _ {\{T _ {a} (\mu) <   \infty \}} e ^ {- \alpha T _ {a} (\mu)}\right) \\ = e ^ {\mu a - \sqrt {2 \alpha + \mu^ {2}} | a |}. \end{array}
$$

Letting $\alpha  0$ , we see that 

$$
P (T _ {a} <   \infty) = e ^ {\mu a - | \mu a |},
$$

this probability being equal to 1 if $\mu$ and a have the same sign. Furthermore, as $e ^ { - \alpha T _ { a } } = 0$ on $\{ T _ { a } = \infty \}$ , we have 

$$
E \left(e ^ {- \alpha T _ {a} (\mu)}\right) = e ^ {\mu a - \sqrt {2 \alpha + \mu^ {2}} | a |}.
$$

## 7.3 Martingale Representation

We first recall concepts related to martingales and stable subspaces of martingales. 

Definition 7.3.1. Two local martingales M, $N \in \mathcal { M } _ { \ell \mathrm { o c } }$ are orthogonal if their product MN is in $\mathcal { L }$ 

Remark 7.3.2. We then write $M \perp N$ . Note that orthogonality implies that $M _ { 0 } N _ { 0 } = 0$ a.s. 

We now have the following result. 

Lemma 7.3.3. Suppose $M , N \in \mathcal H ^ { 2 }$ are orthogonal. For every stopping time $\tau$ the random variables $M _ { \tau } , N _ { \tau }$ are orthogonal in $L ^ { 2 } ( \Omega , \mathcal { F } , P )$ and $M N \in \mathcal H ^ { 1 }$ 

Conversely, if $M _ { 0 } N _ { 0 } = 0 ~ a . s$ . and the random variables $M _ { \tau } , N _ { \tau }$ are orthogonal in $L ^ { 2 }$ for every stopping time $\tau ,$ , then $M \perp N$ 

Proof. Because $M _ { \infty } ^ { * }$ and $N _ { \infty } ^ { * }$ are in $L ^ { 2 }$ , the product $M _ { \infty } ^ { * } N _ { \infty } ^ { * }$ is in $L ^ { 1 }$ Furthermore, 

$$
(M N) _ {\infty} ^ {*} = \sup _ {t} | M _ {t} N _ {t} | \leq M _ {\infty} ^ {*} N _ {\infty} ^ {*}
$$

so $M N \in \mathcal { H } ^ { 1 } \mathrm { ~ i f ~ } M \perp N$ , and $M _ { 0 } N _ { 0 } = 0 ~ \mathrm { a . s }$ . Consequently, 

$$
E \left(M _ {\tau} N _ {\tau}\right) = E \left(M _ {0} N _ {0}\right) = 0.
$$

Conversely, suppose that, for any stopping time $\tau ,$ we have $M _ { \tau } \in L ^ { 2 }$ and $N _ { \tau } \in L ^ { 2 }$ . Then $M _ { \tau } N _ { \tau } \in L ^ { 1 }$ , so $E \left( | M _ { \tau } N _ { \tau } | \right) < \infty$ and $E \left( M _ { \tau } N _ { \tau } \right) = 0$ . From [109, Lemma 4.18] this condition is suficient for $M N$ to be a uniformly integrable martingale, and the result follows. □ 

Notation 7.3.4. If $( X _ { t } ) _ { t \geq 0 }$ is a stochastic process and $\tau$ is a stopping time, $X ^ { \tau }$ will denote the process X stopped at time $\tau \ ( \mathrm { i . e . , } X _ { t } ^ { \tau } = X _ { t \wedge \tau } )$ 

Definition 7.3.5. A linear subspace $\kappa \subset \mathcal H ^ { 2 }$ is called stable if 

(a) It is closed in the $L ^ { 2 }$ norm. 

(b) If $M \in \kappa$ and $\tau$ is a stopping time, then $M ^ { \tau } \in \kappa$ 

(c) If $M \in \kappa$ and $A \in { \mathcal { F } } _ { 0 }$ , then $\mathbf { 1 } _ { A } M \in { \mathcal { K } } .$ 

Theorem 7.3.6. Suppose $\kappa$ is a stable subspace of $\mathcal { H } ^ { 2 }$ . Write $\kappa ^ { \perp }$ for the set $o f$ martingales $N \in \mathcal H ^ { 2 }$ such that $E \left( M _ { \infty } N _ { \infty } \right) = 0$ for all $M \in \kappa$ . Then $\kappa ^ { \perp }$ is a stable subspace, and $M \perp N$ for all $M \in \mathcal { K } , N \in \mathcal { K } ^ { \perp }$ 

Proof. Suppose $M \in \mathcal { K } , N \in \mathcal { K } ^ { \perp }$ , and τ is a stopping time. Then $E \left( L _ { \infty } N _ { \infty } \right) = 0$ for all $L \in { \mathcal { K } }$ . Now $M ^ { \tau } \in \kappa$ , so 

$$
E \left(M _ {\infty} ^ {\tau} N _ {\infty}\right) = E \left(M _ {\tau} N _ {\infty}\right) = 0.
$$

Therefore 

$$
E \left(E \left(M _ {\tau} N _ {\infty} \mid \mathcal {F} _ {\tau}\right)\right) = E \left(M _ {\tau} E \left(N _ {\infty} \mid \mathcal {F} _ {\tau}\right)\right) = E \left(M _ {\tau} N _ {\tau}\right) = 0.
$$

Taking $\tau = 0$ , we have $\mathbf { 1 } _ { A } M \in \mathcal { K }$ for any $A \in { \mathcal { F } } _ { 0 }$ , so $E \left( \mathbf { 1 } _ { A } M _ { 0 } N _ { 0 } \right) = 0$ 

Therefore, $M _ { 0 } N _ { 0 } = 0 ~ \mathrm { a . s }$ . and M and N are orthogonal. We also have 

$$
E \left((\mathbf {1} _ {A} M _ {\tau}) N _ {\tau}\right) = E \left(M _ {\infty} (\mathbf {1} _ {A} N ^ {\tau}) _ {\infty}\right) = 0,
$$

so $\mathbf { 1 } _ { A } N ^ { \tau } \in \mathcal { K } ^ { \bot }$ for any $N \in { \cal K } ^ { \perp }$ , any stopping time $\tau ,$ and any $A \in { \mathcal { F } } _ { 0 }$ Consequently, $\kappa ^ { \perp }$ is a stable subspace. □ 

Corollary 7.3.7. Suppose $\kappa \subset \mathcal { H } ^ { 2 }$ is a stable subspace. Then every element $M \in \mathcal { H } ^ { 2 }$ has a unique decomposition 

$$
M = N + N ^ {1},
$$

where $N \in { \mathcal { K } }$ and $N ^ { 1 } \in { \cal K } ^ { \perp }$ 

Proof. Suppose $\kappa _ { \infty }$ is the closed subspace of $L ^ { 2 } ( \Omega , \mathcal { F } _ { \infty } )$ generated by the random variables $M _ { \infty }$ for $M \in \mathcal { K } . ~ \mathcal { K } _ { \infty } ^ { \perp }$ is defined analogously. Then $\kappa _ { \infty }$ and $\kappa _ { \infty } ^ { \perp }$ give an orthogonal decomposition of $L ^ { 2 } ( \Omega , \mathcal { F } _ { \infty } )$ and, for any $M \in$ $\mathcal { H } ^ { 2 } , M _ { \infty }$ has a unique decomposition, 

$$
M _ {\infty} = N _ {\infty} + N _ {\infty} ^ {1},
$$

where $N _ { \infty } \in \mathcal { K } _ { \infty }$ and $N _ { \infty } ^ { 1 } \ \in \ K _ { \infty } ^ { \perp }$ . Then define $N \ ( \mathrm { r e s p . } \ N ^ { 1 } )$ to be the right-continuous version, with left limits, of the martingale 

$$
N _ {t} = E \left(N _ {\infty} \mid \mathcal {F} _ {t}\right), \quad \text {(resp.} N _ {t} ^ {1} = E \left(N _ {\infty} ^ {1} \mid \mathcal {F} _ {t}\right)).
$$

Remark 7.3.8. From the isometry properties of the stochastic integral, it can be shown that the stable subspace generated by $M \in \mathcal { H } ^ { 2 }$ is the set of all stochastic integrals with respect to M. See [199, page 140]. 

We now prove the basic representation theorem for Brownian martingales; the proof is adapted from [109]. The stochastic process $( B _ { t } ) _ { t \geq 0 }$ is a Brownian motion on the probability space $( \Omega , { \mathcal { F } } , P )$ with respect to the filtration $\mathcal { F } _ { t } ^ { 0 } = \sigma \left\{ B _ { s } : s \leq t \right\}$ . For $t \geq 0 , \mathcal { F } _ { t }$ is the completion of $\mathcal { F } _ { t } ^ { 0 }$ , so that $( \mathcal { F } _ { t } ) _ { t \geq 0 }$ is the filtration generated by B, which satisfies the ‘usual conditions’ of right-continuity and completeness. We have seen that if $( H _ { t } ) _ { 0 \leq t \leq T }$ is a measurable adapted process on $[ 0 , T ]$ such that $\begin{array} { r } { E \left( \int _ { 0 } ^ { T } H _ { s } ^ { 2 } d s \right) < \infty , } \end{array}$ then $\begin{array} { r l r } {  { ( \int _ { 0 } ^ { t } H _ { s } d B _ { s } ) } } \end{array}$ is a square integrable martingale. The representation result tells us that all square integrable martingales on $( \mathcal { F } _ { t } ) _ { 0 \leq t \leq T }$ are of this form. 

Theorem 7.3.9. Suppose $( M _ { t } , \mathcal { F } _ { t } ) _ { 0 \leq t \leq T }$ is a square integrable martingale, where $\mathcal { F } _ { t }$ is the completion of the Brownian filtration $\mathcal { F } _ { t } ^ { 0 }$ . Then there is a measurable, adapted process $( H _ { t } ) _ { 0 \leq t \leq T }$ such that $\begin{array} { r } { E \left( \int _ { 0 } ^ { T } H _ { s } ^ { 2 } d s \right) < \infty } \end{array}$ and 

$$
M _ {t} = M _ {0} + \int_ {0} ^ {t} H _ {s} d B _ {s} a. s. f o r a l l t \in [ 0, T ].\tag{7.10}
$$

Proof. First note that, by subtracting $M _ { 0 } = E \left( M _ { t } \right)$ from each side of $( 7 . 1 0 )$ , we can assume $M _ { 0 } = 0$ . Second, $M _ { T }$ is <sub>T</sub>-measurable and square integrable, so all we have to establish is that any square integrable, ${ \mathcal { F } } _ { T ^ { - } }$ measurable, zero mean random variable $M _ { T }$ has a representation 

$$
M _ {T} = \int_ {0} ^ {T} H _ {s} d B _ {s} \mathrm{a.s.}
$$

Write $\mathcal { H } _ { T } ^ { 2 }$ for the space of square integrable $( \mathcal { F } _ { t } )$ -martingales on $[ 0 , T ]$ We can consider the stable subspace of $\mathcal { H } _ { T } ^ { 2 }$ generated by stochastic integrals with respect to $( B _ { t } ) ;$ this is closed in the norm of $L ^ { 2 } ( \Omega , \mathcal { F } _ { T } )$ . Consequently, the martingale $( M _ { t } )$ has a projection on this stable subspace, which we denote by $( Y _ { t } ) _ { 0 \leq t \leq T }$ . From Remark $7 . 3 . 8 , ( Y _ { t } )$ is a stochastic integral with respect to $\left( B _ { t } \right)$ , so there is a measurable adapted integrand $( H _ { t } ) _ { 0 \leq t \leq T }$ such that 

$$
Y _ {t} = \int_ {0} ^ {t} H _ {s} d B _ {s} \text { a.s. for } t \in [ 0, T ].
$$

By construction, $M _ { t } - Y _ { t }$ is orthogonal to the stable subspace $\mathcal { H } _ { T } ^ { 2 } ( B )$ of $\mathcal { H } _ { T } ^ { 2 }$ generated by stochastic integrals with respect to B. We can therefore suppose that the martingale $( M _ { t } )$ is orthogonal to $\mathcal { H } _ { T } ^ { 2 } ( B )$ and show that this implies $M _ { T } = 0$ a.s. Write 

$$
\sigma_ {n} = \inf \left\{t: | M _ {t} | \geq n \right\}, \quad M _ {t} ^ {N} = \frac {1}{2 n} M _ {t \wedge \sigma_ {n}}.
$$

Then $\begin{array} { r } { | M _ { t } ^ { N } | \leq \frac { 1 } { 2 } } \end{array}$ and $( M _ { t } )$ is orthogonal to both the martingales $\left( B _ { t } \right)$ and $\begin{array} { r } { ( B _ { t } ^ { 2 } - t ) = \left( 2 \int _ { 0 } ^ { t } B _ { s } d B _ { s } \right) } \end{array}$ 

A new probability measure Q can be defined on $\mathcal { F } _ { T }$ by putting $\begin{array} { r } { { \frac { d Q } { d P } } = } \end{array}$ $\Lambda = 1 + M _ { t } ^ { \bar { N } }$ . Now $( B _ { t } )$ and $\left( B _ { t } ^ { 2 } - t \right)$ are continuous martingales on $( \Omega , { \bar { \mathcal { F } } } , Q )$ Consequently, $\left( B _ { t } \right)$ is a Brownian motion under $Q$ as well as $P ,$ so $P$ and $Q$ coincide on $\mathcal { F } _ { T }$ . This implies that $M _ { t } ^ { N } = 0$ a.s. Letting $n \to \infty$ , we see that $M _ { T } = 0 ~ \mathrm { a . s }$ . and the theorem is proved. □ 

We now extend Theorem 7.3.9 to the situation where the filtration is generated by the weak solution of a stochastic diferential equation. Suppose we have a probability space $( \Omega , { \mathcal { F } } , P )$ and an $\mathbb { R } ^ { n } .$ -valued stochastic process $( x _ { t } ) _ { 0 \leq t \leq T }$ . Denote by $( \mathcal { F } _ { t } )$ the filtration generated by $\left( { x _ { t } } \right)$ , and let $( B _ { t } )$ be an $( \mathcal { F } _ { t } )$ -Brownian motion, such that 

$$
x _ {t} = x _ {0} + \int_ {0} ^ {t} f (s, x) d s + \int_ {0} ^ {t} \sigma (s, x) d B _ {s} \text { a   .   s   . } (P).
$$

Here, f and $\sigma$ satisfy measurability and growth conditions, as in Theorem 6.5.3. 

In the continuous-time setting, the predictable σ-field on $\Omega \times [ 0 , T ]$ is the σ-field generated by the left-continuous processes. A process is called predictable if it is measurable with respect to this $\sigma { - } \mathrm { f i e l d }$ . (Compare this with the discrete-time definition given in Section 2.2.) 

Theorem 7.3.10. Suppose $( N _ { t } ) _ { 0 \leq t \leq T }$ , with $N _ { 0 } = 0$ , is a square integrable P-martingale with respect to the filtration $( \mathcal { F } _ { t } ) _ { 0 \leq t \leq T }$ . Then there is an -predictable process $( \gamma _ { t } )$ such that 

$$
\int_ {0} ^ {t} E \left(\left| \gamma_ {s} \right| ^ {2}\right) d s <   \infty , \qquad N _ {t} = \int_ {0} ^ {t} \gamma_ {s} d B _ {s} a. s.
$$

Proof. For $n \in \mathbb { Z } ^ { + }$ , define 

$$
T _ {n} = \min \left\{T, \inf \left\{t: \int_ {0} ^ {t} \left| \sigma_ {s} ^ {- 1} f _ {s} \right| ^ {2} d s \geq n \right\} \right\}.
$$

Then $T _ { n }$ is an $( \mathcal { F } _ { t } )$ -stopping time and lim $T _ { n } = T$ . Write 

$$
\Lambda_ {t} ^ {*} = \exp \left\{- \int_ {0} ^ {t} \sigma_ {s} ^ {- 1} f _ {s} d B _ {s} - \frac {1}{2} \int_ {0} ^ {t} \left| \sigma_ {s} ^ {- 1} f _ {s} \right| ^ {2} d s \right\},\tag{7.11}
$$

and define a new measure $P _ { n } ^ { * }$ by setting 

$$
\left. \frac {d P ^ {*}}{d P} \right| _ {\mathcal {F} _ {t}} = \Lambda_ {t \wedge T _ {n}} ^ {*}.
$$

For each $n , P _ { n } ^ { * }$ is a probability measure. From Girsanov’s theorem (Theorem 7.2.3), the process 

$$
z _ {t} ^ {n} = B _ {t} + \int_ {0} ^ {t \wedge T _ {n}} \sigma_ {s} ^ {- 1} f _ {s} d s
$$

is a Brownian motion under $P _ { n } ^ { * }$ . Write 

$$
\mathcal {Z} _ {t} ^ {n} = \sigma \left\{z _ {s} ^ {n}: 0 \leq s \leq t \right\}.
$$

From Theorem 7.3.9, if $\left( \widetilde { N } _ { t } \right)$ is a square integrable, zero mean martingale under $P _ { n } ^ { * }$ -with respect to the filtration $\left( \mathcal { Z } _ { t } ^ { n } \right)$ , then there is a process $\left( \phi _ { t } ^ { n } \right)$ ， adapted to $\left( \mathcal { Z } _ { t } ^ { n } \right)$ , such that $\begin{array} { r } { \widetilde { N } _ { t } = \int _ { 0 } ^ { t } \phi _ { s } ^ { n } \dot { d z } _ { s } ^ { n } } \end{array}$ a.s. Now 

$$
z _ {t} ^ {n} = \int_ {0} ^ {t} \sigma_ {s} ^ {- 1} (\sigma_ {s} d B _ {s} + f _ {s} d s) = \int_ {0} ^ {t} \sigma_ {s} ^ {- 1} d x _ {s} \mathrm{for} t <   T _ {n},
$$

so $\left( \mathcal { Z } _ { t } ^ { n } \right) = \left( \mathcal { F } _ { t \wedge T _ { n } } \right)$ . We have shown that if $\left( \widetilde { N } _ { t } \right)$ is a square integrable, zero-mean $P _ { n } ^ { * }$ --martingale with respect to the filtration $\left( \mathcal { Z } _ { t } ^ { n } \right)$ , then 

$$
\widetilde {N} _ {t \wedge T _ {n}} = \int_ {0} ^ {t \wedge T _ {n}} \phi_ {s} ^ {n} \sigma_ {s} ^ {- 1} d x _ {s} \mathrm{a.s.}\tag{7.12}
$$

Now, from Lemma 7.3.3, if $\left( B _ { t } \right)$ is a square integrable P-martingale with respect to the filtration $( \mathcal { F } _ { t } )$ , then $\left( \widetilde { N } _ { t } \right)$ is a square integrable martingale with respect to the filtration $( \mathcal { F } _ { t \wedge T _ { n } } )$ -, where 

$$
\widetilde {N} _ {t} = (\Lambda_ {t \wedge T _ {n}} ^ {*}) ^ {- 1} N _ {t \wedge T _ {n}}.
$$

In this situation, we certainly have $\widetilde { N } _ { t } = \widetilde { N } _ { t \wedge T _ { n } }$ , so, from (7.10), 

$$
\widetilde {N} _ {t} = \int_ {0} ^ {t \wedge T _ {n}} \phi_ {s} ^ {n} \sigma_ {s} ^ {- 1} d x _ {s} = \int_ {0} ^ {t \wedge T _ {n}} \phi_ {s} ^ {n} d B _ {s} + \int_ {0} ^ {t \wedge T _ {n}} \phi_ {s} ^ {n} \sigma_ {s} ^ {- 1} f _ {s} d s.
$$

From (7.11), we have 

$$
\Lambda_ {t \wedge T _ {n}} ^ {*} = 1 - \int_ {0} ^ {t \wedge T _ {n}} \Lambda_ {s} ^ {*} \sigma_ {s} ^ {- 1} f _ {s} d B _ {s}.
$$

Therefore, using the Itˆo rule, 

$$
\begin{array}{l} N _ {t \wedge T _ {n}} = \widetilde {N} _ {t} \Lambda_ {t \wedge T _ {n}} ^ {*} \\ = \int_ {0} ^ {t \wedge T _ {n}} \widetilde {N} _ {s} d \Lambda_ {s} ^ {*} + \int_ {0} ^ {t \wedge T _ {n}} \Lambda_ {s} ^ {*} d \widetilde {N} _ {s} + \left\langle \widetilde {N}, \Lambda^ {*} \right\rangle_ {t \wedge T _ {n}} \\ = - \int_ {0} ^ {t \wedge T _ {n}} \widetilde {N} _ {s} \Lambda_ {s} ^ {*} \sigma_ {s} ^ {- 1} f _ {s} d B _ {s} + \int_ {0} ^ {t \wedge T _ {n}} \Lambda_ {s} ^ {*} \phi_ {s} ^ {n} \sigma_ {s} ^ {- 1} d x _ {s} - \int_ {0} ^ {t} \Lambda_ {s} ^ {*} \phi_ {s} ^ {n} \sigma_ {s} ^ {- 1} d s \\ = \int_ {0} ^ {t \wedge T _ {n}} \gamma_ {s} ^ {n} d B _ {s}, \end{array}
$$

where $\gamma _ { s } ^ { n } = \Lambda _ { s } ( \phi _ { s } ^ { n } - \widetilde { N } _ { s } \sigma _ { s } ^ { - 1 } f _ { s } )$ . Furthermore, 

$$
E \left(N _ {t \wedge T _ {n}} ^ {2}\right) = \int_ {0} ^ {t} E \left(\left(\mathbf {1} _ {\{0 \leq s \leq T _ {n} \}} \gamma_ {s} ^ {n}\right) ^ {2}\right) d s \leq E \left(N _ {T} ^ {2}\right) <   \infty .
$$

The representation is unique, so that 

$$
\gamma_ {s} ^ {n} = \gamma_ {s} ^ {m} \text {   for   } s \leq T _ {n} \text {   for   all   } m \geq n.
$$

Define $\gamma _ { s }$ to be the process such that 

$$
\gamma_ {s} = \gamma_ {s} ^ {n} \mathrm{for} 0 \leq s \leq T _ {n}.
$$

Then 

$$
N _ {t \wedge T _ {n}} = \int_ {0} ^ {t \wedge T _ {n}} \gamma_ {s} d B _ {s}, \quad N _ {t} = \int_ {0} ^ {t} \gamma_ {s} d B _ {s} \text { for } t <   T _ {n}.
$$

However, lim $T _ { n } ~ = ~ T$ a.s. so that $\begin{array} { r } { \int _ { 0 } ^ { 1 } E \left( \left| \gamma _ { s } \right| ^ { 2 } \right) d s < \infty } \end{array}$ , and the result follows. □ 

Corollary 7.3.11. Suppose B is a P-Brownian motion and $( \mathcal { F } _ { t } ) _ { t \geq 0 }$ is the completed filtration generated by B. Suppose further that $( \theta _ { t } ) _ { t \geq 0 }$ is a predictable process such that if Λ is given by 

$$
d \Lambda_ {t} = \Lambda_ {t} \theta_ {t} d B _ {t},
$$

$$
\Lambda_ {0} = 1,
$$

then Λ is a (positive) martingale under $( P , ( { \mathcal { F } } _ { t } ) _ { t \geq 0 } )$ . Define a probability measure Q by 

$$
\left. \frac {d Q}{d P} \right| _ {\mathcal {F} _ {t}} = \Lambda_ {t},
$$

so that, by Girsanov’s theorem, W is a Brownian motion under Q, where $d W _ { t } = d B _ { t } - \theta _ { t } d t$ . Then, if M is an $( \mathcal { F } _ { t } , Q )$ -martingale, there is a predictable process ψ such that 

$$
M _ {t} = M _ {0} + \int_ {0} ^ {t} \psi_ {s} d W _ {s}.
$$

Corollary 7.3.12. By considering stopping times and pasting, the representation results apply to locally square integrable martingales. 

## Description of the Integrand in a Markov Model

In the Markov case, when the coeficients are suficiently diferentiable, the form of the integrand in the martingale representation can be made more explicit. 

Suppose again that $B = \left( B ^ { 1 } , B ^ { 2 } , \ldots , B ^ { m } \right)$ is an m-dimensional Brownian motion defined for $t \geq 0 \mathrm { ~ o n ~ } ( \Omega , \mathcal { F } , P )$ . Consider the stochastic diferential equation 

$$
d x _ {t} = f (t, x _ {t}) d t + \sigma (t, x _ {t}) d B _ {t} \text { for } t \geq 0,\tag{7.13}
$$

where $f : [ 0 , \infty ) \times \mathbb { R } ^ { n } \to \mathbb { R } ^ { n }$ and $\sigma : [ 0 , \infty ) \times \mathbb { R } ^ { n } \to \mathbb { R } ^ { n } \times \mathbb { R } ^ { n }$ are measurable functions that are three times diferentiable in x, and, together with their derivatives, have linear growth in x. 

Write $\xi _ { s , t } ( x )$ for the solution of (7.13) for $t \geq s$ under the initial condi tion 

$$
\xi_ {s, s} (x) = x \in \mathbb {R} ^ {n}.
$$

From results of Bismut [25] or Kunita [204], we know there is a set $N \subset \Omega$ of measure zero such that, for $\omega \not \in N$ , there is a version of $\xi _ { s , t } ( x )$ that is twice diferentiable in x and continuous in t and s. Write 

$$
D _ {s, t} (k) = \frac {\partial \xi_ {s , t} (x)}{\partial x}
$$

for the Jacobian of the map $x  \xi _ { s , t } ( x )$ . Then D is the solution of the linearised equation 

$$
d D _ {s, t} (x) = f _ {x} (t, x _ {t}) D _ {s, t} d t + \sigma_ {x} (t, x _ {t}) D _ {s, t} d B _ {t}
$$

with initial condition $D _ { s , s } ( x ) = I$ , the $n \times n$ identity matrix. It is known that the inverse $D _ { s , t } ^ { - 1 } ( x )$ exists; see [25], [204]. 

Suppose $g : [ 0 , \infty ) \times \mathbb { R } ^ { n }  \mathbb { R } ^ { m }$ satisfies conditions similar to those of f and define the exponential $M _ { s , t } ( x )$ by 

$$
M _ {s, t} (x) = 1 + \int_ {0} ^ {t} M _ {s, r} (x) g (r, \xi_ {s, r} (x)) d B _ {r}.
$$

Write $( \mathcal { F } _ { t } )$ for the right-continuous complete family of σ-fields generated by B. As $g$ satisfies a linear growth condition, a new probability measure $\overline { { P } }$ can be defined by putting 

$$
\left. \frac {d \overline {{P}}}{d P} \right| _ {\mathcal {F} _ {t}} = M _ {0, t} (x _ {0}).
$$

From Girsanov’s theorem, W is an $( \mathcal { F } _ { t } )$ -Brownian motion under $\overline { { P } }$ if 

$$
d W _ {t} = d B _ {t} - g (t, \xi_ {0, t} (x _ {0})) d t.\tag{7.14}
$$

Suppose $c : \mathbb { R } ^ { n }  \mathbb { R }$ is $\mathrm { a } ~ C ^ { 2 }$ function, that, together with its derivatives has linear growth, and consider the ${ \overline { { P } } } .$ -martingale 

$$
N _ {t} = E _ {\overline {{P}}} \left(c (\xi_ {0, T} (x _ {0}))   | \mathcal {F} _ {t}\right) \text { for } 0 \leq t \leq T.
$$

Then, from Theorem 7.3.10, $( N _ { t } )$ has a representation 

$$
N _ {t} = N _ {0} + \int_ {0} ^ {t} \gamma_ {s} d W _ {s} \mathrm{for} 0 \leq t \leq T,\tag{7.15}
$$

where $\begin{array} { r } { \int _ { 0 } ^ { T } E \left( \left| \gamma _ { s } \right| ^ { 2 } \right) } \end{array}$ ds $< \infty$ . We can now describe $\gamma$ . 

Theorem 7.3.13. 

$$
\begin{array}{l} \gamma_ {t} = E \Big \{\int_ {t} ^ {T} d W _ {r} ^ {*} g _ {\xi} \left(r, \xi_ {0, r} (x _ {0})\right) D _ {0, r} (x _ {0}) c \left(\xi_ {0, T} (x _ {0})\right) \\ \qquad + c _ {\xi} \left(\xi_ {0, T} (x _ {0})\right) D _ {0, T} (x _ {0})   | \mathcal {F} _ {t} \Big \} D _ {0, t} ^ {- 1} (x _ {0}) \sigma \left(t, \xi_ {0, t} (x _ {0})\right). \end{array}
$$

Here the asterisk denotes the transpose. 

Proof. For $0 \leq t \leq T$ , write $x = \xi _ { 0 , t } ( x _ { 0 } )$ . From the semigroup property of the solution of stochastic diferential equations, Lemma 6.6.4, it follows that 

$$
\xi_ {0, T} (x _ {0}) = \xi_ {t, T} (\xi_ {0, t} (x _ {0})) = \xi_ {t, T} (x).\tag{7.16}
$$

Diferentiating (7.16), we have 

$$
D _ {0, T} (x _ {0}) = D _ {t, T} (x) D _ {0, t} (x _ {0}).
$$

Furthermore, the exponential M satisfies 

$$
M _ {0, T} (x _ {0}) = M _ {0, t} (x _ {0}) M _ {t, T} (x).
$$

For $y \in \mathbb { R } ^ { n }$ , define 

$$
V (t, y) = E \left(M _ {t, T} (y) c (\xi_ {t, T} (y))\right)
$$

and consider the martingale 

$$
\begin{array}{l} N _ {t} = E _ {\overline {{P}}} \left(c (\xi_ {0, t} (x _ {0}))   | \mathcal {F} _ {t}\right) \\ \quad = \frac {E \left(M _ {0 , T} (x _ {0}) c (\xi_ {0 , T} (x _ {0}))   | \mathcal {F} _ {t}\right)}{E \left(M _ {0 , T} (x _ {0})   | \mathcal {F} _ {t}\right)} \\ \quad = E \left(M _ {t, T} (x) c (\xi_ {t, T} (x))   | \mathcal {F} _ {t}\right) \\ \quad = E \left(M _ {t, T} (x) c (\xi_ {t, T} (x))\right) \\ \quad = V (t, x), \end{array}
$$

the last two equalities being due to the Markov property and Theorem 6.6.7, respectively. The diferentiability of $\xi _ { t , T } ( x )$ in x and t was established by Kunita [204]. 

Under ${ \overline { { P } } } .$ , we have 

$$
\begin{array}{l} \xi_ {0, t} (x _ {0}) \\ = x _ {0} + \int_ {0} ^ {t} (f (x, \xi_ {0, s} (x _ {0})) + \sigma g (s, \xi_ {0, s} (x _ {0}))) d s + \int_ {0} ^ {t} \sigma (s, \xi_ {0, s} (x _ {0})) d W _ {s}. \end{array}
$$

Expand $V ( t , x ) = V ( t , \xi _ { 0 , t } ( x _ { 0 } ) )$ by the Itˆo rule to get 

$$
V (t, \xi_ {0, t} (x _ {0})) = N _ {t} = V (0, x _ {0}) + \int_ {0} ^ {t} \left(\frac {\partial V}{\partial t} + L V\right) (s, \xi_ {0, s} (x _ {0})) d s
$$

$$
+ \int_ {0} ^ {t} \frac {\partial V}{\partial x} (s, \xi_ {0, s} (x _ {0})) \sigma (s, \xi_ {0, s} (x _ {0})) d W _ {s},\tag{7.17}
$$

where 

$$
L = \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \left(f ^ {i} + \sum_ {j = 1} ^ {m} \sigma_ {i j} g ^ {j}\right) \frac {\partial}{\partial x _ {i}} + \frac {1}{2} \sum_ {i, j = 1} ^ {n} a _ {i j} \frac {\partial^ {2}}{\partial x _ {i} \partial x _ {j}},
$$

and $( a _ { i j } )$ is the matrix $\sigma \sigma ^ { * }$ 

A special semimartingale is a semimartingale that is the sum of a (local) martingale and a predictable process of (locally) integrable variation. The decomposition is unique when it exists (see [109, Theorem 12.38]). 

Now N is a special semimartingale, so the decompositions given in (7.15) and (7.17) must be the same. As there is no bounded variation term in (7.15), we must have 

$$
\frac {\partial V}{\partial t} + L V = 0 \mathrm{with} V (T, x) = c (x).
$$

In addition, 

$$
\gamma_ {s} = \frac {\partial V}{\partial x} \left(x, \xi_ {0, s} (x _ {0})\right) \sigma \left(s, \xi_ {0, s} (x _ {0})\right).
$$

However, $\xi _ { t , T } ( x ) = \xi _ { 0 , T } ( x _ { 0 } )$ so, from the diferentiability and linear growth of g, we have 

$$
\frac {\partial V (t , x)}{\partial x} = E \left(\frac {\partial M _ {t , T} (x)}{\partial x} c (\xi_ {0, T} (X _ {0})) + M _ {t, T} (x) \frac {\partial c}{\partial x} (\xi_ {t, T} (x))\right).
$$

Now, using the existence of solutions of stochastic diferential equations that are diferentiable in their initial conditions, we have, 

$$
\begin{array}{c} \frac {\partial M _ {t , T} (x)}{\partial x} = \int_ {t} ^ {T} g (r, \xi_ {t, r} (x)) \frac {\partial M _ {t , r} (x)}{\partial x} d B _ {r} \\ + \int_ {t} ^ {T} d B _ {r} g _ {\xi} (r, \xi_ {t, r} (x)) \frac {\partial \xi_ {t , r} (x)}{\partial x} M _ {t, r} (x). \end{array}\tag{7.18}
$$

Equation (7.18) can be solved by variation of constants to obtain 

$$
\frac {\partial M _ {t , T} (x)}{\partial x} = M _ {t, T} (x) \int_ {t} ^ {T} d W _ {r} ^ {*} g (r, \xi_ {t, r} (x)) D _ {t, r} (x).
$$

Therefore, with $x = \xi _ { 0 , t } ( x _ { 0 } )$ 

$$
\begin{array}{l} \frac {\partial V (t , x)}{\partial x} = E \left(M _ {t, T} (x) \left[ \int_ {t} ^ {T} d W _ {r} ^ {*} g _ {\xi} \left(r, \xi_ {t, r} (x)\right) D _ {t, r} (x) c \left(\xi_ {0, T} (x _ {0})\right) \right. \right. \\ \quad \left. + c _ {\xi} (\xi_ {t, T} (x)) D _ {t, T} (x) ]\right) \\ = E _ {\overline {{P}}} \left(\int_ {t} ^ {T} d W _ {r} ^ {*} g _ {\xi} \left(r, \xi_ {0, r} (x _ {0})\right) D _ {0, r} (x _ {0}) c \left(\xi_ {0, T} (x _ {0})\right) \right. \\ \quad \left. + c _ {\xi} \left(\xi_ {0, T} (x _ {0})\right) D _ {0, T} (x _ {0})   | \mathcal {F} _ {t}\right) D _ {0, t} ^ {- 1} (x _ {0}) \end{array}
$$

and the result follows. 

## 7.4 Self-Financing Strategies

## One-factor Model

In a pricing model with one risky stock, a hedging strategy is a measurable process $\left( \phi _ { t } \right) = \left( H _ { t } ^ { 0 } , H _ { t } ^ { 1 } \right)$ , with values in $\mathbb { R } ^ { 2 }$ that is adapted to the filtration $( \mathcal { F } _ { t } ) _ { t \geq 0 }$ , where $\mathcal { F } _ { t } = \sigma \left\{ B _ { u } : u \leq t \right\} = \sigma \left\{ S _ { u } ^ { 1 } : u \leq t \right\}$ . The quantity $H _ { t } ^ { 0 }$ (resp. $H _ { t } ^ { 1 } )$  denotes the amount of the bond $S _ { t } ^ { 0 }$ (resp. the risky asset $S _ { t } ^ { 1 } )$ that is held at time t. Consequently, the value, or wealth, of the portfolio at time t is 

$$
V _ {t} (\phi) = H _ {t} ^ {0} S _ {t} ^ {0} + H _ {t} ^ {1} S _ {t} ^ {1}.\tag{7.19}
$$

In discrete time, we have established (see (2.2)) that a self-financing strategy should satisfy the identity 

$$
\begin{array}{c} V _ {n + 1} (\phi) - V _ {n} (\phi) = \phi_ {n + 1} (S _ {n + 1} - S _ {n}) \\ = H _ {n + 1} ^ {0} (S _ {n + 1} ^ {0} - S _ {n} ^ {0}) + H _ {n + 1} ^ {1} (S _ {n + 1} ^ {1} - S _ {n} ^ {1}). \end{array}
$$

The natural continuous-time analogue of this condition therefore appears to be 

$$
d V _ {t} (\phi) = H _ {t} ^ {0} d S _ {t} ^ {0} + H _ {t} ^ {1} d S _ {t} ^ {1}.\tag{7.20}
$$

Indeed, if $H ^ { 0 }$ and $H ^ { 1 }$ are of bounded variation, then 

$$
d V _ {t} (\phi) = H _ {t} ^ {0} d S _ {t} ^ {0} + H _ {t} ^ {1} d S _ {t} ^ {1} + S _ {t} ^ {0} d H _ {t} ^ {0} + S _ {t} ^ {1} d H _ {t} ^ {1}\tag{7.21}
$$

and equation (7.20) is equivalent to saying that 

$$
S _ {t} ^ {0} d H _ {t} ^ {0} + S _ {t} ^ {1} d H _ {t} ^ {1} = 0,\tag{7.22}
$$

which is the analogue of (2.5). The intuitive meaning of (7.22) is that changes in the holdings of the bond, $S _ { t } ^ { 0 } d H _ { t } ^ { 0 }$ , can only take place due to corresponding changes in holding of the stock $S _ { t } ^ { 1 } d H _ { t } ^ { 1 }$ ; that is, there is no net inflow or outflow of capital. 

We consider European claims with an expiration time T. Consequently, for (7.20) to make sense, we require 

$$
\int_ {0} ^ {T} \left| H _ {t} ^ {0} \right| d t <   \infty \text {a.s.}, \quad \int_ {0} ^ {T} \left(H _ {t} ^ {1} S _ {t} ^ {1}\right) ^ {2} d t <   \infty \text {a.s.}\tag{7.23}
$$

From the dynamics for $S ^ { 1 }$ , namely 

$$
d S _ {t} ^ {1} = S _ {t} ^ {1} (\mu d t + \sigma d B _ {t}),
$$

we have 

$$
\left. \begin{array}{l} \int_ {0} ^ {T} H _ {t} ^ {0} d S _ {t} ^ {0} = \int_ {0} ^ {T} H _ {t} ^ {0} r e ^ {r t} d t, \\ \int_ {0} ^ {T} H _ {t} ^ {1} d S _ {t} ^ {1} = \int_ {0} ^ {T} (H _ {t} ^ {1} S _ {t} ^ {1} \mu) d t + \int_ {0} ^ {T} (H _ {t} ^ {1} S _ {t} ^ {1} \sigma) d B _ {t}. \end{array} \right\}\tag{7.24}
$$

We can therefore give the following definition. 

Definition 7.4.1. A self-financing strategy $\phi = ( \phi _ { t } ) _ { 0 \leq t \leq T }$ is given by two measurable adapted processes $( H _ { t } ^ { 0 } ) , ( H _ { t } ^ { 1 } )$ satisfying (7.20) and (7.23). The corresponding wealth process is given for all $t \in [ 0 , T ]$ by 

$$
V _ {t} (\phi) = H _ {t} ^ {0} S _ {t} ^ {0} + H _ {t} ^ {1} S _ {t} ^ {1} = H _ {0} ^ {0} S _ {0} ^ {0} + H _ {0} ^ {1} S _ {0} ^ {1} + \int_ {0} ^ {t} H _ {u} ^ {0} d S _ {u} ^ {0} + \int_ {0} ^ {t} H _ {u} ^ {1} d S _ {u} ^ {1} \text {a.s.}
$$

Notation 7.4.2. Write $\widetilde { S } _ { t } ^ { 1 } = e ^ { - r t } S _ { t } ^ { 1 }$ for the discounted price of the risky asset and $\widetilde { V } _ { t } ( \phi ) = e ^ { - r t } V _ { t } ( \phi )$ for the discounted wealth process. 

We can then establish the following result, whose discrete-time analogue was discussed in Section 2.2. 

Theorem 7.4.3. Suppose $\phi = \left( \phi _ { t } \right) = \left( H _ { t } ^ { 0 } , H _ { t } ^ { 1 } \right) _ { 0 < t < T }$ is a pair of measurable adapted processes satisfying (7.23). Then φ is a self-financing strategy if and only if 

$$
\widetilde {V} _ {t} (\phi) = V _ {0} (\phi) + \int_ {0} ^ {t} H _ {u} ^ {1} d \widetilde {S} _ {u} ^ {1} a. s. f o r a l l t \in [ 0, T ].\tag{7.25}
$$

Proof. Suppose $\phi = \left( H _ { t } ^ { 0 } , H _ { t } ^ { 1 } \right)$ is self-financing, so (7.20) holds. Then 

$$
\begin{array}{r l} & {d \widetilde {V} _ {t} (\phi) = d \left(e ^ {- r t} V _ {t} (\phi)\right)} \\ & {\quad = - r \widetilde {V} _ {t} (\phi) d t + e ^ {- r t} d V _ {t} (\phi)} \\ & {\quad = - r e ^ {- r t} (H _ {t} ^ {0} e ^ {r t} + H _ {t} ^ {1} S _ {t} ^ {1}) d t + e ^ {- r t} H _ {t} ^ {0} d (e ^ {r t}) + e ^ {- r t} H _ {t} ^ {1} d S _ {t} ^ {1}} \\ & {\quad = H _ {t} ^ {1} (- r e ^ {- r t} S _ {t} ^ {1} d t + e ^ {- r t} d S _ {t} ^ {1})} \\ & {\quad = H _ {t} ^ {1} d \widetilde {S} _ {t},} \end{array}
$$

and (7.25) follows. 

The converse follows by considering $V _ { t } ( \phi ) \ : = \ : e ^ { r t } \widetilde { V } _ { t } ( \phi )$ , reversing the steps above and using (7.25). □ 

Remark 7.4.4. Although in general the continuous-time stochastic integral requires predictable integrands (see [109]), we have only required the trading strategies $\phi = \left( H _ { t } ^ { 0 } , H _ { t } ^ { 1 } \right)$ to be measurable and adapted. This is possible -here because the filtration $( \mathcal F _ { t } ) _ { t \geq 0 }$ is generated by the continuous Brownian motion $\left( B _ { t } \right)$ (or, equivalently, by the continuous process $\left( S _ { t } ^ { 1 } \right) )$ . 

Let us write SF for the set of self-financing strategies $\phi = ( \phi _ { t } ) _ { 0 \leq t \leq T }$ so that $\phi _ { t } = ( H _ { t } ^ { 0 } , H _ { t } ^ { 1 } )$ , where $H ^ { 0 }$ and $H ^ { 1 }$ satisfy (7.23). If there are no contributions or withdrawals, the corresponding wealth process is given in (7.19) as 

$$
V _ {t} (\phi) = H _ {t} ^ {0} S _ {t} ^ {0} + H _ {t} ^ {1} S _ {t} ^ {1}.
$$

Suppose more generally (as we did for discrete-time models in Section 5.6) that the model allows contributions to the wealth process (say, from dividends) or withdrawals (consumption). Let these be modelled by the adapted right-continuous, increasing processes $D _ { t }$ (for contributions) and $C _ { t }$ (for accumulated consumption). Then 

$$
\begin{array}{c} V _ {t} (\phi) = H _ {t} ^ {0} S _ {t} ^ {0} + H _ {t} ^ {1} S _ {t} ^ {1} + D _ {t} - C _ {t} \\ = V _ {0} (\phi) + \int_ {0} ^ {t} H _ {u} ^ {0} d S _ {u} ^ {0} + \int_ {0} ^ {t} H _ {u} ^ {1} d S _ {u} ^ {1} + D _ {t} - C _ {t}. \end{array}
$$

The self-financing condition (7.20) now becomes 

$$
S _ {t} ^ {0} d H _ {t} ^ {0} + S _ {t} ^ {1} d H _ {t} ^ {1} = d D _ {t} - d C _ {t}.
$$

## 7.5 An Equivalent Martingale Measure

Consider the situation of Section 7.1 where the bond is described by a price process $( S _ { t } ^ { 0 } )$ satisfying 

$$
d S _ {t} ^ {0} = r S _ {t} ^ {0} d t\tag{7.26}
$$

and the risky asset has a price process $\left( S _ { t } ^ { 1 } \right)$ satisfying 

$$
d S _ {t} ^ {1} = S _ {t} ^ {1} (\mu d t + \sigma d B _ {t}).\tag{7.27}
$$

The discounted price of the risky asset is 

$$
\widetilde {S} _ {t} = e ^ {- r t} S _ {t} ^ {1}
$$

with dynamics 

$$
d \widetilde {S} _ {t} = - r e ^ {- r t} S _ {t} ^ {1} + e ^ {- r t} d S _ {t} ^ {1} = \widetilde {S} _ {t} ((\mu - r) d t + \sigma d B _ {t}).
$$

If we apply Girsanov’s theorem Theorem 7.2.3 with $\textstyle \theta _ { t } = { \frac { \mu - r } { \sigma } }$ , we see there is a probability measure $P ^ { \mu }$ defined on $\mathcal { F } _ { T }$ by putting 

$$
\frac {d P ^ {\mu}}{d P} = \Lambda_ {T} = \exp \left\{- \int_ {0} ^ {t} \theta_ {s} d B _ {s} - \frac {1}{2} \int_ {0} ^ {t} \theta_ {s} ^ {2} d s \right\}
$$

such that if 

$$
W _ {t} ^ {\mu} = \left(\frac {\mu - r}{\sigma}\right) t + B _ {t},\tag{7.28}
$$

then $( W _ { t } ^ { \mu } ) _ { 0 \leq t \leq T }$ is a standard Brownian motion under $P ^ { \mu }$ . Under $P ^ { \mu }$ , we then have 

$$
d \widetilde {S} _ {t} = \widetilde {S} _ {t} \sigma d W _ {t} ^ {\mu}, \quad \widetilde {S} _ {t} = S _ {0} \exp \left\{\sigma W _ {t} ^ {\mu} - \frac {\sigma^ {2}}{2} t \right\}.
$$

Definition 7.5.1. A strategy $\phi = \left( H _ { t } ^ { 0 } , H _ { t } ^ { 1 } \right) _ { 0 \leq t \leq T }$ is admissible if it is - self-financing and the discounted value process 

$$
\widetilde {V} _ {t} (\phi) = H _ {t} ^ {0} + H _ {t} ^ {1} \widetilde {S} _ {t}
$$

is non-negative and square integrable under P. 

Definition 7.5.2. A European contingent claim is a positive random variable $h ,$ , measurable with respect to $\mathcal { F } _ { T }$ 

If $h = f ( S _ { T } )$ and $f ( S _ { T } ) = ( S _ { T } - K ) ^ { + }$ (resp. $f ( S _ { T } ) = ( K - S _ { T } ) ^ { + } )$ , then the option is a European call option (resp. European put option). 

Recall that a claim is attainable (sometimes also called replicable) if its value at time $T$ (the exercise time) is equal to the value $V _ { T } ( \phi ) = H _ { T } ^ { 0 } S _ { t } ^ { 0 } +$ $H _ { T } ^ { 1 } S _ { T } ^ { 1 }$ of an admissible strategy $\phi .$ 

Suppose that $\phi \in S F$ , and $\mathbf { \Psi } ( \phi _ { t } ) = ( H _ { t } ^ { 0 } , H _ { t } ^ { 1 } )$ . The corresponding wealth process is 

$$
V _ {t} (\phi) = H _ {t} ^ {0} S _ {t} ^ {0} + H _ {t} ^ {1} S _ {t} ^ {1} = V _ {0} (\phi) + \int_ {0} ^ {t} H _ {u} ^ {0} d S _ {u} ^ {0} + \int_ {0} ^ {t} H _ {u} ^ {1} d S _ {u} ^ {1}.
$$

From (7.26) and (7.27), it follows that 

$$
\begin{array}{c} V _ {t} (\phi) = V _ {0} (\phi) + \int_ {0} ^ {t} r H _ {u} ^ {0} S _ {u} ^ {0} d u + \int_ {0} ^ {t} H _ {u} ^ {1} S _ {u} ^ {1} (\mu d u + \sigma d B _ {u}) \\ = V _ {0} (\phi) + \int_ {0} ^ {t} r V _ {u} (\phi_ {u}) d u + \int_ {0} ^ {t} \sigma H _ {u} ^ {1} S _ {u} ^ {1} d W _ {u} ^ {\mu}, \end{array}
$$

where $W ^ { \mu }$ is defined in (7.28). Consider the discounted wealth 

$$
\widetilde {V} _ {t} (\phi) = \left(S _ {t} ^ {0}\right) ^ {- 1} V _ {t} (\phi_ {t}).
$$

From the diferentiation rule, we have 

$$
\widetilde {V} _ {t} (\phi) = V _ {0} \left(\phi_ {0}\right) + \int_ {0} ^ {t} \sigma H _ {u} ^ {1} \left(S _ {u} ^ {0}\right) ^ {- 1} \left(S _ {u} ^ {1}\right) d W _ {u} ^ {\mu}.
$$

We have seen above that, under the measure $P ^ { \mu }$ , the process $W ^ { \mu }$ is a standard Brownian motion. Consequently, under $P ^ { \mu }$ , we see that 

$$
M _ {t} = \int_ {0} ^ {t} \sigma H _ {u} ^ {1} (S _ {u} ^ {0}) ^ {- 1} S _ {u} ^ {1} d W _ {u} ^ {\mu}
$$

is a local martingale. In fact, consider the stopping times 

$$
T _ {n} = \inf \left\{t \geq 0: \int_ {0} ^ {t} \left(\sigma H _ {u} ^ {1} \left(S _ {u} ^ {0}\right) ^ {- 1} S _ {u} ^ {1}\right) ^ {2} d u \geq n \right\}.
$$

Then the $\left( T _ { n } \right)$ are increasing and $\operatorname* { l i m } _ { n } T _ { n } = T$ . Furthermore, $\left( M _ { t \wedge T _ { n } } \right)$ is a uniformly integrable martingale under measure $P ^ { \mu }$ for each n. Consequently, $\left( \widetilde { V } _ { t } ( \phi _ { t } ) \right)$ is a local martingale under $P ^ { \mu }$ 

-Suppose $\xi = \xi ( \omega )$ is a non-negative ${ \mathcal { F } } .$ measurable random variable with $E ^ { \mu } \left( \xi \right) < \infty$ , where $E ^ { \mu } \left( \cdot \right)$ denotes expectation with respect to the measure $P ^ { \mu }$ . A strategy $\phi \in S F$ will belong to $S F ( \xi )$ if 

$$
\widetilde {V} _ {t} (\phi_ {t}) \geq - E ^ {\mu} \left(\xi   | \mathcal {F} _ {t}\right) \text { a.s. for } t \geq 0.
$$

A strategy $\phi \in S F ( \xi )$ will provide a hedge against a maximum loss of $\xi .$ Under this condition, Fatou’s lemma ( [279, Chap. II, 6]) can be applied, and the local martingale $\widetilde { V } _ { t } ( \phi )$ is a supermartingale under measure P<sup>µ</sup>. 

Consequently, if $\tau _ { 1 }$ -and $\tau _ { 2 }$ are two stopping times, with $\tau _ { i } \leq T$ and $\tau _ { 1 } \leq \tau _ { 2 } \ \mathrm { a . s . }$ , then 

$$
E ^ {\mu} (\widetilde {V} _ {\tau_ {2}} (\phi) | \mathcal {F} _ {\tau_ {1}}) \leq \widetilde {V} _ {\tau_ {1}} (\phi_ {\tau_ {1}})
$$

from the optimal stopping theorem. In particular, if $V _ { 0 } ( \phi _ { 0 } ) = x \ge 0$ and $\phi \in S F ( \xi )$ , then 

$$
E ^ {\mu} \left(\widetilde {V} _ {\tau} (\phi)\right) = E ^ {\mu} \left(e ^ {- r t} V _ {\tau} (\phi_ {\tau})\right) \leq x.\tag{7.29}
$$

Let us summarise these observations in the following result. 

Lemma 7.5.3. a) $I f \phi \in S F$ , then $\widetilde V ( \phi )$ is a local martingale. 

b) If $\phi \in S F ( \xi )$ , then $\widetilde V ( \phi )$ is also a supermartingale. 

c) $I f \phi \in S F ( 0 )$ , then $\widetilde V ( \phi )$ is also a non-negative supermartingale. 

Note that if $\phi \in S F ( 0 )$ , then simultaneous borrowing from the bank and stocks is not permitted. 

Definition 7.5.4. A strategy $\phi \in S F$ is said to provide an arbitrage $o p \textmd { - }$ portunity if, with $V _ { 0 } ( \phi ) = x \le 0$ , we have $V _ { T } ( \phi ) \geq 0$ a.s. and 

$$
P (V _ {T} (\phi) > 0) > 0.
$$

We can then establish the following lemma. 

Lemma 7.5.5. $I f \xi$ is a non-negative -measurable random variable with 

$$
E ^ {\mu} (\xi) <   \infty ,
$$

then any $\phi \in S F ( \xi )$ does not provide an arbitrage opportunity. 

Proof. Equation (7.29) rules out the possibility of $\phi$ providing an arbitrage opportunity. □ 

## Superhedging

We turn to the continuous-time analogue of the brief discussion outlined in Section 2.4. 

Definition 7.5.6. Suppose $T ~ > ~ 0$ and $f _ { T }$ is an $\mathcal { F } _ { T ^ { - } } \mathrm { m e a s u r a b l e } .$ , nonnegative random variable. A strategy $\phi \in S F$ is a hedge for the European claim $f _ { T }$ with initial investment x if 

$$
V _ {0} (\phi) = x,
$$

$$
V _ {T} (\phi) \geq f _ {T} \mathrm{a.s.}
$$

We call $\phi \in S F$ an $( x , f _ { T } )$ -hedge. 

Although the next definition can be given for strategies in $S F$ , we shall restrict ourselves to strategies in $S F ( 0 )$ 

Definition 7.5.7. The investment price $C ( T , f _ { T } )$ for the European claim $f _ { T }$ at time $T > 0$ is the smallest initial investment with which the investor can attain an amount $f _ { T }$ at time T using strategies from $S F ( 0 )$ 

More precisely, write $\Sigma ( T , x , f _ { T } )$ for the set of $( x , f _ { T } )$ -hedges that belong to $S F ( 0 )$ . Then 

$$
C (T, f _ {T}) = \inf \left\{x \geq 0: \Sigma (T, x, f _ {T}) \neq \emptyset \right\}.
$$

For any European claim $f _ { T }$ , we must therefore do the following: 

a) Determine the investment price $C ( T , f _ { T } )$ 

b) Determine the $( x , f _ { T } )$ -hedging strategy $\phi \in S F ( 0 )$ for $x = C ( T , f _ { T } )$ 

Remark 7.5.8. A European option with exercise time $T > 0$ and payment $f _ { T }$ gives the buyer of the contract the right to obtain an amount $f _ { T }$ at time $T .$ 

Clearly, if the seller of the contract can start with an amount $x =$ $C ( T , f _ { T } )$ and obtain $V _ { T } ( \phi _ { T } ) \ge f _ { T }$ at time T, then $C ( T , f _ { T } )$ is the fair, or rational, price for the option from the seller’s point of view. The discussion in Section 2.4 also applies here, in the continuous-time setting, and shows that (in a complete market, which is the case here) $C ( T , f _ { T } )$ is also the fair price from the buyer’s standpoint. 

Recall that the price at time t of the European call option on $S ^ { 1 }$ with an exercise time $T$ and a strike price $K$ corresponds to taking $f _ { T } = ( S _ { T } ^ { 1 } - K ) ^ { + }$ 

## Pricing

Suppose that $\phi \in S F ( 0 )$ . Then, since $\widetilde V ( \phi )$ is a supermartingale, we have 

$$
x = V _ {0} (\phi_ {0}) \geq E ^ {\mu} \left(e ^ {- r T} V _ {T} (\phi_ {T})\right).
$$

If, further, $\phi$ is an $( x , f _ { T } ) \mathrm { - h e d g e }$ , then 

$$
x \geq E ^ {\mu} \left(e ^ {- r T} f _ {T}\right).
$$

Consequently, the rational investment price $C ( T , f _ { T } )$ satisfies 

$$
C (T, f _ {T}) \geq E ^ {\mu} \left(e ^ {- r T} f _ {T}\right).
$$

From (7.27), 

$$
d S _ {t} ^ {1} = S _ {t} ^ {1} (\mu d t + \sigma d B _ {t}),
$$

where B is a standard Brownian motion under P. Write $S ^ { 1 } ( \mu )$ for the solution of (7.27). Then, from (7.28), under the measure $P ^ { \mu }$ , the process $S ^ { 1 } ( \mu )$ satisfies 

$$
d S _ {t} ^ {1} = S _ {t} ^ {1} \left(r d t + \sigma d W _ {t} ^ {\mu}\right),
$$

where $W ^ { \mu }$ is a standard Brownian motion. 

Let us write $S ^ { 1 } ( r )$ for the solution of 

$$
d S _ {t} ^ {1} (r) = S _ {t} ^ {1} (r) (r d t + \sigma d B _ {t}).
$$

Then 

$$
\operatorname{Law} \left(f _ {T} \left(S ^ {1} (\mu)\right) | P ^ {\mu}\right) = \operatorname{Law} \left(f _ {T} \left(S ^ {1} (r)\right) | P\right)
$$

and 

$$
E ^ {\mu} \left(e ^ {- r T} f _ {T}\right) = E ^ {\mu} \left(e ^ {- r T} f _ {T} \left(S ^ {1} (\mu)\right)\right) = E \left(e ^ {- r T} f _ {T} \left(S ^ {1} (r)\right)\right).
$$

This quantity has the unexpected property that it does not depend on $\mu .$ 

Suppose that $f _ { T }$ is a non-negative ${ \mathcal { F } } _ { T } .$ -measurable random variable such that 

$$
E ^ {\mu} \left(f _ {T} ^ {2}\right) <   \infty .\tag{7.30}
$$

Recall that $E ^ { \mu } \left( f _ { T } ^ { 2 } \right) = E \left( \Lambda _ { T } f _ { T } ^ { 2 } \right)$ , where 

$$
\Lambda_ {T} = \exp \left\{- \int_ {0} ^ {T} \theta d B _ {s} - \frac {1}{2} \int_ {0} ^ {T} \theta^ {2} d s \right\}, \quad \theta = \frac {\mu - r}{\sigma}.
$$

A suficient condition for (7.30) is that $E \left( f _ { T } ^ { 2 + \delta } \right) < \infty$ for some $\delta > 0$ 

Consider the square integrable $( P ^ { \mu } , \mathcal { F } _ { t } )$ -martingale 

$$
N _ {t} = E ^ {\mu} \left(e ^ {- r T} f _ {T} | \mathcal {F} _ {t}\right) \text {   for   } 0 \leq t \leq T.
$$

From the martingale representation result, Theorem 7.3.9, there is a predictable process $\gamma$ such that 

$$
E \left(\int_ {0} ^ {T} \gamma_ {s} ^ {2} d s\right) <   \infty , \quad N _ {t} = N _ {0} + \int_ {0} ^ {t} \gamma_ {s} d W _ {s} ^ {\mu} \text { a.s. }\tag{7.31}
$$

Here, $N _ { 0 } = E ^ { \mu } \left( e ^ { - r T } f _ { T } \right)$ 

Now take 

$$
H _ {t} ^ {1} = \gamma_ {t} e ^ {r t} \sigma^ {- 1} (S _ {t} ^ {1}) ^ {- 1} H _ {t} ^ {0} = N _ {t} - \sigma^ {- 1} \gamma_ {t};
$$

consider the trading strategy $\phi _ { t } ^ { * } = ( H _ { t } ^ { 0 } , H _ { t } ^ { 1 } )$ ). 

Lemma 7.5.9. a) The strategy $\phi _ { t } ^ { * }$ is self-financing and 

b) 

$$
N _ {t} = \widetilde {V} _ {t} (\phi^ {*}) = e ^ {- r t} V _ {t} (\phi^ {*}).
$$

Proof. By definition, 

$$
V _ {t} (\phi^ {*}) = H _ {t} ^ {0} S _ {t} ^ {0} + H _ {t} ^ {1} S _ {t} ^ {1} = N _ {t} S _ {t} ^ {0}.\tag{7.32}
$$

Therefore 

$$
\begin{array}{l} d V _ {t} (\phi^ {*}) = N _ {t} d S _ {t} ^ {0} + S _ {t} ^ {0} d N _ {t} \\ \qquad = r N _ {t} S _ {t} ^ {0} d t + S _ {t} ^ {0} \gamma_ {t} d W _ {t} ^ {\mu} \\ \qquad = \left(N _ {t} - \sigma^ {- 1} \gamma_ {t}\right) d S _ {t} ^ {0} + \sigma^ {- 1} \gamma_ {t} S _ {t} ^ {0} (r d t + \sigma d W _ {t} ^ {\mu}) \\ \qquad = H _ {t} ^ {0} d S _ {t} ^ {0} + H _ {t} ^ {1} d S _ {t} ^ {1}. \end{array}
$$

Consequently, the strategy $\phi _ { t } ^ { * } = \left( H _ { t } ^ { 0 } , H _ { t } ^ { 1 } \right)$ is self-financing. (The condi-- tion (7.22) is satisfied because of (7.31) and the path-continuity of N.) 

From (7.32) we see that 

$$
N _ {t} = \left(S _ {t} ^ {0}\right) ^ {- 1} V _ {t} (\phi^ {*}),
$$

that is, the $P ^ { \mu } .$ -martingale $N$ is the discounted wealth process of the strategy $\phi _ { t } ^ { * }$ . Also, 

$$
V _ {t} (\phi^ {*}) = N _ {t} S _ {t} ^ {0} = E ^ {\mu} \left(S _ {t} ^ {0} \left(S _ {t} ^ {0}\right) ^ {- 1} f _ {T} | \mathcal {F} _ {t}\right) = E ^ {\mu} \left(e ^ {- r (T - t)} f _ {T} | \mathcal {F} _ {t}\right).
$$

In particular, 

$$
V _ {0} (\phi^ {*}) = E ^ {\mu} \left(e ^ {- r T} f _ {T}\right), \qquad V _ {T} (\phi^ {*}) = f _ {T}.
$$

These equations mean that $\phi ^ { * }$ is an $( x , f _ { T } )$ -hedge with initial capital 

$$
x = E ^ {\mu} \left(e ^ {- r T} f _ {T}\right).
$$

Clearly, if $\phi \in S F$ is any other hedge for $f _ { T }$ with initial capital x, then 

$$
V _ {T} (\phi) \geq V _ {T} (\phi_ {T} ^ {*}) = f _ {T}.
$$

Consequently, the rational price for the European option $f _ { T }$ is 

$$
C (T, f _ {T}) = E ^ {\mu} \left(e ^ {- r T} f _ {T}\right) = E \left(e ^ {- r T} f _ {T} \left(S ^ {1} (r)\right)\right).
$$
