d S _ {t} ^ {1} = r _ {t} S _ {t} ^ {1} d t + \sigma (t) S _ {t} ^ {1} d W _ {t}.
$$

Here $W$ is a Brownian motion on a probability space $( \Omega , { \mathcal { F } } , P )$ with a filtration $( \mathcal { F } _ { t } ) _ { 0 \leq t \leq T ^ { * } }$ . In general, $( \mathcal { F } _ { t } )$ may be larger than the filtration generated by W. The short-term rate r and volatility $\sigma$ are adapted (random) processes. The value of a dollar in the money market is, as before, $\begin{array} { r } { S _ { t } ^ { 0 } = \exp \left\{ \int _ { 0 } ^ { t } r _ { u } d u \right\} } \end{array}$ . We note that 

$$
d \left(\frac {S _ {t} ^ {1}}{S _ {t} ^ {0}}\right) = \frac {S _ {t} ^ {1}}{S _ {t} ^ {0}} \sigma (t) d W _ {t},
$$

so the discounted asset price is a martingale. 

When we consider the discounted price $\frac { S _ { t } ^ { 1 } } { S _ { t } ^ { 0 } }$ , we are saying that, at time t, one unit of stock is worth $\frac { S _ { t } ^ { 1 } } { S _ { t } ^ { 0 } }$ units of the money market account. Similarly, from the expression after Definition 9.1.2 at time t, with $T \leq T ^ { * }$ , the $T _ { - }$ maturity bond is worth $\frac { B ( t , T ) } { S _ { t } ^ { 0 } }$ units of the money market account; again this discounted price is $E \left( \left( S _ { t } ^ { 0 } \right) ^ { - 1 } \left| \mathcal { F } _ { t } \right. \right)$ and so is a martingale. 

Now any strictly positive price process could play the role of $S ^ { 0 }$ , and other assets can be expressed in terms of this process. As in the discretetime setting, we have the following 

Definition 9.3.1. A strictly positive process $Z$ is the num´eraire of the model if all asset prices are expressed in terms of $Z .$ . 

For example, the T-maturity bond price $B ( t , T )$ could be taken as the num´eraire for $t \leq T$ . In terms of $B ( t , T )$ , at time t, the risky asset is worth $\begin{array} { r } { \frac { S _ { t } ^ { 1 } } { B ( t , T ) } = F ( t , T ) } \end{array}$ units of $B ( t , T )$ , where $B ( t , T )$ is the forward price of Definition 9.1.3. Of course, the price of the bond itself in terms of the num´eraire $B ( t , T )$ is just $\begin{array} { r } { \frac { B ( t , T ) } { B ( t , T ) } = 1 } \end{array}$ unit. 

We could also, for example, take $S ^ { 1 }$ to be the num´eraire. Then the price at time t of a T-maturity bond in units of $S _ { t } ^ { 1 }$ is $\begin{array} { r } { \frac { B ( t , T ) } { S _ { t } ^ { 1 } } = \frac { 1 } { F ( t , T ) } } \end{array}$ 

Definition 9.3.2. Suppose $Z$ is a strictly positive process so $Z$ can be taken as a num´eraire. A probability measure $P _ { Z }$ on $( \Omega , { \mathcal { F } } , P )$ is said to be risk-neutral for $Z$ if the price of any asset divided by $Z \ ( { \mathrm { i . e . } }$ , expressed in units of $Z )$ is a martingale under $P _ { Z }$ 

We assumed in Notation 9.1 that the original measure $P$ was riskneutral for the num´eraire $S ^ { 0 }$ 

Theorem 9.3.3. Suppose $Z$ is a num´eraire, so it is the strictly positive price process of some asset. Define a new probability measure $P _ { Z }$ on $( \Omega , { \mathcal { F } } , P )$ by putting for any $A \in \mathcal { F } _ { T }$ 

$$
P _ {Z} (A) = \frac {1}{Z _ {0}} \int_ {A} \frac {Z _ {T}}{S _ {T} ^ {0}} d P.
$$

Then $P _ { Z }$ is equivalent to $P$ and is a risk-neutral measure for the num´eraire Z. 

Proof. Note that, for $A \in \mathcal { F } _ { T ^ { * } }$ ∗ 

$$
P (A) = Z _ {0} \int_ {A} S _ {T} ^ {0} Z _ {T} ^ {- 1} d P _ {Z},
$$

so P and $P _ { Z }$ have the same null sets. 

From the definition of $P , { \frac { Z } { S ^ { 0 } } }$ is a martingale under P. Consequently, 

$$
P _ {Z} (\Omega) = \frac {1}{Z _ {0}} \int_ {\Omega} \frac {Z _ {T}}{S _ {T} ^ {0}} d P = \frac {1}{Z _ {0}} E \left(\frac {Z _ {T}}{S _ {T} ^ {0}}\right) = \frac {Z _ {0}}{Z _ {0} S _ {0} ^ {0}} = 1
$$

because $\frac { Z } { S ^ { 0 } }$ is a P-martingale. Consequently, $P _ { Z }$ is a probability measure. 

Now suppose X is an asset price process, so $\frac { X } { S ^ { 0 } }$ is a P-martingale. We shall show that $\textstyle { \frac { X } { Z } }$ is then a $P _ { Z }$ -martingale. We have 

$$
M _ {t} = \frac {Z _ {t}}{Z _ {0} S _ {t} ^ {0}} = \frac {1}{Z _ {0}} E \left(\frac {Z _ {T ^ {*}}}{S ^ {0} (T ^ {*})} | \mathcal {F} _ {t}\right)
$$

because $\frac { Z } { S ^ { 0 } }$ is a P-martingale. From Lemma 7.2.2, $\textstyle { \frac { X } { Z } }$ is a P<sub>Z</sub>-martingale if and only if $\begin{array} { r } { \frac { X } { Z } M = \frac { 1 } { Z _ { 0 } } \frac { X } { Z } \frac { \bar { Z } } { S ^ { 0 } } } \end{array}$ is a P-martingale. The result follows. □ 

Remark 9.3.4. Note that, if we take the num´eraire $Z$ to be the bond price $B ( t , T )$ for $0 < T \leq T ^ { * }$ , then the risk-neutral measure $P _ { B ( t , T ) }$ for this bond has a density 

$$
\frac {B (T , T)}{B (0 , T) S _ {T} ^ {0}} = \frac {1}{B (0 , T) S _ {T} ^ {0}}.
$$

Consequently, the risk-neutral measure for the bond B is just the forward measure given in Definition 9.1.5. Note that, as the bond is not defined after time $T ,$ the measure change is defined only on $\mathcal { F } _ { T }$ , that is, only up to time $T .$ 

With the T-maturity bond as num´eraire, we have seen that the price of the risky asset $S ^ { 1 }$ is given by its forward price 

$$
F (t, T) = \frac {S _ {t} ^ {1}}{B (t , T)} \mathrm{for} 0 \leq t \leq T.
$$

Now $F ( t , T )$ must be a martingale under the risk-neutral measure $P _ { B ( t , T ) }$ for B and consequently the diferential d $F ( t , T )$ must be of the form 

$$
d F (t, T) = \sigma_ {F} (t, T) F (t, T) d W _ {B} (t) \text {   for   } 0 \leq t \leq T.\tag{9.2}
$$

We note that this is a diferential without any bounded variation dt terms and $W _ { B } ( t ) , 0 \leq t \leq T$ , is a process that is a standard Brownian motion under the measure $P _ { B } ( t , T )$ . As usual, $\sigma _ { F } ( t , T )$ can be taken to be non- negative. 

Suppose now the price $S ^ { 1 }$ of the risky asset is taken as the num´eraire. Of course, in terms of $S ^ { 1 }$ , the price of the risky asset $S ^ { 1 }$ is always 1 unit. The risk-neutral measure for the num´eraire $S ^ { 1 }$ is defined by 

$$
P _ {S} (A) = \frac {1}{S _ {0} ^ {1}} \int_ {A} \frac {S ^ {1} (T ^ {*})}{S ^ {0} (T ^ {*})} d P \text {   for   } A \in \mathcal {F} _ {T ^ {*}}.
$$

In terms of units of $S ^ { 1 }$ , the value of a T-maturity bond is just 

$$
\frac {B (t , T)}{S _ {t} ^ {1}} = \frac {1}{F (t , T)} \text {   for   } 0 \leq t \leq T \leq T ^ {*}.
$$

However, this is to be a martingale under $P _ { S }$ , so it has a diferential 

$$
d \left(\frac {1}{F (t , T)}\right) = \sigma_ {F ^ {- 1}} (t, T) \left(\frac {1}{F (t , T)}\right) d W _ {S} (t) \text {   for   } 0 \leq t \leq T \leq T ^ {*}.\tag{9.3}
$$

Again there will be no dt terms in the diferential, and $( W _ { S } ( t ) ) _ { 0 \leq t \leq T }$ is a standard Brownian motion under $P _ { S }$ . Again, $\sigma _ { F ^ { - 1 } } ( t , T )$ can be taken as non-negative. 

Theorem 9.3.5. $\sigma _ { F } ( t , T ) = \sigma _ { F ^ { - 1 } } ( t , T )$ 

Proof. Applying the Itˆo rule to (9.3), we have 

$$
\begin{array}{r l} d \left(\frac {1}{F (t , T)}\right) & = - \frac {1}{F (t , T) ^ {2}} \sigma_ {F} (t, T) F (t, T) d W _ {B} (t) \\ & + \frac {1}{F (t , T) ^ {3}} \sigma_ {F} (t, T) ^ {2} F (t, T) ^ {2} d t \\ & = \sigma_ {F} (t, T) \left(\frac {1}{F (t , T)}\right) (- d W _ {B} (t) + \sigma_ {B} (t, T) d t). \end{array}\tag{9.4}
$$

We know that $\left( W _ { B } ( t ) \right)$ is a standard Brownian motion under $P _ { B ( t , T ) }$ , as is $\left( - W _ { B } ( t ) \right)$ . Therefore, under $P _ { B ( t , T ) }$ the process $\frac { 1 } { F ( t , T ) }$ has volatility $\sigma _ { F } ( t , T )$ and mean rate of return $\sigma _ { F } ( t , T ) ^ { 2 }$ . Changing the measure from $P _ { B ( t , T ) }$ to $P _ { S }$ transforms $\frac { 1 } { F ( t , T ) }$ into a P<sub>S</sub>-martingale. Consequently, under $P _ { S }$ the mean rate of return of $\frac { 1 } { F ( t , T ) }$ is zero, but the volatility is not changed. In fact, from (9.4) 

$$
d \left(\frac {1}{F (t , T)}\right) = \sigma_ {F ^ {- 1}} (t, T) \frac {1}{F (t , T)} d W _ {S} (t) \text {   for   } 0 \leq t \leq T \leq T ^ {*}.\tag{9.5}
$$

Comparing (9.4) and (9.5), we see that 

$$
\sigma_ {F} (t, T) = \sigma_ {F ^ {- 1}} (t, T), \qquad W _ {S} (t) = - W _ {B} (t) + \int_ {0} ^ {t} \sigma_ {F} (s, T) d s.
$$

## 9.4 A General Option Pricing Formula

Following El Karoui, Geman, and Rochet [102] the risk-neutral measures for the num´eraires $S ^ { 1 }$ and B can be used to express the price of a European call option: 

$$
\begin{array}{l} V _ {0} = E \left(\left(S _ {T} ^ {0}\right) ^ {- 1} \left(S _ {T} ^ {1} - K\right) ^ {+}\right) \\ \quad = E \left(\left(S _ {T} ^ {0}\right) ^ {- 1} S _ {T} ^ {1} \mathbf {1} _ {\left\{S _ {T} ^ {1} > K \right\}}\right) - K E \left(\left(S _ {T} ^ {0}\right) ^ {- 1} \mathbf {1} _ {\left\{S _ {T} ^ {1} > K \right\}}\right) \\ \quad = S _ {0} ^ {1} \int_ {\left\{S _ {T} ^ {1} > K \right\}} \frac {S _ {T} ^ {1}}{S _ {0} ^ {1} S _ {T} ^ {0}} d P - K B (0, T) \int_ {\left\{S _ {T} ^ {1} > K \right\}} \frac {1}{B (0 , T) S _ {T} ^ {0}} d P \\ \quad = S _ {0} ^ {1} P _ {S} \left(S _ {T} ^ {1} > K\right) - K B (0, T) P _ {B} \left(S _ {T} ^ {1} > K\right) \\ \quad = S _ {0} ^ {1} P _ {S} \left(F (T, T) > K\right) - K B (0, T) P _ {B} \left(F (T, T) > K\right) \\ \quad = S _ {0} ^ {1} P _ {S} \left(\frac {1}{F (T , T)} <   \frac {1}{K}\right) - K B (0, T) P _ {B} \left(F (T, T) > K\right). \end{array}
$$

Let us suppose that $\sigma _ { F } ( t , T )$ is a constant $\sigma _ { F }$ . Then, from (9.3), recalling that $\sigma _ { F } = \sigma _ { F ^ { - 1 } }$ , we have 

$$
\frac {1}{F (T , T)} = \frac {B (0 , T)}{S _ {0} ^ {1}} \exp \left\{\sigma_ {F} W _ {S} (T) - \frac {1}{2} \sigma_ {F} ^ {2} T \right\}
$$

where $W _ { S }$ is a standard Brownian motion under $P _ { S }$ . Consequently, 

$$
\begin{array}{l} P _ {S} \left(\frac {1}{F (T , T)} <   \frac {1}{K}\right) = P _ {S} \left(\sigma_ {F} W _ {S} (T) - \frac {1}{2} \sigma_ {F} ^ {2} T <   \log \left(\frac {S _ {0} ^ {1}}{K B (0 , T)}\right)\right) \\ = P _ {S} \left(\frac {W _ {S} (T)}{\sqrt {T}} <   \frac {1}{\sigma_ {F} \sqrt {T}} \log \left(\frac {S _ {0} ^ {1}}{K B (0 , T)}\right) + \frac {1}{2} \sigma_ {F} \sqrt {T}\right). \end{array}
$$

Now $\frac { W _ { S } ( T ) } { \sqrt { T } }$ is a standard normal random variable. Writing, as usual, Φ for the standard normal distribution, we have 

$$
P _ {S} \left(\frac {1}{F (T , T)} <   \frac {1}{K}\right) = \Phi (h _ {1}),
$$

where 

$$
h _ {1} = \frac {1}{\sigma_ {F} \sqrt {T}} \left(\log \left(\frac {S _ {0} ^ {1}}{K B (0 , T)}\right) + \frac {1}{2} \sigma_ {F} ^ {2} T\right).
$$

From (9.2), we have that, with $P _ { B } = P _ { B } ( t , T )$ ， 

$$
F (T, T) = \frac {S _ {0} ^ {1}}{B (0 , T)} \exp \left\{\sigma_ {F} W _ {B} (T) - \frac {1}{2} \sigma_ {F} ^ {2} T \right\},
$$

where $W _ { B }$ is a standard Brownian motion under $P _ { B }$ . Therefore 

$$
\begin{array}{l} P _ {B} \left(F (T, T) > K\right) = P _ {B} \left(\sigma_ {F} W _ {B} (T) - \frac {1}{2} \sigma_ {F} ^ {2} T > \log \left(\frac {K B (0 , T)}{S _ {0} ^ {1}}\right)\right) \\ = P _ {B} \left(\frac {W _ {B} (T)}{\sqrt {T}} <   \frac {1}{\sigma_ {F} \sqrt {T}} \left(\log \left(\frac {K B (0 , T)}{S _ {0} ^ {1}}\right) + \frac {1}{2} \sigma_ {F} ^ {2} T\right)\right) \\ = P _ {B} \left(- \frac {W _ {B} (T)}{\sqrt {T}} <   \frac {1}{\sigma_ {F} \sqrt {T}} \left(\log \left(\frac {S _ {0} ^ {1}}{K B (0 , T)}\right) - \frac {1}{2} \sigma_ {F} ^ {2} T\right)\right). \end{array}
$$

Again, $\frac { W _ { B } ( T ) } { \sqrt { T } }$ is a standard normal random variable, so that 

$$
P _ {B} (F (T, T) > K) = \Phi (h _ {2}),
$$

where 

$$
h _ {2} = \frac {1}{\sigma_ {F} \sqrt {T}} \left(\log \left(\frac {S _ {0} ^ {1}}{K B (0 , T)}\right) - \frac {1}{2} \sigma_ {F} ^ {2} T\right).
$$

Consequently, the price of the European call is 

$$
V _ {0} = S _ {0} ^ {1} \Phi (h _ {1}) - K B (0, T) \Phi (h _ {2}).
$$

If r is constant, then $B ( 0 , T ) ~ = ~ e ^ { - r T }$ and this formula reduces to the Black-Scholes formula of Theorem 7.6.2. 

A modification of this argument shows that for any intermediate time $0 \leq t \leq T$ , the value of the European call, with strike price K and expiration time T, is 

$$
V _ {t} = S _ {t} ^ {1} \Phi (h _ {1} (t)) - K B (0, T) \Phi (h _ {2} (t)),\tag{9.6}
$$

where now, recalling that $\begin{array} { r } { F ( t , T ) = \frac { S _ { t } ^ { 1 } } { B ( t , T ) } } \end{array}$ 2 

$$
h _ {1} (t) = \frac {1}{\sigma_ {F} \sqrt {T - t}} \left(\log \left(\frac {F (t , T)}{K}\right) + \frac {1}{2} \sigma_ {F} ^ {2} (T - t)\right),
$$

$$
h _ {2} (t) = \frac {1}{\sigma_ {F} \sqrt {T - t}} \left(\log \left(\frac {F (t , T)}{K}\right) - \frac {1}{2} \sigma_ {F} ^ {2} (T - t)\right).
$$

Formula (9.6) suggests the European call can be hedged, at each time t, by holding $\Phi ( h _ { 1 } ( t ) )$ units of $S ^ { 1 }$ and shorting $K \Phi ( h _ { 2 } ( t ) )$ bonds. 

We shall establish that this is a self-financing strategy. However, first we show that a change of num´eraire does not change a trading strategy. 

Lemma 9.4.1. Suppose $S ^ { 1 } , S ^ { 2 } , \ldots , S ^ { d }$ are the price processes of k assets. Consider a self-financing strategy $( \theta ^ { 1 } , \theta ^ { 2 } , \dots , \theta ^ { d } )$ , where $\theta _ { t } ^ { i }$ represents the number of units of asset i held at time t. Suppose Z is a num´eraire and $\begin{array} { r } { \widehat { S } ^ { i } = \frac { S ^ { i } } { Z } , \ : 1 \leq i \leq \dot { d } , } \end{array}$ is the price of asset i in units of Z. Then $\theta ^ { i }$ represents ,the number of units of ${ \widehat { S } } ^ { i }$ in the portfolio, evaluated in terms of the new ,num´eraire (there are no other riskless assets). 

Proof. The wealth process is 

$$
X _ {t} = \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {d} \theta_ {t} ^ {i} S _ {t} ^ {i}.
$$

As the strategy is self-financing, we have 

$$
d X _ {t} = \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {d} \theta_ {t} ^ {i} d S _ {t} ^ {i}.
$$

Write $\begin{array} { r } { \widehat { X } _ { t } = \frac { X _ { t } } { Z _ { t } } } \end{array}$ for the wealth process expressed in terms of the num´eraire ,Z. Then 

$$
\begin{array}{l} d \widehat {X} = Z ^ {- 1} d X + X d \left(\frac {1}{Z}\right) + d \left\langle X, \frac {1}{Z} \right\rangle \\ \quad = \frac {1}{Z} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {d} \theta^ {i} d S ^ {i} + \left(\sum_ {i = 1} ^ {d} \theta^ {i} S ^ {i}\right) d \left(\frac {1}{Z}\right) + \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {d} \theta^ {i} d \left\langle S ^ {i}, \frac {1}{Z} \right\rangle \\ \quad = \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {d} \theta^ {i} d \widehat {S} ^ {i}. \end{array}
$$

Corollary 9.4.2. In Lemma $9 . 4 . 1 ,$ , the strategy $\left( \theta ^ { 1 } , \theta ^ { 2 } , \dots , \theta ^ { d } \right)$ determined -the wealth process X. Suppose now that components $\theta ^ { 1 } , \theta ^ { 2 } , \ldots , \theta ^ { d - 1 }$ are given, together with the wealth process X. Then 

$$
\theta_ {t} ^ {d} = \frac {1}{S _ {t} ^ {d}} \left(X _ {t} - \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {d - 1} \theta_ {t} ^ {i} S _ {t} ^ {i}\right)
$$

and 

$$
d X _ {t} = \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {d} \theta_ {t} ^ {i} d S _ {t} ^ {i} = \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {d - 1} \theta_ {t} ^ {i} d S _ {t} ^ {i} + \frac {1}{S _ {t} ^ {d}} \left(X _ {t} - \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {d - 1} \theta_ {t} ^ {i} S _ {t} ^ {i}\right) d S _ {t} ^ {d}.
$$

In terms of the num´eraire $Z _ { i }$ , we still have 

$$
\theta_ {t} ^ {d} = \frac {1}{S _ {t} ^ {d}} \left(X _ {t} - \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {d - 1} \theta_ {t} ^ {i} S _ {t} ^ {i}\right) = \frac {1}{\widehat {S} _ {t} ^ {d}} \left(\widehat {X} _ {t} - \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {d - 1} \theta_ {t} ^ {i} \widehat {S} _ {t} ^ {i}\right)
$$

and 

$$
d \widehat {X} _ {t} = \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {d - 1} \theta_ {t} ^ {i} d \widehat {S} _ {t} ^ {i} + \frac {1}{\widehat {S} _ {t} ^ {d}} \left(\widehat {X} _ {t} - \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {d - 1} \theta_ {t} ^ {i} \widehat {S} _ {t} ^ {i}\right) d \widehat {S} _ {t} ^ {d}.
$$

Let us return to the price (9.6) at time t for a European call option. 

Theorem 9.4.3. Holding $\Phi ( h _ { 1 } ( t ) )$ units of $S ^ { 1 }$ and shorting $K \Phi ( h _ { 2 } ( t ) )$ bonds at each time $t \in [ 0 , T ]$ is a self-financing strategy for the European call option with strike price K and expiration time $T .$ 

Proof. This result could be established using Theorem 9.4.1. Alternatively, suppose we start with an initial investment of $\$ 1$ and hold $\Phi ( h _ { 1 } ( t ) )$ units of $S ^ { 1 }$ at each time t. To maintain this position, we short as many bonds as necessary. 

If we can show that the number of bonds we must short at time t is $K \Phi ( h _ { 2 } ( t ) )$ , then the value of our portfolio is indeed 

$$
\Phi (h _ {1} (t)) S _ {t} ^ {1} - K B (t, T) \Phi (h _ {2} (t)),
$$

which equals $V _ { t }$ , the price of the call option at time $t \in [ 0 , T ]$ , and we have a hedge. 

Let us write $\theta _ { t } ^ { 1 } = \Phi ( h _ { 1 } ( t ) )$ so that at time t we hold $\theta _ { t } ^ { 1 }$ units of $S ^ { 1 }$ . 

Suppose $X _ { t }$ is the value of our portfolio at time t. Then we invest $X _ { t } - \theta _ { t } ^ { 1 } S _ { t } ^ { 1 }$ in the bond and the number of bonds in the portfolio is 

$$
\theta_ {t} ^ {2} = \frac {X _ {t} - \theta_ {t} ^ {1} S _ {t} ^ {1}}{B (t , T)}.
$$

Then 

$$
d X _ {t} = \theta_ {t} ^ {1} d S _ {t} ^ {1} + \theta_ {t} ^ {2} d B (t, T) = \theta_ {t} ^ {1} d S _ {t} ^ {1} + \frac {X _ {t} - \theta_ {t} ^ {1} S _ {t} ^ {1}}{B (t , T)} d B (t, T).
$$

We must show that, if $X _ { 0 } = V _ { 0 }$ , then 

$$
X _ {t} = V _ {t} \text { for } 0 \leq t \leq T.
$$

To establish this, it is easier to work with $B ( t , T )$ as num´eraire. In terms of this zero coupon bond, the asset values $S ^ { 1 }$ , B, and X become 

$$
\widehat {S} _ {t} ^ {1} = \frac {S _ {t} ^ {1}}{B (t , T)} = F (t, T),
$$

$$
\begin{array}{r l} & {\widehat {B} (t, T) = 1,} \\ & {\widehat {X} _ {t} = \Phi (h _ {1} (t)) F (t, T) + \widehat {X} _ {t} - \theta_ {t} ^ {1} S _ {t} ^ {1},} \end{array}
$$

and $d \widehat { X } _ { t } = \Phi ( h _ { 1 } ( t ) ) d F ( t , T )$ 

,The option value is 

$$
V _ {t} = \Phi (h _ {1} (t)) S _ {t} ^ {1} - K B (t, T) \Phi (h _ {2} (t)),
$$

and in terms of the num´eraire $B ( t , T )$ this becomes 

$$
\widehat {V} _ {t} = \Phi (h _ {1} (t)) F (t, T) - K \Phi (h _ {2} (t)).
$$

Consequently, 

$$
\begin{array}{l} d \widehat {V} _ {t} = \Phi (h _ {1} (t)) d F (t, T) + F (t, T) d \Phi (h _ {1} (t)) - K d \Phi (h _ {2} (t)) \\ + d \left\langle \Phi (h _ {1} (t)), F (t, T) \right\rangle . \end{array}
$$

Recall the dynamics (9.2) given by 

$$
d F (t, T) = \sigma_ {F} F (t, T) d W _ {B} (t).
$$

Now consider $\big ( \boldsymbol { \Phi } ( h _ { 1 } ( t ) ) \big )$ , where 

$$
h _ {1} (t) = \frac {1}{\sigma_ {F} \sqrt {T - t}} \left(\log \left(\frac {F (t , T)}{K}\right) + \frac {1}{2} \sigma_ {F} ^ {2} (T - t)\right).
$$

The Itˆo rule gives, after some cancellation, with $\phi$ as the standard normal density, 

$$
d \Phi (h _ {1} (t)) = \phi (h _ {1}) \cdot \frac {1}{\sigma_ {F} \sqrt {T - t}} \cdot \frac {1}{F} d F - \phi (h _ {1}) \frac {\sigma_ {F}}{2 \sqrt {T - t}} d t,
$$

where $\phi$ is the standard normal density function. 

Also, $F \phi ( h _ { 1 } ) = K \phi ( h _ { 2 } )$ , and some elementary but tedious calculations confirm that 

$$
F d \Phi (h _ {1}) - K d \Phi (h _ {2}) + d \left\langle \Phi (h _ {1}), F \right\rangle = 0.
$$

The result follows. 

## 9.5 Term Structure Models

Again let W be a standard Brownian motion on $( \Omega , { \mathcal { F } } , P )$ and $( \mathcal { F } _ { t } ) _ { 0 \leq t \leq T }$ the (completed) filtration generated by W. 

The instantaneous interest rate $r _ { t }$ is an adapted, measurable process and the num´eraire asset $S _ { t } ^ { 0 }$ has value 

$$
S _ {t} ^ {0} = \exp \left\{\int_ {0} ^ {t} r _ {u} d u \right\} \text { for } 0 \leq t \leq T.
$$

We have seen that the price at time $t \in [ 0 , T ]$ of a zero coupon bond maturing at time T is 

$$
B (t, T) = S _ {t} ^ {0} E \left(\left(S _ {t} ^ {0}\right) ^ {- 1} | \mathcal {F} _ {t}\right).
$$

If r is non-random, then 

$$
B (t, T) = \exp \left\{- \int_ {t} ^ {T} r _ {u} d u \right\}.
$$

Zero coupon bonds are traded in the market, and their prices can be used to calibrate the model. They are known as ‘zeros’. 

Definition 9.5.1. A term structure model is a mathematical model for the prices $B ( t , T )$ for all t and T with $0 \leq t \leq T \leq T _ { 2 }$ 

The yield $\begin{array} { r } { R ( t , T ) = \frac { \log B ( t , T ) } { T - t } } \end{array}$ provides a yield curve for each fixed time t as the graph of $R ( t , T )$ against T, which displays the average return of bonds after elimination of the distorting efects of maturity. We expect diferent yields at diferent maturities, reflecting market beliefs about future changes in interest rates. While greater uncertainty about interest rates in the distant future will tend to lead to increases in yield with maturity, high current rates (which may be expected to fall) can produce ‘inverted yield curves, in which long bonds will have a lower yield than short ones. A satisfactory term structure model should be able to handle both situations. 

Remark 9.5.2. Recall that we are working under a martingale, or riskneutral, measure $P$ and that 

$$
B (t, T) = S _ {t} ^ {0} E \left(\left(S _ {t} ^ {0}\right) ^ {- 1} | \mathcal {F} _ {t}\right).
$$

That is, 

$$
\frac {B (t , T)}{S _ {t} ^ {0}} = E \left(\left(S _ {t} ^ {0}\right) ^ {- 1} | \mathcal {F} _ {t}\right),
$$

and so $\left( \frac { B ( t , T ) } { S _ { t } ^ { 0 } } \right)$ is a martingale under $P .$ 

If the market measure P does not have the property that all processes $\left( \frac { B ( t , T ) } { S _ { t } ^ { 0 } } \right)$ are martingales, then the term structure model is free of arbitrage only if there is an equivalent measure $\widetilde { P }$ such that, under ${ \widetilde { P } } _ { ; }$ , all processes $\left( \frac { B ( t , T ) } { S _ { t } ^ { 0 } } \right)$ -are martingales for all maturity times $T .$ 

$B ( t , T )$ is a positive process for all T, so that, using the martingale representation theorem, dynamics for $B ( t , T )$ can be expressed in a lognormal form 

$$
d B (t, T) = \mu (t, T) B (t, T) d t + \sigma (t, T) B (t, T) d W _ {t} \text {   for   } t \in [ 0, T ].
$$

Consequently, 

$$
d \left(\frac {B (t , T)}{S _ {t} ^ {0}}\right) = (\mu (t, T) - r _ {t}) \frac {B (t , T)}{S _ {t} ^ {0}} d t + \sigma (t, T) \frac {B (t , T)}{S _ {t} ^ {0}} d W _ {t}
$$

and $\left( \frac { B ( t , T ) } { S _ { t } ^ { 0 } } \right)$ is a martingale under P if and only if $\mu ( t , T ) = r _ { t }$ 

The statement that 

$$
B (t, T) = E \left(\exp \left\{- \int_ {t} ^ {T} r _ {u} d u \right\} | \mathcal {F} _ {t}\right)
$$

is sometimes called the Local Expectations Hypothesis. 

The assumption that holding a discount bond to maturity gives the same return as rolling over a series of single-period bonds is called the Return to Maturity Expectations Hypothesis. In continuous time, it would state that, under some probability $P ^ { \prime }$ 

$$
\frac {1}{B (t , T)} = E _ {P ^ {\prime}} \left(\exp \left\{\int_ {t} ^ {T} r _ {u} d u \right\} | \mathcal {F} _ {t}\right).
$$

The Yield to Maturity Expectations Hypothesis states that the yield from holding a bond equals the yield from rolling over a series of singleperiod bonds. In continuous time, this would imply 

$$
B (t, T) = \exp \left\{- E _ {P ^ {\prime}} \left(\int_ {t} ^ {T} r _ {u} d u | \mathcal {F} _ {t}\right) \right\}
$$

for some probability $P ^ { \prime }$ . A fuller discussion of these concepts can be found in the papers of Frachot and Lesne [137], [138]. 

## 9.6 Short-rate Difusion Models

## Vasicek’s Model

Vasicek [296] proposed a mean-reverting version of the Ornstein-Uhlenbeck process for the short term rate r. Specifically, under the risk-neutral measure $P , r$ is given by 

$$
d r _ {t} = a (b - r _ {t}) d t + \sigma d W _ {t}
$$

for $r _ { 0 } > 0 , a > 0 , b > 0$ , and $\sigma > 0$ . Then 

$$
r _ {t} = e ^ {- a t} \left(r _ {0} + b (e ^ {a t} - 1) + \sigma \int_ {0} ^ {t} e ^ {a u} d W _ {u}\right).
$$

Consequently, $r _ { t }$ is a normal random variable with mean 

$$
E \left(r _ {t}\right) = e ^ {- a t} \left(r _ {0} + b \left(e ^ {a t} - 1\right)\right)
$$

and variance 

$$
\operatorname{Var} (r _ {t}) = \sigma^ {2} \left(\frac {1 - e ^ {- 2 a t}}{2 a}\right).
$$

However, a normal random variable can be negative with positive probability so this model for $r$ is not too realistic (unless the probability of being negative is small). Nonetheless, its simplicity validates its discussion. 

As $t  \infty$ , we see that $r _ { t }$ converges in law to a Gaussian random variable with mean b and variance $\frac { \sigma ^ { 2 } } { 2 a }$ 

The price of a zero coupon bond in the Vasicek model is therefore 

$$
\begin{array}{c} B (t, T) = E \left(\exp \left\{- \int_ {t} ^ {T} r _ {u} d u \right\} | \mathcal {F} _ {t}\right) \\ = e ^ {- b (T - t)} E \left(\exp \left\{- \int_ {t} ^ {T} X _ {u} d u \right\} | \mathcal {F} _ {t}\right), \end{array}\tag{9.7}
$$

where $X _ { u } ~ = ~ r _ { u } - b$ . Now X is the solution of the classical Ornstein-Uhlenbeck equation 

$$
d X _ {t} = - a X _ {t} d t + \sigma d W _ {t},\tag{9.8}
$$

with $X _ { 0 } = r _ { 0 } - b$ . Write 

$$
Z (t, x) = E \left(\exp \left\{- \int_ {0} ^ {t} X (u, x) d u \right\}\right),\tag{9.9}
$$

where $X ( u , x )$ is the solution of (9.2) with $X ( 0 , x ) = x$ . Now 

$$
X (u, x) = e ^ {- a u} \left(x + \int_ {0} ^ {u} \sigma e ^ {a s} d W _ {s}\right),
$$

so $X ( u , x )$ is a Gaussian process with continuous sample paths. Consequently, $\textstyle \left( \int _ { 0 } ^ { t } X ( u , x ) d u \right)$ is a Gaussian process; this can be established by considering moment-generating functions exp $\{ u _ { 1 } X ( t _ { 1 } ) + \cdot \cdot \cdot + u _ { n } X ( t _ { n } ) \}$ 

If Y is a Gaussian random variable with $E \left( \boldsymbol { Y } \right) = m$ and $\operatorname { V a r } ( Y ) = \gamma ^ { 2 }$ 2 we know that 

$$
E \left(e ^ {Y}\right) = e ^ {- m + \frac {1}{2} \gamma^ {2}}.
$$

Now 

$$
E \left(X (u, x)\right) = x e ^ {- a u}, \qquad E \left(\int_ {0} ^ {t} X (u, x) d u\right) = \frac {x}{a} \left(1 - e ^ {- a t}\right),
$$

and 

$$
\begin{array}{c} \operatorname{Cov} [ X (t, x), X (u, x) ] = \sigma^ {2} e ^ {- a (u + t)} E \left(\int_ {0} ^ {t} e ^ {a s} d W _ {s} \int_ {0} ^ {u} e ^ {a s} d W _ {s}\right) \\ = \sigma^ {2} e ^ {- a (u + t)} \int_ {0} ^ {u \wedge t} e ^ {2 a s} d s \end{array}
$$

$$
= \frac {\sigma^ {2}}{2 a} e ^ {- a (u + t)} \left(e ^ {2 a (u \wedge t)} - 1\right).\tag{9.10}
$$

Therefore, 

$$
\begin{array}{l} \operatorname{Var} \left(\int_ {0} ^ {t} X (u, x) d u\right) = \operatorname{Cov} \left(\int_ {0} ^ {t} X (u, x) d u, \int_ {0} ^ {t} X (s, x) d s\right) \\ \qquad = \int_ {0} ^ {t} \int_ {0} ^ {t} \operatorname{Cov} [ X (u, x), X (s, x) ] d u d s \\ \qquad = \int_ {0} ^ {t} \int_ {0} ^ {t} \frac {\sigma^ {2}}{2 a} e ^ {- a (u + s)} \left(e ^ {2 a (u \wedge s)} - 1\right) d u d s \\ \qquad = \frac {\sigma^ {2}}{2 a ^ {3}} \left(2 a t - 3 + 4 e ^ {- a t} - e ^ {- 2 a t}\right). \end{array}
$$

Consequently, 

$$
\begin{array}{l} Z (t, x) = E \left(\exp \left\{- \int_ {0} ^ {t} X (u, x) d u \right\}\right) \\ = \exp \left\{- \frac {x}{a} \left(1 - e ^ {- a t}\right) + \frac {1}{4} \frac {\sigma^ {2}}{a ^ {3}} \left(2 a t - 3 + 4 e ^ {- a t} - e ^ {- 2 a t}\right) \right\}. \end{array}
$$

Using the time homogeneity of the X process, 

$$
B (t, T) = e ^ {- b (T - t)} Z (T - t, r _ {t} - b).
$$

This can be written as 

$$
B (t, T) = \exp \left\{- (T - t) R (T - t, r _ {t}) \right\},
$$

where $R ( T - t , r _ { t } )$ can be thought of as the interest rate between times t and T. With $\begin{array} { r } { R _ { \infty } = b - \frac { \sigma ^ { 2 } } { 2 a ^ { 2 } } } \end{array}$ , we can write 

$$
R (t, r) = R _ {\infty} - \frac {1}{a t} \left(\left(R _ {\infty} - r\right) \left(1 - e ^ {- a t}\right) - \frac {\sigma^ {2}}{4 a ^ {2}} \left(1 - e ^ {- a t}\right) ^ {2}\right).
$$

Note that $R _ { \infty } = \operatorname* { l i m } _ { t \to \infty } R ( t , r )$ , so $R _ { \infty }$ can be thought of as the longterm interest rate. However, $R _ { \infty }$ does not depend on the instantaneous rate $r _ { t }$ . Practitioners consider this to be a weakness of the Vasicek model. Exercise 9.6.1. Let $0 \leq t \leq T \leq T ^ { }$ and consider a call option with expiry $T$ and strike K on the zero coupon bond $B ( t , T ^ { * } )$ . Show that this option will be exercised if and only if $r ( T ) < r ^ { * }$ , where, with $R _ { \infty }$ as above, 

$$
\begin{array}{c} r ^ {*} = R _ {\infty} \left(1 - \frac {\alpha (T ^ {*} - T)}{1 - e ^ {- \alpha (T ^ {*} - T)}}\right) - \frac {\sigma^ {2} [ 1 - e ^ {- \alpha (T ^ {*} - T)} ]}{4 \alpha^ {2}} \\ - \log (K) \left(\frac {\alpha}{1 - e ^ {- \alpha (T ^ {*} - T)}}\right). \end{array}\tag{9.11}
$$

## The Hull-White Model

In its simplest form this model is a generalisation of the Vasicek model using deterministic, time-varying coeficients. It is popular with practitioners. Its more general form includes a term $r _ { t } ^ { \beta }$ in the volatility, in which case it generalises the Cox-Ingersoll-Ross model discussed in the next section. 

In this model, the short rate process is supposed given by the stochastic diferential equation 

$$
d r _ {t} = (\alpha (t) - \beta (t) r _ {t}) d t + \sigma (t) d W _ {t}\tag{9.12}
$$

for $r _ { 0 } > 0$ . Here, $\alpha , \beta ,$ , and $\sigma$ are deterministic functions of $t .$ . 

Write $\begin{array} { r } { b ( t ) = \int _ { 0 } ^ { t } \beta ( u ) d u } \end{array}$ , so b is also non-random. We solve (9.12) by 4variation of constants to obtain 

$$
r _ {t} = e ^ {- b (t)} \left(r _ {0} + \int_ {0} ^ {t} e ^ {b (u)} \alpha (u) d u + \int_ {0} ^ {t} e ^ {b (u)} \sigma (u) d W _ {u}\right).
$$

Again, $r _ { t }$ is a deterministic quantity plus the stochastic integral of a deterministic function. 

Consequently, $r _ { t }$ is a Gaussian Markov process with mean 

$$
E \left(r _ {t}\right) = m (t) = e ^ {- b (t)} \left(r _ {0} + \int_ {0} ^ {t} e ^ {b (u)} \alpha (u) d u\right)
$$

and covariance 

$$
\mathrm{Cov} (r _ {t}, r _ {s}) = e ^ {- b (s) - b (t)} \int_ {0} ^ {s \wedge t} e ^ {2 b (u)} \sigma^ {2} (u) d u.
$$

Again we can argue that $\int _ { 0 } ^ { T } r _ { t } d t$ is normal. Its mean is 

$$
E \left(\int_ {0} ^ {T} r _ {t} d t\right) = \int_ {0} ^ {T} e ^ {- b (t)} \left(r _ {0} + \int_ {0} ^ {t} e ^ {b (u)} \alpha (u) d u\right) d t
$$

and its variance is 

$$
\mathrm{Var} \left(\int_ {0} ^ {T} r _ {t} d t\right) = \int_ {0} ^ {T} e ^ {2 b (u)} \sigma^ {2} (u) \left(\int_ {u} ^ {T} e ^ {- b (s)} d s\right) ^ {2} d u.
$$

The price of a zero coupon bond for this model is 

$$
B (0, T) = E \left(\exp \left\{- \int_ {0} ^ {T} r _ {t} d t \right\}\right).
$$

The quantity in the exponential is Gaussian, so we have 

$$
B (0, T) = \exp \left\{- E \left(\int_ {0} ^ {T} r _ {t} d t\right) + \frac {1}{2} \operatorname{Var} \left(\int_ {0} ^ {T} r _ {t} d t\right) \right\}
$$

$$
\begin{array}{l} = \exp \left\{- r _ {0} \int_ {0} ^ {T} e ^ {- b (t)} d t - \int_ {0} ^ {T} \int_ {0} ^ {t} e ^ {- b (t) + b (u)} \alpha (u) d u d t \right. \\ \quad \left. + \frac {1}{2} \int_ {0} ^ {T} e ^ {2 b (u)} \sigma^ {2} (u) \left(\int_ {u} ^ {T} e ^ {- b (s)} d s\right) ^ {2} d u \right\} \\ = \exp [ - r _ {0} C (0, T) - A (0, T) ], \end{array}
$$

where 

$$
C (0, T) = \int_ {0} ^ {T} e ^ {- b (t)} d t
$$

and 

$$
\begin{array}{l} A (0, T) = \int_ {0} ^ {T} \int_ {0} ^ {t} e ^ {- b (t) + b (u)} \alpha (u) d u d t \\ - \frac {1}{2} \int_ {0} ^ {T} e ^ {2 b (u)} \sigma^ {2} (u) \left(\int_ {u} ^ {T} e ^ {- b (s)} d s\right) ^ {2} d u. \end{array}
$$

Note that the first term in A can be written, using Fubini’s theorem, as 

$$
\int_ {0} ^ {T} \int_ {u} ^ {T} e ^ {- b (t) + b (u)} \alpha (u) d u d t = \int_ {0} ^ {T} e ^ {b (u)} \alpha (u) \left(\int_ {u} ^ {T} e ^ {- b (s)} d s\right) d u.
$$

Therefore 

$$
A (0, T) = \int_ {0} ^ {T} \left(e ^ {b (u)} \alpha (u) \gamma (u) - \frac {1}{2} e ^ {2 b (u)} \sigma^ {2} (u) \gamma^ {2} (u)\right) d u,
$$

where 

$$
\gamma (u) = \int_ {u} ^ {T} e ^ {- b (s)} d s.
$$

The price at time t of a zero coupon bond is 

$$
B (t, T) = E \left(\exp \left\{- \int_ {t} ^ {T} r _ {u} d u \right\} | \mathcal {F} _ {t}\right) = E \left(\exp \left\{- \int_ {t} ^ {T} r _ {u} d u \right\} | r _ {t}\right),
$$

where the final step follows because r is Markov. Write 

$$
C (t, T) = e ^ {b (t)} \int_ {t} ^ {T} e ^ {- b (u)} d u = e ^ {b (t)} \gamma (t)
$$

and 

$$
A (t, T) = \int_ {t} ^ {T} \left(e ^ {b (u)} \alpha (u) \gamma (u) - \frac {1}{2} e ^ {2 b (u)} \sigma^ {2} (u) \gamma^ {2} (u)\right) d u.
$$

Then it can be shown that 

$$
B (t, T) = \exp \left\{- r _ {t} C (t, T) - A (t, T) \right\}.\tag{9.13}
$$

Now $\alpha , \beta ,$ , and $\gamma$ are deterministic functions of time, t; consequently $C ( t , T )$ and $A ( t , T )$ are also functions only of t. Write $C _ { t } ( t , T )$ and $A _ { t } ( t , T )$ for their derivatives in t. From (9.13), we have 

$$
\begin{array}{r l} & {d B (t, T) = B (t, T) \Big [ - C (t, T) (\alpha (t) - \beta (t) r _ {t}) d t} \\ & {\qquad - C (t, T) \sigma (t) d W _ {t} - \frac {1}{2} C ^ {2} (t, T) \sigma^ {2} (t) d t - r _ {t} C _ {t} (t, T) d t - A _ {t} (t, T) d t \Big ].} \end{array}\tag{9.14}
$$

We are working under the risk-neutral measure, so 

$$
d B (t, T) = r _ {t} B (t, T) d t + \Delta (t) d W _ {t},\tag{9.15}
$$

where $\Delta$ is some coeficient function. Comparing (9.14) and (9.15), we see that we must have 

$$
r _ {t} = - C (t, t) (\alpha (t) - \beta (t) r _ {t}) - \frac {1}{2} C ^ {2} (t, T) \sigma^ {2} (t) - r _ {t} c _ {t} (t, T) - A (t, T).\tag{9.16}
$$

Consequently, 

$$
d B (t, T) = r _ {t} B (t, T) d t - B (t, T) \sigma (t) C (t, T) d W _ {t}.
$$

The volatility of the zero coupon bond is $\sigma ( t ) C ( t , T )$ 

## Some Normal Densities

Consider times $0 \leq t \leq T _ { 1 } < T _ { 2 }$ . In the Hull-White framework, we have seen that $r ( T _ { 1 } )$ is Gaussian with 

$$
E \left(r (T _ {1})\right) = m _ {1} = e ^ {- b (T _ {1})} \left(r _ {0} + \int_ {0} ^ {T _ {1}} e ^ {b (u)} \alpha (u) d u\right),
$$

$$
\operatorname{Var} [ r (T _ {1}) ] = \sigma_ {1} ^ {2} = e ^ {- 2 b (T _ {1})} \left(\int_ {0} ^ {T _ {1}} e ^ {2 b (u)} \sigma^ {2} (u) d u\right).
$$

Also, $\int _ { 0 } ^ { T _ { 1 } } r _ { u }$ du is Gaussian with 

$$
E \left(\int_ {0} ^ {T _ {1}} r _ {u} d u\right) = m _ {2} = \int_ {0} ^ {T _ {1}} e ^ {- b (v)} \left(r _ {0} + \int_ {0} ^ {v} e ^ {b (u)} \alpha (u) d u\right) d v,
$$

$$
\operatorname{Var} \int_ {0} ^ {T _ {1}} r _ {u} d u = \sigma_ {2} ^ {2} = \int_ {0} ^ {T _ {1}} e ^ {2 b (u)} \sigma^ {2} (u) \left(\int_ {u} ^ {T _ {1}} e ^ {- b (s)} d s\right) ^ {2} d u.
$$

The covariance of $r ( T _ { 1 } )$ and $\int _ { 0 } ^ { T _ { 1 } } r _ { u } d u$ is 

$$
E \left(\left(\int_ {0} ^ {T _ {1}} \left(r _ {u} - E \left(r _ {u}\right)\right) d u\right) \left(r (T _ {1}) - E \left(r (T _ {1})\right)\right)\right)
$$

$$
\begin{array}{l} = \int_ {0} ^ {T _ {1}} E \left((r _ {u} - E (r _ {u})) (r (T _ {1}) - E (r (T _ {1})))\right) d u \\ = \int_ {0} ^ {T _ {1}} \operatorname{Cov} (r _ {u}, r (T _ {1})) d u \\ = \int_ {0} ^ {T} \left(e ^ {- b (u) - b (T _ {1})} \int_ {0} ^ {T} e ^ {2 b (s)} \sigma^ {2} (s) d s\right) d u \\ = \rho \sigma_ {1} \sigma_ {2}, \end{array}
$$

say. 

## Bond Options

Consider a European call option on the zero coupon bond that has strike price K and expiration time $T _ { 1 }$ . The bond matures at time $T _ { 2 } > T _ { 1 }$ 

The calculations above imply that $( r ( T _ { 1 } ) , \int _ { 0 } ^ { T _ { 1 } } r _ { u } d u )$ is Gaussian with density 

$$
\begin{array}{l} f (x, y) = \frac {1}{2 \pi \sigma_ {1} \sigma_ {2} \sqrt {1 - \rho^ {2}}} \\ \times \exp \left\{- \frac {1}{2 (1 - \rho^ {2})} \left(\frac {(x - m _ {1}) ^ {2}}{\sigma_ {1} ^ {2}} - \frac {2 \rho (x - m _ {1}) (y - m _ {2})}{\sigma_ {1} \sigma_ {2}} + \frac {(y - m _ {2}) ^ {2}}{\sigma_ {2} ^ {2}}\right) \right\}. \end{array}
$$

The price of the European option on B with expiration time $T _ { 1 }$ and strike K at time 0 is 

$$
\begin{array}{l} V _ {0} = E \left(e ^ {- \int_ {0} ^ {T _ {1}} r _ {u} d u} (B (T _ {1}, T _ {2}) - K) ^ {+}\right) \\ \quad = E \left(e ^ {- \int_ {0} ^ {T _ {1}} r _ {u} d u} (\exp \{- r (T _ {1}) C (T _ {1}, T _ {2}) - A (T _ {1}, T _ {2}) \} - K) ^ {+}\right) \\ \quad = \int_ {- \infty} ^ {\infty} \int_ {- \infty} ^ {\infty} e ^ {- y} (\exp \{- x C (T _ {1}, T _ {2}) - A (T _ {1}, T _ {2}) \} - K) ^ {+} f (x, y) d x d y. \end{array}
$$

To determine the price of the bond option at time $t \leq T _ { 1 } < T _ { 2 }$ , we note that the random variable $\begin{array} { r l } {  { \Big ( r ( T _ { 1 } ) , \int _ { t } ^ { T _ { 1 } } r _ { u } d u \Big ) } } & { { } } \end{array}$ is Gaussian with a density similar to $f ( x , y )$ , except that $m _ { 1 } , m _ { 2 } , \sigma _ { 2 } , \sigma _ { 2 }$ , and $\rho$ are replaced by 

$$
\begin{array}{l} m _ {1} (t) = E \left(r (t _ {1}) | r _ {t}\right) = e ^ {- b (T _ {1})} \left(e ^ {b (t)} r _ {t} + \int_ {t} ^ {T _ {1}} e ^ {b (u)} \alpha (u) d u\right), \\ \sigma_ {1} ^ {2} (t) = E \left((r (T _ {1}) - m _ {1} (t)) ^ {2} | r _ {t}\right) = e ^ {- 2 b (T _ {1})} \int_ {t} ^ {T _ {1}} e ^ {2 b (u)} \sigma^ {2} (u) d u, \\ m _ {2} (t) = E \left(\int_ {t} ^ {T _ {1}} r _ {u} d u | r _ {t}\right) \\ = \int_ {t} ^ {T _ {1}} \left(r _ {t} e ^ {- b (v) + b (t)} + e ^ {- b (v)} \int_ {t} ^ {v} e ^ {b (u)} \alpha (u) d u\right) d v, \end{array}
$$

$$
\begin{array}{l} \sigma_ {2} ^ {2} (t) = E \left(\left(\int_ {t} ^ {T _ {1}} r _ {u} d u - m _ {2} (r)\right) ^ {2} | r _ {t}\right) \\ = \int_ {t} ^ {T _ {1}} e ^ {2 b (v)} \sigma^ {2} (v) \left(\int_ {v} ^ {T _ {1}} e ^ {- b (s)} d s\right) ^ {2} d v, \end{array}
$$

and 

$$
\begin{array}{l} \rho (t) \sigma_ {1} (t) \sigma_ {2} (t) = E \left(\left(\int_ {t} ^ {T _ {1}} r _ {u} d u - m _ {2} (t)\right) (r (T _ {1}) - m _ {1} (t)) | r _ {t}\right) \\ = \int_ {t} ^ {T _ {1}} e ^ {- b (u) - b (T _ {1})} \int_ {t} ^ {u} e ^ {2 b (s)} \sigma^ {2} (s) d s d u. \end{array}
$$

These quantities now depend on $r _ { t }$ and so are stochastic as is, therefore, the corresponding option price 

$$
\begin{array}{l} E \left(e ^ {- \int_ {t} ^ {T _ {1}} r _ {u} d u} (B (T _ {1}, T _ {2}) - K) ^ {+} | \mathcal {F} _ {t}\right) \\ = E \left(e ^ {- \int_ {t} ^ {T _ {1}} r _ {u} d u} (\exp \{- r (T _ {1}) C (T _ {1}, T _ {2}) - A (T _ {1}, T _ {2}) \} - K) ^ {+} | r _ {t}\right). \end{array}
$$

This price can be expressed in terms of an integration with respect to a density analogous to $f _ { t } ( x , y )$ in which $m _ { 1 } , \sigma _ { 1 } , m _ { 2 } , \sigma _ { 2 } , \rho$ are replaced by $m _ { 1 } ( t ) , \sigma _ { 1 } ( t ) , m _ { 2 } ( t ) , \sigma _ { 2 } ( t ) , \rho ( t )$ , respectively. 

The Hull-White model leads to a closed form expression for the option on the bond. Also, the parameters of the model can be estimated so the initial yield curve is matched exactly. However, it is a ‘one-factor’ model and 

$$
B (t, T) = \exp \left\{- r _ {t} C (t, T) - A (t, T) \right\},
$$

so all bond prices for all $T$ are perfectly correlated. Further, the short rate $r _ { t }$ is normally distributed. This means it can take negative values with positive probability, and the bond price can exceed 1. 

## The Cox-Ingersoll-Ross Model

We have noted in the Vasicek and Hull-White models for $r _ { t }$ that, because $r _ { t }$ is Gaussian, there is a positive probability that $r _ { t } < 0$ 

The Cox-Ingersoll-Ross model for $r _ { t }$ provides a stochastic diferential equation for $r _ { t }$ , the solution of which is always non-negative. To describe this process, recall the Ornstein-Uhlenbeck equation (9.8) 

$$
d X _ {t} = - a X _ {t} d t + \sigma d W _ {t}\tag{9.17}
$$

with solution 

$$
X (t, x) = e ^ {- a t} \left(x + \int_ {0} ^ {t} \sigma e ^ {a s} d W _ {s}\right).
$$

Here W is a standard Brownian motion on a probability space $( \Omega , { \mathcal { F } } , P )$ In fact, suppose we have n independent Brownian motions $W _ { 1 } , \ldots , W _ { n }$ on $( \Omega , { \mathcal { F } } , P )$ and n Ornstein-Uhlenbeck processes $X _ { 1 } , \ldots , X _ { n }$ given by equations 

$$
d X _ {i} (t) = - \frac {1}{2} \alpha X _ {i} (t) d t + \frac {1}{2} \sigma d W _ {i} (t),
$$

so that 

$$
X _ {i} (t) = e ^ {- \frac {1}{2} \alpha t} \left(X _ {i} (0) + \frac {1}{2} \sigma \int_ {0} ^ {t} e ^ {\frac {1}{2} \beta s} d W _ {i} (s)\right).
$$

Consider the process 

$$
r _ {t} = X _ {1} ^ {2} (t) + X _ {2} ^ {2} (t) + \dots + X _ {n} ^ {2} (t).
$$

From Itˆo’s diferential rule, 

$$
\begin{array}{l} d r _ {t} = \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} 2 X _ {i} (t) \left(- \frac {1}{2} \alpha X _ {i} (t) d t + \frac {1}{2} \sigma d W _ {i} (t)\right) + \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \frac {1}{4} \sigma^ {2} d t \\ = - \alpha r _ {t} d t + \sigma \left(\sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} X _ {i} (t) d W _ {i} (t)\right) + \frac {n \sigma^ {2}}{4} d t \\ = \left(\frac {n \sigma^ {2}}{4} - \alpha r _ {t}\right) d t + \sigma \sqrt {r _ {t}} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \frac {X _ {i} (t)}{\sqrt {r _ {t}}} d W _ {i} (t). \end{array}
$$

Consider the process 

$$
W _ {t} = \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \int_ {0} ^ {t} \frac {X _ {i} (u)}{\sqrt {r _ {u}}} d W _ {i} (u).
$$

Then W is a continuous martingale and 

$$
W _ {t} ^ {2} = 2 \int_ {0} ^ {t} W _ {u} d W _ {u} + \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \int_ {0} ^ {t} \frac {X _ {i} ^ {2} (u)}{r _ {u}} d u = 2 \int_ {0} ^ {t} W _ {u} d W _ {u} + t,
$$

so $W _ { t } ^ { 2 } - t$ is a martingale. From $\mathrm { L e v y ` s }$ characterisation, therefore, $W$ is a standard Brownian motion and we can write 

$$
d r _ {t} = \left(\frac {n \sigma^ {2}}{4} - \alpha r _ {t}\right) d t + \sigma \sqrt {r _ {t}} d W _ {t}.
$$

It is known (see [240], for example) that if $n = 1$ , then $P ( r _ { t } > 0 ) = 1$ but 

P (there are infinitely many times $t > 0$ for which $r _ { t } = 0 ) = 1$ 

However, if $n \geq 2$ , then 

P (there is at least one time $t > 0$ for which $r _ { t } = 0 ) = 0$ 

Definition 9.6.2. A Cox-Ingersoll-Ross (CIR) process is the process defined by an equation of the form 

$$
d r _ {t} = (a - b r _ {t}) d t + \sigma \sqrt {r _ {t}} d W _ {t},\tag{9.18}
$$

where $a > 0 , b > 0$ , and $\sigma > 0$ are constant. With $\textstyle n = { \frac { 4 a } { \sigma ^ { 2 } } }$ , we can interpret $r _ { t }$ as $\textstyle \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { n } X _ { i } ^ { 2 } ( t )$ for Ornstein-Uhlenbeck processes $X _ { i }$ as above. However, equation (9.18) makes sense whether or not n is an integer. 

Remark 9.6.3. Geman and Yor [142] explore the relationship between the Vasicek and CIR models and show in particular that the CIR process is a Bessel process. 

Similarly to the results for integer n, we quote the following ([240]). If $\textstyle a < { \frac { \sigma ^ { 2 } } { 2 } }$ , so $n < 2$ , then 

P (there are infinitely many times $t > 0$ for which $r _ { t } = 0 ) = 1$ 

Consequently, this range for a is not too useful. If $\textstyle a \geq { \frac { \sigma ^ { 2 } } { 2 } }$ , so $n \geq 2$ , then 

P (there is at least one time $t > 0$ for which $r _ { t } = 0 ) = 0$ 

Write $r _ { 0 , t } ( x )$ for the solution of (9.18) for which $r _ { 0 } = x$ . The next result describes the law of the pair of random variables $\begin{array} { r } { \left( r _ { 0 , t } ( x ) , \int _ { 0 } ^ { t } r _ { 0 , u } ( x ) d u \right) } \end{array}$ Note that $\phi$ and $\psi$ are functions of t only, reminiscent of the A and $\mathrm { \Delta } ^ { \prime } \mathrm { C }$ functions in the Hull-White model. 

Theorem 9.6.4. For any $\lambda > 0$ and $\mu > 0$ , we have 

$$
E \left(e ^ {- \lambda r _ {0, t} (x)} e ^ {- \mu \int_ {0} ^ {t} r _ {0, u} (x) d u}\right) = e ^ {- a \phi_ {\lambda , \mu} (t)} e ^ {- x \psi_ {\lambda , \mu} (t)},
$$

where 

$$
\phi_ {\lambda , u} (t) = - \frac {2}{\sigma^ {2}} \log \left(\frac {2 \gamma e ^ {t (b + \gamma) / 2}}{\sigma^ {2} \lambda (e ^ {\gamma t} - 1) + \gamma - b + e ^ {\gamma t} (\gamma + b)}\right),
$$

$$
\psi_ {\lambda , u} (t) = \frac {\lambda (\gamma + b) + e ^ {\gamma t} (\gamma - b) + 2 \mu (e ^ {\gamma t} - 1)}{\sigma^ {2} \lambda (e ^ {\gamma t} - 1) + \gamma - b + e ^ {\gamma t} (\gamma + b)}
$$

and $\gamma = \sqrt { b ^ { 2 } + 2 \sigma ^ { 2 } \mu }$ 

Proof. Suppose $0 \leq t \leq T$ . From the uniqueness of solutions of (9.18), we have the following ‘flow’ property: 

$$
r _ {0, T} (x) = r _ {t, T} (r _ {0, t} (x)).
$$

Consider the expectation 

$$
E \left(e ^ {- \lambda r _ {t, T} (r _ {0, t} (x))} e ^ {- \mu \int_ {t} ^ {T} r _ {0, u} (x) d \mu} | \mathcal {F} _ {t}\right).
$$

From the Markov property, this is the same as conditioning on $r _ { 0 , t } ( x )$ , so write 

$$
V \left(t, r _ {0, t} (x)\right) = E \left(e ^ {- \lambda r _ {0, T} (x)} e ^ {- \mu \int_ {t} ^ {T} r _ {0, u} (x) d u} | r _ {0, t} (x)\right).
$$

Now 

$$
e ^ {- \mu \int_ {0} ^ {t} r _ {0, u} (x) d u} V (t, r _ {0, t} (x)) = E \left(e ^ {- \lambda r _ {0, T} (x)} e ^ {- \mu \int_ {0} ^ {T} r _ {0, u} (x) d u} | \mathcal {F} _ {t}\right)
$$

and so is a martingale. However, applying the Itˆo diferentiation rule, we obtain 

$$
\begin{array}{l} e ^ {- \mu \int_ {0} ^ {t} r _ {0, u} d u} V (t, r _ {0, t} (x)) \\ = V (0, x) + \int_ {0} ^ {t} \Big (\frac {\partial V}{\partial u} (u, r _ {0, u} (x)) - \mu r _ {0, u} (x) V (u, r _ {0, u} (x)) \\ + \frac {\partial V}{\partial \xi} (u, r _ {0, u} (x)) (a - b r _ {0, u} (x)) \\ + \frac {1}{2} \frac {\partial^ {2} V}{\partial \xi^ {2}} (u, r _ {0, u} (x)) \sigma^ {2} r _ {0, u} (x) \Big) e ^ {- \mu \int_ {0} ^ {u} r _ {0, s} (x) d s} d u \\ + \int_ {0} ^ {t} e ^ {- \mu \int_ {0} ^ {u} r _ {0, s} (x) d s} \frac {\partial V}{\partial \xi} (u, r _ {0, u} (x)) \sigma \sqrt {r _ {0 , u} (x)} d W _ {u}. \end{array}
$$

As the left-hand side is a martingale and the right-hand side is an Itˆo process, the du integral must be the zero process. Consequently, 

$$
\frac {\partial V}{\partial t} (t, y) - \mu y V (t, y) + \frac {\partial V}{\partial y} (t, y) (a - b y) + \frac {1}{2} \frac {\partial^ {2} V}{\partial y ^ {2}} (t, y) \sigma^ {2} y = 0
$$

with 

$$
V (t, y) = E \left(e ^ {- \lambda r _ {t, T} (y)} e ^ {- \mu \int_ {t} ^ {T} r _ {t, u} (y) d u}\right).
$$

Because the coeficients of (9.18) are independent of t, the solution of (9.18) is stationary and we can write 

$$
V (t, y) = E \left(e ^ {- \lambda r _ {0, T - t} (y)} e ^ {- \mu \int_ {0} ^ {T - t} r _ {0, u} (y) d u}\right).
$$

Define 

$$
F (t, y) = E \left(e ^ {- \lambda r _ {0, t} (y)} e ^ {- \mu \int_ {0} ^ {t} r _ {0, u} (y) d u}\right),
$$

so that $V ( t , y ) = F ( T - t , y )$ and F satisfies 

$$
\frac {\partial F}{\partial t} = \frac {\partial F}{\partial y} (a - b y) - \mu y F + \frac {1}{2} \sigma^ {2} y \frac {\partial^ {2} F}{\partial y ^ {2}}\tag{9.19}
$$

with $F ( 0 , y ) = e ^ { - \lambda y }$ 

Motivated by the formula of the Hull-White model, we look for a solution of (9.19) in the form 

$$
F (t, y) = e ^ {- a \phi (t) - x \psi (t)}.
$$

This is the case if $\phi ( 0 ) = 0$ and $\psi ( 0 ) = \lambda$ with 

$$
\phi^ {\prime} (t) = \psi (t), - \psi^ {\prime} (t) = \frac {\sigma^ {2}}{2} \psi^ {2} (t) + b \psi (t) - \mu .
$$

Solving these equations gives the expressions for $\phi$ and $\psi .$ 

Remark 9.6.5. Taking $\mu = 0$ , we obtain the Laplace transform of $r _ { t } ( x )$ 

$$
E \left(e ^ {\lambda r _ {t} (x)}\right) = (2 \lambda K + 1) ^ {- 2 a / \sigma^ {2}} \exp \left\{\frac {- \lambda K z}{2 \lambda K + 1} \right\},
$$

where 

$$
K = \frac {\sigma^ {2}}{4 b} \left(1 - e ^ {- b t}\right), \quad z = \frac {4 b x}{\sigma^ {2} (e ^ {b t} - 1)}.
$$

Consequently, the Laplace transform of $\frac { r _ { t } ( x ) } { K }$ is given by $\begin{array} { l } { { g _ { \frac { 4 a } { \sigma ^ { 2 } } , z } , } } \end{array}$ , where 

$$
g _ {\delta , z} = \frac {1}{(2 \lambda + 1) ^ {\delta / 2}} \exp \left\{- \frac {\lambda z}{2 \lambda + 1} \right\}.
$$

However, consider the chi-square density $f _ { \delta , z }$ , having $\delta$ degrees of freedom and decentral parameter z, given by 

$$
f _ {\delta , z} (x) = \frac {e ^ {- z / 2}}{2 z ^ {\frac {\delta}{4} - \frac {1}{2}}} e ^ {- x / 2} x ^ {\frac {\delta}{4} - \frac {1}{2}} I _ {\frac {\delta}{2} - 1} (\sqrt {x z}) \mathrm{for} x > 0.
$$

Here $I _ { \nu }$ is the modified Bessel function of order $\nu ,$ given by 

$$
I _ {\nu} (x) = \left(\frac {x}{2}\right) ^ {\nu} \sum_ {n = 0} ^ {\infty} \frac {\left(\frac {x}{2}\right) ^ {2 n}}{n ! \Gamma (\nu + n + 1)}.
$$

Then it can be shown that $g _ { \delta , z }$ is the Laplace transform of the law of a random variable having density $f _ { \delta , z } ( x )$ . Consequently, $\frac { r _ { t } ( x ) } { K }$ is a random variable having a chi-square density with δ degrees of freedom. 

Recall that we are working under the risk-neutral probability P. The price of a zero coupon bond at time 0 is 

$$
B (0, T) = E \left(\exp \left\{- \int_ {0} ^ {T} r _ {u} (x) d u \right\}\right) = e ^ {- a \phi_ {0, 1} (0, T) - r _ {0} (x) \psi_ {0, 1} (0, T)}.
$$

Here 

$$
\phi_ {0, 1} (T) = - \frac {2}{\sigma^ {2}} \log \left(\frac {2 \gamma e ^ {T (\gamma + b) / 2}}{\gamma - b + e ^ {\gamma T} (\gamma + b)}\right), \quad \psi_ {0, 1} (T) = \frac {2 (e ^ {\gamma T} - 1)}{\gamma - b + e ^ {\gamma T} (\gamma + b)}
$$

with $\gamma = \sqrt { b ^ { 2 } + 2 \sigma ^ { 2 } }$ . The price of a zero coupon bond at time t is similarly, because of stationarity, 

$$
B (t, T) = e ^ {- a \phi_ {0, 1} (T - t) - r _ {t} (x) \psi_ {0, 1} (T - t)}.
$$

Suppose $0 \leq T \leq T ^ { * }$ . Consider a European call option with expiration time $T$ and strike price K on the zero coupon bond $B \left( t , T ^ { * } \right)$ . At time 0, this has a price 

$$
\begin{array}{l} V _ {0} = E \left(e ^ {- \int_ {0} ^ {T} r _ {u} (x) d u} \left(B (T, T ^ {*}) - K\right) ^ {+}\right) \\ \quad = E \left(E \left(e ^ {- \int_ {0} ^ {T} r _ {u} (x) d u} \left(B (T, T ^ {*}) - K\right) ^ {+} | \mathcal {F} _ {t}\right)\right) \\ \quad = E \left(e ^ {- \int_ {0} ^ {T} r _ {u} (x) d u} \left(e ^ {- a \phi_ {0, 1} (T ^ {*} - T) - r _ {T} (x) \psi_ {0, 1} (T ^ {*} - T)} - K\right) ^ {+}\right). \end{array}
$$

Write 

$$
r ^ {*} = \frac {- a \phi_ {0 , 1} (T ^ {*} - T) + \log K}{\psi_ {0 , 1} (T ^ {*} - T)}.
$$

Then 

$$
\begin{array}{c} V _ {0} = E \left(e ^ {- \int_ {0} ^ {T} r _ {u} (x) d u} B (T, T ^ {*}) \mathbf {1} _ {\{r _ {T} (x) <   r ^ {*} \}}\right) \\ - K E \left(e ^ {- \int_ {0} ^ {T} r _ {u} (x) d u} \mathbf {1} _ {\{r _ {T} (x) <   r ^ {*} \}}\right). \end{array}
$$

Now 

$$
E \left(e ^ {- \int_ {0} ^ {T} r _ {u} (x) d u} B (T, T ^ {*})\right) = B (0, T ^ {*}), \quad E \left(e ^ {- \int_ {0} ^ {T} r _ {u} (x) d u}\right) = B (0, T).
$$

Define two new probability measures $P _ { 1 }$ and $P _ { 2 }$ by setting 

$$
\left. \frac {d P _ {1}}{d P} \right| _ {\mathcal {F} _ {T}} = \frac {e ^ {- \int_ {0} ^ {T} r _ {u} (x) d u} B (T , T ^ {*})}{B (0 , T ^ {*})}, \quad \left. \frac {d P _ {2}}{d P} \right| _ {\mathcal {F} _ {T}} = \frac {e ^ {- \int_ {0} ^ {T} r _ {u} (x) d u}}{B (0 , T)}.
$$

Then 

$$
V _ {0} = B (0, T ^ {*}) P _ {1} \left(r _ {T} (x) <   r ^ {*}\right) - K B (0, T) P _ {2} \left(r _ {T} (x) <   r ^ {*}\right).
$$

Write 

$$
\begin{array}{l} K _ {1} = \frac {\delta^ {2}}{2} \cdot \frac {e ^ {\gamma_ {T}} - 1}{\gamma (e ^ {\gamma_ {T}} + 1) + (\sigma^ {2} \psi_ {0 , 1} (T ^ {*} - T) + b) (e ^ {\gamma_ {T}} - 1)}, \\ K _ {2} = \frac {\sigma^ {2}}{2} \cdot \frac {e ^ {\gamma_ {T}} - 1}{\gamma (e ^ {\gamma_ {T}} + 1) + b (e ^ {\gamma_ {T}} - 1)}. \end{array}
$$

Then it can be shown that the law of $\frac { r _ { T } ( x ) } { K _ { 1 } }$ under $P _ { 1 }$ (resp. the law of $\frac { r _ { T } ( x ) } { K _ { 2 } }$ under $P _ { 2 } )$ is a decentral chi-square with $\frac { 4 a } { \sigma ^ { 2 } }$ degrees of freedom and decentral parameter $\xi _ { 1 } \ ( \mathrm { r e s p . } \ \xi _ { 2 } )$ , where 

$$
\xi_ {1} = \frac {8 r _ {0} (x) \gamma^ {2} e ^ {\gamma_ {T}}}{\sigma^ {2} \left(e ^ {\gamma_ {T}} - 1\right) (\gamma \left(e ^ {\gamma_ {T}} + 1\right)) + \left(\sigma^ {2} \psi_ {0 , 1} (T ^ {*} - T) + b\right) \left(e ^ {\gamma_ {T}} - 1\right)},
$$

$$
\xi_ {2} = \frac {8 r _ {0} (x) \gamma^ {2} e ^ {\gamma_ {T}}}{\sigma^ {2} (e ^ {\gamma_ {T}} - 1) (\gamma (e ^ {\gamma_ {T}} + 1) + b (e ^ {\gamma_ {T}} - 1))}.
$$

Consequently, if $F _ { \delta , z }$ is the probability distribution function for a chi-square random variable with $\delta$ degrees of freedom and decentral parameter z, then 

$$
V _ {0} = B (0, T ^ {*}) F _ {\frac {4 a}{\sigma^ {2}}, \xi_ {1}} \left(\frac {r ^ {*}}{K _ {1}}\right) - K B (0, T) F _ {\frac {4 a}{\sigma^ {2}}, \xi_ {2}} \left(\frac {r ^ {*}}{K _ {2}}\right).
$$

## 9.7 The Heath-Jarrow-Morton Model

## Forward Rate Agreement

Suppose $0 \leq t \leq T < T + \varepsilon \leq T ^ { * }$ . ‘Today’ is time t. We wish to enter a contract to borrow $1 at the future time $T$ and repay it (with interest) at the time $T + \varepsilon$ . The rate of interest to be paid between T and $T + \varepsilon$ is to be agreed today and so must be <sub>t</sub>-measurable. 

We could approximate this transaction by buying today a T-maturity zero for $B ( t , T )$ and shorting an amount $\frac { B ( t , T ) } { B ( t , T + \varepsilon ) }$ of $( T + \varepsilon )$ -maturity zeros. 

The cost of this portfolio at time t is 

$$
B (t, T) - \frac {B (t , T)}{B (t , T + \varepsilon)} \cdot B (t, T + \varepsilon) = 0.
$$

Now at the future time $T$ we receive $1 for the T-maturity zero. Then, at the time $( T + \varepsilon )$ , we must pay $\frac { B ( t , T ) } { B ( t , T + \varepsilon ) }$ for the $( T + \varepsilon )$ -maturity zeros. 

In efect, we are looking at borrowing $1 at the future time $T$ and paying $\ S { \frac { B ( t , T ) } { B ( t , T + \varepsilon ) } }$ at time $T + \varepsilon$ . Consequently, the interest rate we are paying on the dollar received at time $T$ is $R ( t , T , T + \varepsilon )$ , where 

$$
\frac {B (t , T)}{B (t , T + \varepsilon)} = \exp \left\{\varepsilon R (t, T, T + \varepsilon) \right\}
$$

so 

$$
R (t, T, T + \varepsilon) = - \frac {1}{\varepsilon} [ \log B (t, T + \varepsilon) - \log B (t, T) ].
$$

Definition 9.7.1. The instantaneous interest rate for money borrowed at time $T ,$ agreed upon at time $t \leq T$ , is the forward rate $f ( t , T )$ 

In fact, 

$$
f (t, T) = \lim _ {\varepsilon \downarrow 0} R (t, T, T + \varepsilon) = \frac {- \partial}{\partial T} \log B (t, T).
$$

Then, as $B ( t , t ) = 1$ • 

$$
\log B (t, T) = \int_ {t} ^ {T} \frac {\partial}{\partial T} \log B (t, u) d u = - \int_ {t} ^ {T} f (t, u) d u.
$$

Therefore, $\begin{array} { r } { B ( t , T ) = \exp ( - \int _ { t } ^ { T } f ( t , u ) d u ) } \end{array}$ 

4We note that this is an alternative representation for $B ( t , T )$ , in contrast to its expression in terms of the short-rate process $r :$ 

$$
B (t, T) = E \left(\exp \left\{- \int_ {t} ^ {T} r _ {u} d u \right\} | \mathcal {F} _ {t}\right).
$$

Agreeing at time t on the forward rate $f ( t , u )$ means one agrees, at time $t ,$ that the instantaneous interest rate at time $u \in [ t , T ]$ will be $f ( t , u )$ 

Thus one agrees that investing $1 at time t will give $\$ 1$ exp $\textstyle \int _ { t } ^ { T } f ( t , u ) d$ u at time $T ;$ investing $\$ 8( t, T )$ at time t will give 

$$
\$ B (t, T) \cdot \exp \left\{\int_ {t} ^ {T} f (t, u) d u \right\} = \$ 1
$$

at time $T .$ 

Lemma 9.7.2. We have $r _ { t } = f ( t , t )$ 

Proof. We have two representations: 

$$
\begin{array}{l} B (t, T) = E \left(\exp \left\{- \int_ {t} ^ {T} r _ {u} d u \right\} | \mathcal {F} _ {t}\right), \\ B (t, T) = \exp \left\{- \int_ {t} ^ {T} f (r, u) d u \right\}. \end{array}\tag{9.20}
$$

(9.21) 

From (9.20), 

$$
\frac {\partial B (t , T)}{\partial T} = E \left(- r (T) \exp (- \int_ {t} ^ {T} r _ {u} d u) | \mathcal {F} _ {t}\right).
$$

Evaluating at $T = t$ , we have 

$$
\left. \frac {\partial B (t , T)}{\partial T} \right| _ {T = t} = - r _ {t}.
$$

From (9.21), 

$$
\frac {\partial B (t , T)}{\partial T} = - f (t, T) \exp \left(- \int_ {t} ^ {T} f (t, u) d u\right)
$$

and 

$$
\left. \frac {\partial B (t , T)}{\partial T} \right| _ {T = t} = - f (t, t).
$$

## The Heath-Jarrow-Morton model

The Heath-Jarrow-Morton (HJM) model for term structure describes a system of stochastic diferential equations for the evolution of the forward rate $f ( t , T )$ . For each $T \in ( 0 , T ^ { * } ]$ , suppose the dynamics of f are given by 

$$
d f (t, T) = \alpha (t, T) d t + \sigma (t, T) d W _ {t}.\tag{9.22}
$$

Here the coeficients $\alpha ( u , T )$ and $\sigma ( u , T )$ , for $0 \leq u \leq T$ , are measurable (in $( u , \omega ) )$ and adapted. The integral form of (9.22) is 

$$
f (t, T) = f (0, T) + \int_ {0} ^ {t} \alpha (u, T) d u + \int_ {0} ^ {t} \sigma (u, T) d W _ {u}.\tag{9.23}
$$

Note that we have two time parameters and recall 

$$
B (t, T) = \exp \left\{- \int_ {t} ^ {T} f (t, u) d u \right\}.
$$

With d denoting a diferential in the t variable, 

$$
\begin{array}{r l} & {d \left(- \int_ {t} ^ {T} f (t, u) d u\right) = f (t, t) d t - \int_ {t} ^ {T} (d f (t, u)) d u} \\ & {\qquad = r _ {t} d t - \int_ {t} ^ {T} [ \alpha (t, u) d t + \sigma (t, u) d W _ {t} ] d u} \\ & {\qquad = r _ {t} d t - \alpha^ {*} (t, T) d t - \sigma^ {*} (t, T) d W _ {t},} \end{array}\tag{9.24}
$$

where 

$$
\alpha^ {*} (t, T) = \int_ {t} ^ {T} \alpha (t, u) d u, \quad \sigma^ {*} (t, T) = \int_ {t} ^ {T} \sigma (t, u) d u.
$$

Recall, that by definition, $f ( t , u )$ is an $( \mathcal { F } _ { t } )$ -adapted process. Therefore, 

$$
X _ {t} = - \int_ {t} ^ {T} f (t, u) d u
$$

is an $\mathcal { F } _ { t } .$ -adapted process. In fact, it is an Itˆo process with, as in (9.24), 

$$
d X _ {t} = (r _ {t} - \alpha^ {*} (t, T)) d t - \sigma^ {*} (t, T) d W _ {t}.
$$

Also, $B ( t , T ) = e ^ { X _ { t } }$ , so 

$$
\begin{array}{c} d B (t, T) = e ^ {X _ {t}} \left(r _ {t} - \alpha^ {*} (t, T) + \frac {1}{2} \sigma^ {*} (t, T) ^ {2}\right) d t - e ^ {X _ {t}} \sigma^ {*} (t, T) d W _ {t} \\ = B (t, T) \left(\left(r _ {t} - \alpha^ {*} (t, T) + \frac {1}{2} \sigma^ {*} (t, T) ^ {2}\right) d t - \sigma^ {*} (t, T) d W _ {t}\right). \end{array}
$$

Now, the discounted $B ( t , T )$ will be a martingale under $P$ (so $P$ is a risk-neutral measure) if 

$$
\alpha^ {*} (t, T) = \frac {1}{2} \left(\sigma^ {*} (t, T)\right) ^ {2} \text {   for   } 0 \leq t \leq T \leq T ^ {*}.
$$

From the definitions of $\alpha ^ { * }$ and $\sigma ^ { * }$ , this means that 

$$
\int_ {t} ^ {T} \alpha (t, u) d u = \frac {1}{2} \left(\int_ {t} ^ {T} \sigma (t, u) d u\right) ^ {2}.
$$

This is equivalent to 

$$
\alpha (t, T) = \sigma (t, T) \int_ {0} ^ {T} \sigma (t, u) d u.
$$

If P itself is not a risk-neutral measure, there may be a probability $P ^ { \theta }$ under which $\left( \frac { B ( t , T ) } { S _ { t } ^ { 0 } } \right)$ is a martingale. This is the content of the following result due to Heath, Jarrow, and Morton [153]. 

Theorem 9.7.3. For each $T \in \mathsf { \Gamma } ( 0 , T ]$ , suppose $\alpha ( u , T )$ and $\sigma ( u , T )$ are adapted processes. We assume $\sigma ( u , T ) > 0$ for all $u , T ,$ and $f ( 0 , T )$ is $a$ deterministic function of $T .$ The instantaneous forward rate $f ( t , T )$ is defined by 

$$
f (t, T) = f (0, T) + \int_ {0} ^ {t} \alpha (u, T) d u + \int_ {0} ^ {t} \sigma (u, t) d W _ {u}.
$$

Then the term structure model determined by the processes $f ( t , T )$ does not allow arbitrage if and only if there is an adapted process $\left( \boldsymbol { \theta } _ { t } \right)$ such that 

$$
\alpha (t, T) = \sigma (t, T) \int_ {t} ^ {T} \sigma (t, u) d u + \sigma (t, T) \theta_ {t} f o r a l l 0 \leq t \leq T \leq T ^ {*},
$$

and the process 

$$
\Lambda_ {t} ^ {\theta} = \exp \left\{- \int_ {0} ^ {t} \theta_ {u} d W _ {u} - \frac {1}{2} \int_ {0} ^ {t} \theta_ {u} ^ {2} d u \right\}
$$

is an $( \mathcal { F } _ { t } , P )$ -martingale. 

Proof. Suppose $\theta$ is an adapted process such that $\Lambda ^ { \theta } ( t )$ is an $( \mathcal { F } _ { t } , P )$ martingale, and define a new probability measure $P ^ { \theta }$ by setting 

$$
\left. \frac {d P ^ {\theta}}{d P} \right| _ {\mathcal {F} _ {T ^ {*}}} = \Lambda^ {\theta} (T ^ {*}).
$$

By Girsanov’s theorem, $W ^ { \theta }$ is a Brownian motion under $P ^ { \theta }$ , where 

$$
W _ {t} ^ {\theta} = \int_ {0} ^ {t} \theta_ {u} d u + W _ {t},
$$

and 

$$
\begin{array}{c} d B (t, T) = B (t, T) \left(\left(r _ {t} - \alpha^ {*} (t, T) + \frac {1}{2} \sigma^ {*} (t, T) ^ {2} + \sigma^ {*} (t, T) \theta_ {t}\right) d t \right. \\ \left. - \sigma^ {*} (t, T) d W _ {t} ^ {\theta}\right). \end{array}
$$

where, as before, $\textstyle \alpha ^ { * } ( t , T ) = \int _ { t } ^ { T } \alpha ( t , u ) d u$ and $\begin{array} { r } { \sigma ^ { * } ( t , T ) = \int _ { t } ^ { T } \sigma ( t , u ) d u . } \end{array}$ 

For $B ( t , T )$ to have a rate of return $r _ { t }$ under $P ^ { \theta } , \theta$ must satisfy 

$$
\alpha^ {*} (t, T) = \frac {1}{2} \sigma^ {*} (t, T) ^ {2} + \sigma^ {*} (t, T) \theta_ {t}.
$$

This must hold for all maturities $T .$ . Diferentiating with respect to $T ,$ that is 

$$
\alpha (t, T) = \sigma (t, T) \sigma^ {*} (t, T) + \sigma (t, T) \theta_ {t} \text {   for   } 0 \leq t \leq T \leq T ^ {*}.
$$

Remark 9.7.4. The point to note is that, if there is such a process $\theta _ { t }$ , it is independent of the time $T$ maturity of the bond $B ( t , T )$ , and 

$$
\theta_ {t} = - \Big (\frac {- \alpha^ {*} (t , T) + \frac {1}{2} \sigma^ {*} (t , T) ^ {2}}{\sigma^ {*} (t , T)} \Big).
$$

Now, under the ‘market’ probability $P _ { : }$ , the rate of return of the bond is 

$$
r _ {t} - \alpha^ {*} (t, T) + \frac {1}{2} \sigma^ {*} (t, T) ^ {2}.
$$

The rate of return above the interest rate $r _ { t }$ is therefore 

$$
- \alpha^ {*} (t, T) + \frac {1}{2} \sigma^ {*} (t, T) ^ {2},
$$

and the market price of risk is just 

$$
\frac {- \alpha^ {*} (t , T) + \frac {1}{2} \sigma^ {*} (t , T) ^ {2}}{\sigma^ {*} (t , T)} = - \theta_ {t}.
$$

The requirement of the theorem therefore is that the market price of the risk be independent of the maturity times $T .$ . Substituting for $\theta .$ , we have that, under $P ^ { \theta }$ ， 

$$
d B (t, T) = B (t, T) \left(r _ {t} d t - \sigma^ {*} (t, T) d W _ {t} ^ {\theta}\right),
$$

$$
d f (t, T) = \sigma (t, T) \sigma^ {*} (t, T) d t + \sigma (t, T) d W _ {t} ^ {\theta}.
$$

## 9.8 A Markov Chain Model

Elliott, Hunter, and Jamieson ([117]) introduced a self-calibrating model for the short-term rate. It is supposed that the short-term rate $r _ { t }$ is a finite state space Markov chain defined on a probability space $( \Omega , { \mathcal { F } } , P )$ taking (positive) values $r _ { 1 } , r _ { 2 } , \ldots , r _ { N }$ . Each of these values can be identified with one of the canonical unit vectors $e _ { i }$ in $\mathbb { R } ^ { N } , e _ { i } = ( 0 , \dots , 0 , 1 , 0 , \dots , 0 )$ (In efect, we are considering an indicator function $\mathbf { 1 } _ { \left\{ r _ { i } \right\} } ( r )$ on the set $\{ r _ { 1 } , r _ { 2 } , \dots , r _ { N } \} )$ . Without loss of generality, we can take the state space of our Markov chain $( X _ { t } ) _ { t \geq 0 }$ to be the set ${ \cal S } = \{ e _ { 1 } , e _ { 2 } , \dots , e _ { N } \}$ . Writing $r = ( r _ { 1 } , r _ { 2 } , \ldots , r _ { N } ) \in \mathbb { R } ^ { N }$ , we then have 

$$
r _ {t} = r \cdot X _ {t} = r (X _ {t}),
$$

where the central dot denotes the scalar product in $\mathbb { R } ^ { N }$ . Considering the Markov chain to have state space S simplifies the notation. 

The unconditional distribution of $X _ { t }$ is $E \left( X _ { t } \right) = p _ { t } = \left( p _ { t } ^ { 1 } , \ldots , p ^ { N } ( t ) \right)$ ， where 

$$
p _ {t} ^ {i} = P (X _ {t} = e _ {i}) = E (e _ {i} \cdot X _ {t}) = P (r _ {t} = r _ {i}).
$$

Suppose this distribution evolves according to the Kolmogorov equation 

$$
\frac {d p _ {t}}{d t} = A p _ {t}.
$$

Here A is $\mathrm { a \ ^ { \cdot } Q { \mathrm { - } m a t r i x } ^ { \cdot } }$ ; that is, $A = ( a _ { j i } ) _ { 1 \leq i , j \leq N }$ with $\begin{array} { r } { \sum _ { j = 1 } ^ { N } a _ { i j } = 0 } \end{array}$ and $a _ { j i } \geq 0 \mathrm { ~ i f ~ } i \neq j$ . The components $a _ { j i }$ could be taken to be time-varying, though this would complicate their estimation. 

The price of a zero coupon bond at time t, with maturity $T ,$ in this model is 

$$
B (t, T) = E \left(\exp \left\{- \int_ {t} ^ {T} r (X _ {s}) d s \right\} | \mathcal {F} _ {t}\right),
$$

where $( \mathcal { F } _ { t } )$ is the filtration generated by X (or, equivalently, by r). 

Because of the Markov property, this is 

$$
E \left(\exp \left\{- \int_ {t} ^ {T} r (X _ {s}) d s \right\} | X _ {t}\right) = B (t, T, X _ {t}),
$$

say, and so is a function of $X _ { t } \in S$ . Any (real) function of $X _ { t } \in S$ is given as the scalar product of some function $\boldsymbol { \dot { \phi } } _ { t } = ( \dot { \phi } _ { t } ^ { 1 } , \phi _ { t } ^ { 2 } , \dots , \phi ^ { N } ( t ) ) ^ { \prime } \in \mathbb { R } ^ { N }$ with $X _ { t }$ . That is, we can write 

$$
B (t, T, X _ {t}) = \phi_ {t} \cdot X _ {t},
$$

where $\phi _ { t } ^ { i } = B ( t , T , e _ { i } )$ 

Now 

$$
e ^ {- \int_ {0} ^ {t} r (X _ {s}) d s} B (t, T, X _ {t}) = e ^ {- \int_ {0} ^ {t} r (X _ {s}) d s} \phi_ {t} \cdot X _ {t}
$$

$$
= E \left(e ^ {- \int_ {0} ^ {t} r (X _ {s}) d s} | \mathcal {F} _ {t}\right)
$$

and so is a martingale. 

Lemma 9.8.1. Define the R $ { \boldsymbol { N } }$ -valued process M by 

$$
M _ {t} = X _ {t} - X _ {0} - \int_ {0} ^ {t} A X _ {s} d s.
$$

Then M is an $( \mathcal { F } _ { t } , P )$ -martingale. 

Proof. Consider the matrix exponential $e ^ { A ( t - s ) }$ . By the Markov property, 

$$
E \left(X _ {t} \mid X _ {s}\right) = e ^ {A (t - s)} X _ {s}
$$

for $t \geq s$ . (In efect, one solves the Kolmogorov equation with initial condition $X _ { s } . )$ Now, for $t \geq s$ , if I is the $N \times N$ identity matrix, 

$$
\begin{array}{r l} & E \left(M _ {t} - M _ {s}   | \mathcal {F} _ {s}\right) = E \left(X _ {t} - X _ {s}   | \mathcal {F} _ {s}\right) - E \left(\int_ {s} ^ {t} A X _ {u} d u   | \mathcal {F} _ {s}\right) \\ & \qquad = e ^ {A (t - s)} X _ {s} - X _ {s} - \int_ {s} ^ {t} A e ^ {A (u - s)} X _ {s} d u \\ & \qquad = \left(e ^ {A (t - s)} - I - \int_ {s} ^ {t} A e ^ {A (u - s)} d u\right) X _ {s} \\ & \qquad = \left(e ^ {A (t - s)} - I - [ e ^ {A (u - s)} ] _ {s} ^ {t}\right) X _ {s} = 0. \end{array}
$$

Corollary 9.8.2. The semimartingale representation of $X _ { t }$ is therefore 

$$
X _ {t} = X _ {0} + \int_ {0} ^ {t} A X _ {s} d s + M _ {t}.
$$

Theorem 9.8.3. The process $\phi _ { t } \in \mathbb { R } ^ { N }$ has dynamics 

$$
\frac {d \phi_ {t}}{d t} = (\mathrm{diag} r - A ^ {*}) \phi_ {t}
$$

with terminal condition $\phi _ { T } = \mathbf { 1 } = ( 1 , 1 , \ldots , 1 ) ^ { \prime } \in \mathbb { R } ^ { N }$ 

Proof. We have seen that 

$$
e ^ {- \int_ {0} ^ {t} r (X _ {s}) d s} B (t, T, X _ {t}) = e ^ {- \int_ {0} ^ {t} r (X _ {s}) d s} \phi_ {t} \cdot X _ {t}
$$

is an $( \mathcal { F } _ { t } , P )$ -martingale. Consequently, the dt term in its Itˆo process (or semimartingale) representation must be identically zero. Now 

$$
\begin{array}{c} e ^ {- \int_ {0} ^ {t} r (X _ {s}) d s} \phi_ {t} \cdot X _ {t} = B (0, T, X _ {0}) - \int_ {0} ^ {t} r (X _ {s}) e ^ {- \int_ {0} ^ {s} r (X _ {u}) d u} \phi_ {s} \cdot X _ {s} d s \\ + \int_ {0} ^ {t} e ^ {- \int_ {0} ^ {s} r (X _ {u}) d u} \left(\frac {d \phi_ {s}}{d s} \cdot X _ {s} + \phi_ {s} \cdot (A X _ {s})\right) d s + \int_ {0} ^ {t} e ^ {- \int_ {0} ^ {s} r (X _ {u}) d u} \phi_ {s} \cdot d M _ {s}. \end{array}
$$

Consequently, 

$$
e ^ {- \int_ {0} ^ {s} r (X _ {u}) d u} \left(- r (X _ {s}) \phi_ {s} \cdot X _ {s} + \frac {d \phi_ {s}}{d s} \cdot X _ {s} + \phi_ {s} \cdot (A X _ {s})\right) = 0.
$$

Now $r ( X _ { s } ) = r \cdot X _ { s }$ , where $r = ( r _ { 1 } , r _ { s } , \ldots , r _ { s } ) ^ { \prime }$ , and $r ( X _ { s } ) \phi _ { s } \cdot X _ { s } =$ $( \deg r ) \phi _ { s } \cdot X _ { s }$ , where diag r is the matrix with r on its diagonal. Therefore 

$$
\frac {d \phi_ {s}}{d s} \cdot X _ {s} + A ^ {*} \phi_ {s} \cdot X _ {s} - ((\operatorname{diag} r) \phi_ {s}) \cdot X _ {s} = 0 \text {   for   all   } X _ {s}.
$$

Consequently, $\phi$ is given by the vector equation 

$$
\frac {d \phi_ {t}}{d t} = (\mathrm{diag} r - A ^ {*}) \phi_ {t}
$$

with terminal condition $\phi _ { T } = ( 1 , \ldots , 1 ) ^ { \prime } = 1$ 

Corollary 9.8.4. Write $B = d i a g \ r - A ^ { * }$ . Then $\phi _ { t } = e ^ { - B ( T - t ) } \mathbf { 1 }$ , and the price at time t of a zero coupon bond is 

$$
B (t, T, X _ {t}) = \phi_ {t} \cdot X _ {t} = (e ^ {- B (T - t)} \cdot X _ {t}) \mathbf {1}.
$$

The yield for such a bond is 

$$
y _ {t, T} = - \frac {1}{T - t} \log B (t, T, X _ {t}).
$$

Yield values are quoted in the market. 

In [117] it is supposed that yield values give noisy information about such a Markov chain term structure model. The techniques of filtering from Hidden Markov models (see [110]), are then applied to estimate the state of X and the model parameters. 

Chapter 10 

# Consumption-Investment Strategies

## 10.1 Utility Functions

The results of this chapter are a presentation of the comprehensive, fundamental, and elegant contributions of Karatzas, Lehoczky, Sethi, Shreve and Xu. See, for example, the papers [186] through [190]. 

We first review in the multi-asset situation concepts relating to trading strategies, consumption processes, and utility functions. 

On a probability space $( \Omega , { \mathcal { F } } , P )$ , consider a market that includes a bond $S ^ { 0 }$ and n risky assets $S ^ { 1 } , \ldots , S ^ { N }$ . Their dynamics are given by the equations 

(10.1) 

$$
\begin{array}{l l} d S _ {t} ^ {0} = S _ {t} ^ {0} r _ {t} d t, & S _ {0} ^ {0} = 1, \\ d S _ {t} ^ {i} = S _ {t} ^ {i} \left(\mu_ {i} (t) d t + \sum_ {j = 1} ^ {n} \sigma_ {i j} (t) d W _ {j} (t)\right), & S _ {0} ^ {i} = s _ {i} \text {   for   } i = 1, 2, \dots , n. \end{array} \tag {10}\tag{10.2}
$$

Here $W ( t ) = ( W _ { 1 } ( t ) , \ldots , W _ { d } ( t ) )$ is an n-dimensional Brownian motion defined on $( \Omega , { \mathcal { F } } , P )$ and $( \mathcal { F } _ { t } )$ will denote the completion of the filtration 

$$
\sigma \left\{W (u): 0 \leq u \leq t \right\} = \sigma \left\{S _ {u}: 0 \leq u \leq t \right\}.
$$

The interest rate $r _ { t } .$ , mean rate of return $\mu ( t ) = ( \mu _ { 1 } ( t ) , \ldots , \mu _ { n } ( t ) ) ^ { \prime }$ , and the volatility $\sigma ( t ) = ( \sigma _ { i j } ( t ) ) _ { 1 < i , j < d }$ are taken to be measurable, adapted, and bounded processes. Note that we have taken the dimension, $n ,$ of the Brownian motion equal to the number of risky assets. 

Write $a ( t ) = \sigma ( t ) \sigma ^ { * } ( t )$ . We assume there is an $\varepsilon > 0$ such that, with . denoting the Euclidean norm in $\mathbb { R } ^ { n }$ , 

$$
\xi^ {*} a (t) \xi \geq \varepsilon | \xi | ^ {2} \text {   for   all   } \xi \in \mathbb {R} ^ {n} \text {   and   } (t, \omega) \in [ 0, \infty) \times \Omega .
$$

Consequently, the inverses of $\sigma$ and $\sigma ^ { * }$ exist and are bounded: 

$$
\left| \sigma (t, \omega) ^ {- 1} \xi \right| \leq \varepsilon^ {- \frac {1}{2}} | \xi |, \quad \left| \sigma^ {*} (t, \omega) ^ {- 1} \xi \right| \leq \varepsilon^ {- \frac {1}{2}} | \xi | \text {for all} \xi \in \mathbb {R} ^ {n}.\tag{10.3}
$$

The filtration is then equivalently given as the completion of the filtration generated by the process S. 

Therefore in this situation the market price of risk defined by equation (7.34) has the unique solution 

$$
\theta_ {t} = \sigma (t) ^ {- 1} \left(b (t) - r _ {t} {\bf 1}\right);
$$

furthermore, $\theta$ is bounded and progressively measurable. 

As in Chapter 8, introduce 

$$
\Lambda_ {t} = \exp \left\{- \int_ {0} ^ {t} \theta_ {s} ^ {\prime} d W (s) - \frac {1}{2} \int_ {0} ^ {t} | \theta_ {s} ^ {\prime} | ^ {2} d s \right\}
$$

and define a new probability measure $P ^ { \theta }$ by setting 

$$
\left. \frac {d P ^ {\theta}}{d P} \right| _ {\mathcal {F} _ {t}} = \Lambda_ {t}.
$$

We know from Girsanov’s theorem that $W ^ { \theta }$ is a Brownian motion under $P ^ { \theta }$ , where 

$$
W _ {t} ^ {\theta} = W (t) + \int_ {0} ^ {t} \theta_ {s} d s.
$$

Furthermore, under $P ^ { \theta }$ 

$$
d S _ {t} ^ {i} = S _ {t} ^ {i} \left(r _ {t} d t + \sum_ {j = 1} ^ {n} \sigma_ {i j} (t) d W _ {j} ^ {\theta} (t)\right) \text { for } i = 1, 2, \dots , n.
$$

That is, in this situation, $P ^ { \theta }$ is the unique risk-neutral, or martingale, measure. 

Definition 10.1.1. A utility function $U : [ 0 , \infty ) \times ( 0 , \infty ) \to \mathbb { R }$ is a $C ^ { 0 , 1 }$ function such that: 

a) $U ( t , \cdot )$ is strictly increasing and strictly concave. 

b) The derivative $\begin{array} { r } { U ^ { \prime } ( t , c ) = \frac { \partial } { \partial c } U ( t , c ) } \end{array}$ is such that, for every $t > 0$ • 

$$
\lim _ {c \to \infty} U ^ {\prime} (t, c) = 0, \quad \lim _ {c \downarrow 0} U ^ {\prime} (t, c) = U ^ {\prime} (t, 0 +) = \infty .
$$

These conditions have natural economic interpretations. The increasing property of $U$ represents the fact that the investor prefers higher levels of consumption or wealth. The strict concavity of $U ( t , c )$ in c implies $U ^ { \prime } ( t , c )$ is decreasing in $c ;$ this models the concept that the investor is risk averse. 

The condition that $U ^ { \prime } ( t , 0 + ) = \infty$ is not strictly necessary, but it simplifies some of the proofs. 

$U ^ { \prime } ( t , c )$ is strictly decreasing in $c ;$ therefore, there is an inverse map $I ( t , c )$ so that 

$$
I \left(t, U ^ {\prime} (t, c)\right) = c = U ^ {\prime} (t, I (t, c))
$$

for $c \in ( 0 , \infty )$ . The concavity of U implies that 

$$
U (t, I (t, y)) \geq U (t, c) + y (I (t, y) - c) \text {   for   all   } c, y.\tag{10.4}
$$

For some later results, we require that $U ( t , c )$ is $C ^ { 2 }$ in $c \in ( 0 , \infty )$ for all $t \in [ 0 , T ]$ and $\begin{array} { r } { U ^ { \prime \prime } ( t , c ) = \frac { \partial ^ { 2 } U } { \partial c ^ { 2 } } } \end{array}$ is non-decreasing in c for all $t \in [ 0 , T ]$ . These two conditions imply that $I ( t , c )$ is convex and of class $C ^ { 1 }$ in $c \in ( 0 , \infty )$ , and 

$$
\frac {\partial}{\partial y} U (t, I (t, y)) = y \frac {\partial}{\partial y} I (t, y).
$$

## 10.2 Admissible Strategies

The definitions in this section are the natural counterparts of the discretetime notions introduced and discussed briefly in Section 5.6. 

We recall that in the continuous-time setting a portfolio process or trading strategy $H = \left( H ^ { 1 } , \ldots , H ^ { n } \right) ^ { \prime }$ is a measurable $\mathbb { R } ^ { n }$ -valued process that is adapted $( \mathcal { F } _ { t } )$ - and is such that 

$$
\int_ {0} ^ {T} \left| H _ {s} \right| ^ {2} d s <   \infty \mathrm{a.s.}
$$

A consumption process $( c _ { t } ) _ { 0 \leq t \leq T }$ is a non-negative, measurable, adapted process (with respect to $\left( \mathcal { F } _ { t } \right) )$ such that 

$$
\int_ {0} ^ {T} c _ {t} d t <   \infty \mathrm{a.s.}
$$

The adapted condition means the investor cannot anticipate the future, so ‘insider trading’ is not allowed. 

The wealth of the investor at time t is then 

$$
X _ {t} = \sum_ {i = 0} ^ {n} H _ {t} ^ {i} S _ {t} ^ {i} - \int_ {0} ^ {t} c _ {s} d s.
$$

Here $H _ { t } ^ { i } S _ { t } ^ { i }$ represents the amount invested in asset $i = 0 , 1 , \ldots , n$ , and $\int _ { 0 } ^ { t } c _ { s } d s$ represents the total amount consumed up to time t. 

If the strategy H is self-financing, changes in the wealth derive only from changes in the asset prices, interest on the bond, and from consumption, and then 

$$
d X _ {t} = \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {d} H _ {t} ^ {i} d S _ {t} ^ {i} + \left(1 - \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} H _ {t} ^ {i}\right) d S _ {t} ^ {0} - c _ {t} d t.
$$

From (10.1) and (10.2), this is 

$$
\begin{array}{r l} & {(r _ {t} X _ {t} - c _ {t}) d t + H _ {t} ^ {\prime} (\mu (t) - r _ {t} \mathbf {1}) d t + H _ {t} ^ {\prime} \sigma (t) d W (t)} \\ & {\qquad = (r _ {t} X _ {t} - c _ {t}) d t + H _ {t} ^ {\prime} \sigma (t) d W _ {t} ^ {\theta}.} \end{array}
$$

Writing $\begin{array} { r } { \beta _ { t } = \left( S _ { t } ^ { 0 } \right) ^ { - 1 } = \exp \left\{ - \int _ { 0 } ^ { t } r _ { s } d s \right\} } \end{array}$ , we see that 

$$
\beta_ {t} X _ {t} = x - \int_ {0} ^ {t} \beta_ {s} c _ {s} d s + \int_ {0} ^ {t} \beta_ {s} H _ {s} ^ {\prime} \sigma (s) d W ^ {\theta} (s),\tag{10.5}
$$

where $x = X _ { 0 }$ is the initial wealth of the investor. 

Consequently, 

$$
D _ {t} = \beta_ {t} X _ {t} + \int_ {0} ^ {t} \beta_ {s} c _ {s} d s = x + \int_ {0} ^ {t} \beta_ {s} H _ {s} ^ {\prime} \sigma (s) d W ^ {\theta} (s),
$$

which is the present discounted wealth plus the total discounted consumption so far, is a continuous local martingale under $P ^ { \theta }$ 

Definition 10.2.1. The deflator for the market is the process ξ defined by 

$$
\xi_ {t} = \beta_ {t} \Lambda_ {t}.
$$

This equals the discount factor $\beta$ modified by the Girsanov density Λ to take account of the financial market. 

Now 

$$
